===PAGE 56===
 The Justice Department
55
The Justice Department places order and justice ahead of 
liberal concerns. Democracy, in as far as certain civilian 
positions are democratically elected, is at best tolerated and, 
at worst, crushed if it even remotely threatens the order of 
the city. Mega-City One citizens have few rights and lead 
essentially drab, meaningless lives that are blighted by mass 
unemployment, boredom and the bewildering pace of 22
nd 
Century living. Citizens are sel ﬁ sh, capricious, uncaring 
about others and largely ignorant of anything but their 
immediate concerns.
Without the means to impose law and order immediately 
and ruthlessly, Mega-City One would descend rapidly into 
anarchy – as amply demonstrated by the Block Mania started 
by the Sov Block Judges that heralded the Apocalypse War. 
Given even the remotest chance, Mega-City One citizens 
resort to their base instincts and only the Judges keep them 
from destroying themselves.
In such a vast metropolis – Mega-City One has a population 
of around 400 million – the only way to maintain order is 
to deliver justice and punishment swiftly and to maximum 
effect. A traditional court system would be unable to cope 
with the sheer weight of crime that would be forced through 
its apparatus and the delivery of a sentence at the point of 
a crime sends out a clear message and deterrent to other, 
would-be law breakers. Without the Justice Department and 
the Judges, crime would be at a vastly higher rate, quickly 
leading to a complete and utter social meltdown.
The Justice Department maintains control through vast 
resources. It is in control of the city’s budget, in control 
of its information systems and takes the crucial decisions 
relating to the city’s administration. The Judges who patrol 
the streets are the public face of the Justice Department but 
the organisation is immense and there is almost no area 
of Mega-City One’s operation that the Justice Department 
does not inﬂ uence or control outright.
This means that the Justice Department is supported by 
a huge civilian and robot infrastructure that is responsible 
for certain, non-judicial, administrative and operational 
tasks. The Justice Department has a dedicated civil 
service in addition to the army of Judges and specialist 
teams who ensure that the citizens of Mega-City One are 
kept in their place. This administrative function is largely 
invisible to the populace of the city and, indeed, to the 
Judges who rely on its support. But without it, the Justice 
Department would be incapable of functioning with the 
ruthless efficiency it displays.
Origins of 
the Justice 
Department 
–  Fargo
As the Mega-City gained momentum, the judicial system lost 
it. Due Process was too slow, too inﬂ exible and woefully under-
equipped to cope with the social conditions of Mega-City One. 
If the justice process was too slow, then the sentences it 
passed on those who broke the law were meaningless. Social 
liberalism and the prevailing attitude of rehabilitating, rather 
than punishing, had created a system where criminals readily 
sneered at the idea of justice. And, in the ever-expanding 
warrens of the city, it was increasingly easy for criminals to 
perpetrate their activities without fear of apprehension.
Eustace Fargo, a presiding judge of the old United States 
Supreme Court, saw the need for a radical new direction. 
Disillusioned with the crumbling infrastructure of Due Process, 
Fargo suggested a new way: judges moved out of the courtrooms 
and onto the streets, given the power and means to apprehend 
criminals and sentence them at the point of the crime, removing 
weeks, months and sometimes years, from the process that 

===PAGE 57===
 The Justice Department
56
had underpinned American society since before the Declaration 
of Independence. The old system was fair and just; but society, 
through the Mega Cities, had out-run its reach. Between 2025 
and 2027 the east coast of America was rocked by uncontrollable 
gang warfare. By this time Fargo had been appointed by 
Congress to the position of Government Special Prosecutor for 
Street Crime and under his regime the gang leaders and major 
perpetrators of anarchy were brought to justice. But the justice 
system was too slow and open to intimidation: every single 
defendant walked free. The beleaguered citizens saw the need 
for instant justice even though some in government opposed it. 
When President Gurney was swept back into ofﬁ ce on a ticket 
of implementing Fargo’s ‘New Deal’ for justice, the people had 
signalled their willingness to see justice being brought to bear 
in a new way. Under Fargo’s direction the ﬁ rst academies to 
train judges in the new form of justice were established but it 
would be 2030 before the remnants of the old judicial system 
would be swept away. The Judges were not universally popular 
and nor were they immune from making mistakes; but with 
brutal efﬁ ciency the gang wars were halted, those responsible 
incarcerated without having the time or opportunity to intimidate 
juries and, for the ﬁ rst time in decades, the war against crime 
looked like it could be won.
Fargo’s system ﬂ ourished under his careful eye. Judges 
were trained to be incorruptible and this included a monastic 
approach to their personal lives, controlling normal human 
urges so that they would be immune from the temptations 
of both cash and ﬂ esh. Regrettably, Fargo, being only too 
human, was caught in ﬂ agrante with Sequenta Tells, the Vice 
Chair of the Commission for Legal Reform. This discovery 
would be ruinous for the justice system and Fargo knew it: 
he took the honourable approach of putting a bullet through 
his head. This attempt at suicide did not kill Fargo, instead 
causing dreadful brain damage, but Justice Department 
could not afford for the world to know the truth. So, news 
of Fargo’s death was broadcast, the story being that he 
was the victim of a drive-by shooting whilst on duty on the 
streets. Fargo’s attempted suicide and brain damage was 
made a complete secret, even from the president; only the 
most senior members of the Justice Department – Solomon, 
Goodman, Judd and others – knew the truth and they were 
sworn to secrecy.
Attitudes to 
the Justice 
Department
Most people know the Justice Department only through 
the presence of the Judges on the streets and in the City 
Blocks. To them, the Justice Department is The Law and 
Protector. The Judges are stern, harsh but scrupulously fair; 
there to be supported and, often, admired. But to others 
– those who can be bothered to look past the four walls of 
their apartments and mopads – the Justice Department is a 
dictatorial organisation that deliberately crushes the human 
spirit in order to preserve its own power. The Judges are 
fascist bullyboys, wielding too much power. The city is 
beholden to them and therefore at their mercy. The attitudes 
of both the general populace and the small number of Justice 
Department deniers are undoubtedly narrow-minded; neither 
sees the Big Picture which is, curiously, all around them. 
Mega-City One’s size, diversity and complexity exist only 
because the Judges keep the forces of chaos at bay. And 
there are many sources of chaos: the disenfranchised and 
bitter mutants of the Cursed Earth; the criminal gangs who 
have never been more ruthless in their exploitation of the 
weak and gullible; supernatural and trans-dimensional forces 
that see Mega-City One as something to be destroyed; 
egomaniacs like Judge Cal and Owen Krysler; and the Judge 
Child, who will stop at nothing to achieve supreme power. 
The Judges understand their position clearly and appreciate 
how they are viewed by some sections of society; but they 
do not let it in ﬂ uence their role. Many may not like the way 
justice is imposed or order maintained but the Judges know it 
is essential. Every Judge, from the moment he steps into the 
Academy of Justice, is trained to recognise and understand 
that the populace of Mega-City One is too large, too diverse 
and too immature to govern itself in the old, traditional ways. 
Every Judge is taught to understand the inherent selﬁ shness 
of the ordinary individual and how to deal with it so that that 
selﬁ shness has the least possible impact. Each new crisis 
faced by Mega-City One strengthens the Judges’ position 
and, even though the Justice Department’s existence has 
been threatened many times in its history, it has prevailed 
on each occasion due to its stoicism and refusal to capitulate 
even when doing so would have been the easiest option.
Thus, the Justice Department is the ultimate symbol of 
unyielding authority, and is the most ef ﬁ cient and effective 
expression of it. It cares little for its own popularity but much 
for its ability to do the immense job it was created to do: the 
protection of Mega-City One.
Organisation
The Justice Department works to a set hierarchy that has 
changed little throughout its history – even during the turbulent 
times when the very nature of the Justice Department was 
called into question (the reign of Judge Cal, for instance). 
The diagram on page 55 illustrates this hierarchy:

===PAGE 58===
 The Justice Department
57
The  Chief Judge
The supreme guardian of law and order in Mega-City One, 
the position of Chief Judge is a combination of the political, 
military, ceremonial and judicial: every crucial aspect of the 
state is wrapped into the role. The Chief Judge heads the 
Council of Five and, whilst above the council in terms of rank 
and experience, the Chief Judge can still be vetoed by it and 
is bound, by law, to act according to the majority decision of 
the council unless it is split and a deciding vote is called for.
The Chief Judge is usually elected by Mega-City One’s Senior 
Judges on a simple ‘one Judge, one Vote’ system. No more 
than seven candidates can ever run for the position (and it is 
rare that such an electoral race occurs) and all candidates must 
be of a senior rank. Once elected, the Chief Judge undergoes 
a rigorous period of brieﬁ ng by the Council of Five to prepare 
him or her for the demands of ofﬁ ce. The Chief Judge is briefed 
on the location of the network of strategic operations bunkers 
buried deep beneath Mega-City One; the detailed orders 
necessary for committing the city to war; and countless other 
secrets regarding the city, its history and the relations with the 
other Mega Cities scattered around the world.
A Brief History of Chief 
Judges
Every Chief Judge brings a different approach and form 
of rule to the position. Chief Judge Goodman, a brilliant 
administrator, was a poor judge of character and easily 
duped by those close to him. Chief Judge Cal was an 
insane tyrant who manipulated many Judges (not least his 
personal cohort, the SJS) into accepting his increasingly 
insane and murderous schemes – including making his pet 
goldﬁ sh a Judge and sentencing the entire city to death. His 
successor, Grifﬁ n, was the Principal of the Academy of Law 
who fought against Cal and was elected to the position on 
Dredd’s recommendation. 
Grifﬁ n was an excellent Chief Judge, leading the city through 
many dif ﬁ cult times, including the Apocalypse War. It was 
this conﬂ ict that brought about his sad demise. Grif ﬁ n was 
captured by the East Meg invader, Kazan, and brainwashed 
into supporting the Sov Block invasion. Dredd himself had 
no choice but to assassinate Grif ﬁ n, understanding that 
although the Chief Judge had turned traitor, this was beyond 
Grifﬁ n’s control.
*Covert Operations Establishment
*

===PAGE 59===
 The Justice Department
58
After Grifﬁ n came McGruder. Previously, McGruder had 
been the head of the SJS and noted for her hard-line, no-
nonsense attitude. The only member of the Council of Five 
to survive the Apocalypse War, she assumed the position 
on Grifﬁ n’s death, leading the Mega-City One resistance 
and had her position con ﬁ rmed upon the city’s liberation 
and the end of the war. McGruder led the rebuilding of the 
city in the wake of the war and became popular through 
her efforts (the rebuilding effort naturally created jobs). 
However, despite her undoubted capabilities, McGruder 
was not without her ﬂ aws. She took a curiously favourable 
stance to Shojun, Warlord of Ji, a powerful psion who 
threatened not only Mega-City One but the entire world 
with his plan to raise the Seven Samurai. McGruder 
countermanded Dredd’s recommendation for Shojun’s 
assassination. The resulting massacre hit McGruder hard 
and she opted to take the Long Walk, even though many 
senior Judges wanted her to remain in post, remembering 
her undoubted strengths. Those members of the Council of 
Five who disagreed with her decision to leave the city were 
dismissed – McGruder’s last act as Chief Judge and one 
that showed that, for all her ﬂ aws, she was still a staunch 
proponent of justice.
McGruder was succeeded by Silver, the senior lecturer in 
Applied Violence at the Academy of War and a distinguished 
war veteran. McGruder appointed Silver to the Council of 
Five in one of her last acts in ofﬁ ce and Silver’s election was 
a certainty. Chief Judge Silver rapidly proved to be one of 
the most hard-line, uncompromising Chief Judges Mega-City 
One has ever enjoyed. Silver was completely prepared to 
use smear tactics and a bending of justice to clamp-down on 
the Democracy Movement of 2109, giving Dredd himself the 
power to bring the Democrats to heel. As Silver said to Dredd: 
‘On this one, you write the Law...’ Silver used professional 
agitators and blackmail to undermine the Democracy 
movement, turning a massacre into an anti-democracy 
propaganda war. This signalled Silver’s willingness to use 
ever-greater draconian laws against the citizens and it led, 
inevitably, to the death of an innocent young boy (killed by a 
citizen brain damaged in a riot-put down by Judges). Dredd, 
seeing how the law had been twisted and his own complicity 
in it, was left with no option but to take the Long Walk 
himself. Silver suppressed this news, fearing the impact it 
would have across the city and replaced Dredd with Judge 
Kraken, another clone taken from the same genetic stock as 
Dredd and Rico. These events precipitated the Necropolis 
crisis (see page 247) and led, inevitably, to Silver’s own 
death and undeath at the hands of Judge Death himself. 
This peculiar situation led to McGruder replacing Silver (who 
was executed by Dredd, having returned from the Long Walk 
during the Necropolis crisis).
McGruder’s second stint as Chief Judge was to see her 
abilities considerably diminished. Her time in the Cursed Earth 
during Silver’s tenure left her psychologically battered and her 
judgement was considerably ﬂ awed. Due to the deaths of the 
Council of Five, there was little to challenge her increasingly 
unpredictable decisions and even Dredd could not convince 
her to reinstate the council. It took an assassination attempt 
by one of her own robots to convince her that her time as 
Chief Judge was completely through. She ﬁ nally retired as a 
citizen and, ultimately, succumbed to Alzheimer’s Disease. 
She would have declined into complete senility had Dredd 
not saved her from compulsory euthanasia and taken her 
into the Cursed Earth to battle lawlessness and die a more 
noble, deserving death (something that was, like Dredd’s own 
resignation, covered up).
McGruder was succeeded by Chief Judge Volt. Volt was 
elected over the other candidates, which included Judge 
Dredd himself (Dredd ironically voting for Volt rather than 
himself). Under Volt’s reforms, the Council of Five was 
reinstated and a new, elected civilian body created, which 
included the abolished position of Mayor, to rebuild the 
city’s shattered trust in the Justice Department. Despite an 
outstanding record against crime in those sectors where he 
had had command and his radical social reforms, Volt was 
unable to prevent the Second Robot War of 2121 and Volt 
took the situation personally. Although a distinguished and 
able commander, the burden of wartime leadership was too 
much for him and at the moment of victory over the robots 
Volt took his own life.
After Volt came Chief Judge Hershey. A popular and 
distinguished Street Judge, Hershey accompanied Dredd 
on the quest for the Judge Child, faced-up to Fink Angel 
and formed part of Dredd’s elite strike team against East 
Meg One during the Apocalypse War. Hershey rose through 
the ranks to become a senior Judge and was part of the 
delegation that attempted to convince McGruder to reinstate 
the Council of Five – even though this was unsuccessful. 
She succeeded Volt in 2121, having been appointed to the 
role of Deputy Chief Judge by Volt the year before. Despite 
following Volt’s social reforms Hershey maintained a stern 
side and has shown that she is every bit as capable of taking 
tough decisions and twisting the law when necessary (using 
assassins against the leaders of Neocuba, for instance). 
However, she constantly relied on Dredd for guidance and 
advice and, in 2130, she repealed the anti-mutant laws 
that had banned mutants from residing within the city. This 
created a great deal of unpopularity amongst both Judges 
and citizens and a campaign was mounted to have her voted 
out of ofﬁ ce, to be replaced with someone more authoritarian 
who would reinstate the anti-mutant acts.

===PAGE 60===
 The Justice Department
59
Hershey was replaced in 2131 by Chief Judge Dan Francisco. 
Francisco was a Street Judge who was made famous by a 
24-hour reality TV show following his exploits. Due to his 
fame, in 2131, he was chosen as a candidate to run against 
Hershey for Chief Judge with the intention of bringing back 
mutant apartheid. He was put forward as a candidate by 
Judges Cardew, Sinﬁ eld, and Millan, and won by a landslide 
after the anti-mutant Norm Supremacists organised a mutant 
gang attack on him (in order to manipulate and rally support 
for Francisco).
The  Council of Five
Despite having been disbanded by McGruder and then 
reformed under Chief Judge Volt, the Council of Five has 
always maintained a symbolic and political integrity. The 
Council of Five acts as the advisory body to the Chief Judge 
and comprises of Senior Judges, often the heads of the 
Justice Department’s main divisions. The Chief Judge is 
responsible for appointing the council-members and may 
expel existing councillors and introduce new members from 
any division considered appropriate. Should the Chief Judge 
be incapacitated and no longer able to carry out his or her 
duties, the council elects an interim replacement until a 
formal election can be organised for the successor. More 
often than not, the new Chief Judge will rise from the Council 
of Five’s own ranks. On occasion, a Deputy Chief Judge – the 
Chief Judge’s most trusted adviser – has occupied a slot in 
the Council of Five, although Chief Judge Hershey replaced 
the Deputy Chief position with a Wally Squad operative, an 
individual with closer links to the common citizen than the 
ofﬁ ce-bound Division Chiefs.
The lack of the Council of Five during McGruder’s tenure 
demonstrated the absolute need for the of ﬁ ce. Had the 
council been in place during Silver’s tenure, it is likely that his 
draconian war against democracy would never have been 
allowed to manifest. The council provides valuable checks 
and balances against executive decision-making and to be 
completely effective it requires as wide a representation as 
possible and naturally contrasting views. 
 Senior Judges
The Judges have no particular ranks within their divisions; 
there are no sergeants, inspectors, lieutenants or chief 
inspectors. One is either a Judge or a Senior Judge.
Senior Judges are awarded that title not necessarily through 
length of service (although that is a factor) but through 
their record of service. Arrest records, experience on the 
streets, knowledge of the law, knowledge of the how the city 
operates – all these things are taken into consideration for 
awarding a Senior Judge his title. Some Senior Judges are 
Becoming a Senior Judge
Judge Dredd characters can become Senior Judges 
although there is no hard and fast game mechanism for 
doing so. The Referee is the ﬁ nal arbiter on when a player 
character Judge is eligible for such a promotion and things 
for him to take into consideration are as follows:
Age: There is no minimum age for promotion to Senior 
Judge but it is highly unlikely that any Judge who has served 
for less than two terms (eight years) will be considered.
Experience: The number of four-year terms the 
Judge has served on the streets or in a specialist 
division. No Senior Judge should have served for less 
than two full terms but otherwise there is no speci ﬁ c 
lower limit and no qualifying threshold.
Arrest Record: Takes into account not simply the 
number of arrests a Judge has made but how many 
major arrests (for major crimes) and the diversity of his 
arrest record. Referees may want to keep a tally of the 
arrests the Player Characters make, in preparation for 
awarding the title of Senior Judge at some later date.
Competence: The competence of a Judge should be 
measured by his skills. Any Judge that has four or more skills 
at level 4 or higher is certainly eligible for consideration as 
a Senior Judge, the other factors being taken into account. 
Competence on its own is no guarantee of promotion.
Diligence: This is more difﬁ cult to measure but essentially 
diligence is how the Player Character has handled himself in 
respect to his role as a Judge. Has he always upheld justice? 
Has he applied the law fairly and dispassionately? Has he 
made any major mistakes that might have discredited the 
Justice Department, Sector House or himself? All these 
things should be weighed-up carefully by the Referee.
If the Referee believes a Judge has met all the above criteria 
– or even just some of them (but met them well) then he may 
award the character the title of Senior Judge. He may also, 
if he believes the character is worthy of the responsibility, 
award him command of a Sector House. If so, then the 
character can rightly use the title of Sector Chief.
Similarly, if a player believes his Judge has earned the 
right to promotion he can run through the above criteria 
and attempt to convince the Referee of his eligibility. If 
the Referee agrees, then that character gains promotion. 
However, the Referee’s word is always ﬁ nal and no 
player can insist on being promoted. Also, only 
the Referee can decide if a character should be 
awarded command of a Sector House; the 
player does not have the right to suggest it.

===PAGE 61===
 The Justice Department
 The Justice Department
60

===PAGE 62===
 The Justice Department
61
relatively young but they have demonstrated their capability, 
competence and dedication time and again in the service of 
the Justice Department and Mega-City One. Foremost of the 
Senior Judges is, of course, Judge Dredd but he is not the 
only Senior Judge and his approach to law enforcement not 
necessarily typical of all Senior Judges, either.
Senior Judges may be, depending on their competence, 
awarded the command of a Sector House. Thus, each 
Sector of Mega-City One has a Senior Judge in command 
of its judicial operations. All Sector Chiefs are answerable to 
the Council of Five and the Chief Judge, reporting to both on 
a weekly basis. Senior Judges who do not occupy a Sector 
Chief position are no less inﬂ uential; they can be called upon 
by the Council of Five at any time for advice or an opinion 
and the Chief Judge frequently has her own body of trusted 
Senior Judge advisers (typically those who she associated 
with during her career as a Street Judge).
Only Senior Judges are allowed, following the Volt Reforms, 
to vote for the Chief Judge and they can also stand for the 
position of Chief Judge when it becomes available.
Specialised 
Units
 Psi Division
Justice Department research into the paranormal began 
early in its history. Evidence of psionic capabilities had long 
been suspected but scepticism within the government had 
meant that dedicated psi investigation programmes had 
been poorly funded and their results derided by the sceptics 
who maintained that, simply put, ‘there was no such thing’.
Fargo and his comrades were far less sceptical. Dedicated 
research into psionic talent uncovered, conclusively, that 
certain individuals are able to tap into the considerable powers 
of the mind and demonstrate considerable abilities regarding 
pre-cognition, telepathy, telempathy, and other unusual 
mental traits. The bene ﬁ ts of such talents to the Justice 
Department were considerable: the ability to predict crime and 
perhaps apprehend its perpetrators before they committed it 
was attractive, as was the ability to probe the human mind for 
evidence on guilt after a crime had been committed.
Psi Division was formed to identify psionic talent in cadets, 
develop that talent and then use it to establish an elite team 
of Judges who would use their powers to support the judicial 
process – both in the prediction and solving of crime but also 
wide-scale threats to the city.
All cadets are tested for psionic talent. Those demonstrating 
it can opt to become Psi Division Judges. If so, their career 
path is dedicated to the development of their powers and, 
following graduation, supporting the Justice Department 
through applying their powers to crime prediction, prevention 
and solving.
Psi Division’s nominal base is within the Hall of Justice 
but it has its own dedicated facilities – where psi talent is 
exclusively trained – elsewhere in Mega-City One. Psi 
Judges are stationed either at the Grand Hall of Justice (the 
very best, who serve the city as a whole), a Psi facility (for 
the development of psi talent and involvement in lengthy or 
complex cases) or a Sector House, where they support the 
Street Judges on everyday activities. Psi Judges are not 
numerous; despite the bizarreness of Mega-City One in the 
22
nd Century, psions are still a rarity but there are enough Psi 
Judges so that a squad of between two and twelve (2d6) Psi 
Judges can be stationed at a Sector House to support that 
Sector’s work.
All newly graduated Psi Judges are assigned to a Sector 
House for the ﬁ rst four years of their formal career. Here they 
gain the valuable experience of street life that is essential to 
any Judge. After that, they may continue to serve a Sector 
or may be transferred to a specialist facility for research and 
development duties. After a further four years and depending 
on how the Psi Judge’s powers have developed in that time, 
the opportunity to transfer to the Psi Unit of Justice Central 
arises. Taking this opportunity means temporarily leaving 
street service and becoming party to the deep secrets of 
Mega-City One and Justice Department. The centralised 
Psi units focus on monitoring the known dimensions of 
existence, conducting long-term clairvoyant research into 
potential threats and providing forensic psi specialisation 
into the largest and most important cases.
Of course, Psi Judges may remain in service at a Sector 
House and many do. Psi Judges at this level are of 
considerable beneﬁ t to the Street Judges in combating local 
crime. Psi Judges can and do, undertake active patrols with 
Street Judges but usually they work from the Sector House 
in a support function. Training and developing psionic talent 
is expensive and Psi Judges are not numerous; risking their 
lives on the street is neither advantageous nor a prudent use 
of their talents. But it is understood that, although Psi Judges 
are a rare resource, conﬁ ning them to backroom work leads 
to boredom and stress; active work on the streets, as part of 
a patrol team, is a valuable way of maintaining a Psi Judge’s 
effectiveness and maximising their contribution to justice.

===PAGE 63===
 The Justice Department
62
Specialised Psi Units
Working from the Grand Hall of Justice, Psi Division 
maintains several specialist units of its own, each focused 
on a particular area of psionic talents:
Pre-Cogs specialise in pre-cognition and clairvoyance. 
Their job is to concentrate on the prediction of crime, mostly 
high-proﬁ le cases and citywide threats. Their leads come 
from a variety of sources but mostly from visions senior 
pre-cogs have experienced which are then subsequently 
investigated and veriﬁ ed for accuracy by the junior members 
of the Pre-Cog unit. Once the accuracy of a particular 
vision has been established and more detail on its nature 
gleaned, the ﬁ ndings are referred either to Sector Houses, 
so that Street Judges can act promptly on the information; 
or directly to the Council of Five and the Chief Judge, if the 
information concerns city security at large. It was the senior 
Pre-Cogs who discovered the existence of the Judge Child, 
initiating Judges Dredd, Hershey, Lopez and Larter’s search 
for Owen Krysler across the galaxy.
Trans-D specialises in studying and monitoring the known 
parallel dimensions mirroring Mega-City One, in particular 
the Dead World, the place where Judge Death and the Dark 
Judges originate. Despite the proven existence of parallel 
dimensions, Psi Division still has very limited powers in 
entering or communicating with these separate realities and 
so its activities, largely conducted in complete secrecy, are 
conﬁ ned to monitoring and reporting on what is found – and 
whether or not a risk to the city is posed.
K-Unit is the team dedicated to the use of offensive and 
defensive psionic talents. Few Judges have the ability to 
use the powers of the mind for inﬂ icting mental, emotional or 
physical damage and, following the appearance of Shujan, 
Warlord of Ji, it has been realised that pursuing psionic 
talent to such ends is inherently destabilising and dangerous 
for the Justice Department. Nevertheless, when necessary, 
K-Unit can be summoned to use their combative powers in 
defence of the city or against certain powerful psionic foes.
Psyker-Unit monitors criminal psions – psykers – who 
are in the employ of organised criminal gangs. Psyker-
Unit uses a broad palette of psionic talents to identify, 
monitor and then report to Sector Houses, the activities and 
movements of psykers, allowing the Street Judges to act on 
the information. As many psykers have their own, formidable 
psionic defences, Psyker-Unit’s work is not always easy and 
it remains a full-time job for the Psi Judges who are assigned 
to its duties.
 Tek Division
The Justice Department has always maintained a healthy 
interest in the development and deployment of technology 
in the war against crime. This interest has diversi ﬁ ed 
considerably: Tek Division is not simply a division of 
engineers and mechanics but a talented and wide-reaching 
division of scientists. Tek Division involves itself in research 
and development into just about every scienti ﬁ c area. With 
the considerable ﬁ nancial reserves of Justice Department at 
its disposal, Tek Division has advanced scientiﬁ c knowledge 
in many areas ignored by the private scienti ﬁ c community. 
Tek Division has successfully investigated teleportation, 
dimensional travel, cloning, exotic disease, space travel 
and so forth. Working with the Med and Psi divisions it has 
made scienti ﬁ c advances that dwarf those of the 20
th and 
21st Centuries.
The visible face of Tek Division are the rank and ﬁ le Tek 
Judges. As part of one of the largest divisions of the Justice 
Department outside of Street-Div, the Tek Judge has the 
opportunity to pursue his technical training to almost any end. 
All Tek Judges spend the ﬁ rst four years of their career in the 
vehicle bays and armouries of Sector Houses, where they are 
engaged in maintaining and repairing equipment – although a 
few of the luckier ones are assigned to join street patrols where 
their unique skills can prove beneﬁ cial to many investigations 
of technologically advanced crimes. After four years, Tek 
Judges have the opportunity to transfer to a specialist research 
division where they become involved in speci ﬁ c areas of 
design, research, development and maintenance.
Specialist Tek Units
Weapons: The Weapons unit is involved in the research 
and development of the Justice Department arsenal. The 
oldest of the specialised Tek units, Weapons was created 
in the early days of Justice Department when it became 
apparent that President Booth was taking an ever-more 
dangerous direction to committing America to atomic war. 
Judge Fargo’s advice to Chief Judge Solomon was to 
prepare the Judges for war by concentrating on armaments 
and the tools for civil defence. That logic has prevailed and 
the Weapons Unit of Tek Division focuses on developing and 
improving all the arms and armament Justice Department 
needs in performing its role. Weapons Unit developed the Mk 
I Lawgiver, including perfecting the ability of the weapon to 
ﬁ re specialised ammunition and the DNA recognition sensors 
built into every Lawgiver’s grip, preventing non-Judges from 
using the weapon. The unit also developed weapons such 
as the sonic cannon, which is used more by Citi-Def units 
than Judges but which provides a valuable part of the city’s 

===PAGE 64===
 The Justice Department
63
defensive arsenal. More intriguing products of Weapons 
Unit include riot foam – especially successful in subduing 
the angered masses safely – and stumm gas, which is far 
more brutal in its approach to controlling civil unrest.
Vehicles: With such a vast area to be controlled and 
with such a complex highway infrastructure, the Justice 
Department needs the very best and most diverse vehicles 
it can develop. Vehicles Unit specialises in researching, 
developing and manufacturing new vehicle designs, reﬁ ning 
existing models and researching potential new prototypes. 
From the V-Unit R&D labs have come the Manta tank, the 
Land Raider and the ubiquitous H and P wagons. Vehicles 
also has an entire wing dedicated to the space ﬂ eet; the small 
compliment of starships occasionally used for long-range 
missions within and without the solar system. Members of 
the V Unit are all skilled drivers, ﬂ yers and pilots but also 
have the requisite skills for maintaining and augmenting the 
craft they are familiar with.
Forensics: The Forensics Unit works closely with the 
Med Judges to apply forensic science to existing cases and 
for developing more advanced forensic techniques to aid 
Justice Department’s work. Detailed medical forensics is 
the province of the Med Judges but technical and scienti ﬁ c 
forensics are handled by the Tek Judges of Forensics Unit: 
ballistics, computer forensics, vehicle forensics, building 
forensics and so forth. A highly motivated – some would say 
obsessive – team, with a highly specialised job, Forensics 
Units are found at each Sector House supporting local law 
enforcement, as well as having a headquarters at the Grand 
Hall of Justice.
Psi Sciences: Working with Psi Division, Psi-Sciences 
develops the machinery and science that supports and 
augments the work of Psi Division; technology to both 
enhance and restrict psionic powers. Psi Sciences are also 
responsible for Justice Department’s ongoing research into 
teleportation, time travel and dimensional travel, using the 
unique capabilities of Psi Division to inform the development 
and testing processes. Psi Science’s work is highly secretive 
and conducted in the fortiﬁ ed, shielded, underground bunker-
laboratories far beneath the Hall of Justice.
Space Sciences: Astronomers, planetologists and 
astrophysicists, the Tek Judges of Space Sciences specialise 
in the study of the galaxy. Their work has contributed 
much to the Justice Department’s ability to understand 
alien civilisations and has enhanced the effectiveness of 
Justice Department spaceships, such as Justice 1. A small 
department, Space Sciences tends to follow a narrow 
agenda and spends much of its time assisting other Tek 
Judges with their projects; but whenever there is a need for 
any Judge to make an off-world expedition, Space Sciences 
usually provides an assessment report and brie ﬁ ng for the 
Judges concerned.
Life Sciences: The Life Sciences unit concerns itself with 
the ﬂ ora and fauna of the Earth, particularly so given the wild 
mutations that have arisen since the Atomic War and the 
Apocalypse War. Life Sciences collects specimens, studies 
them and then extrapolates the results to assist with the work 
of the Justice Department. For example, the phenomenon of 
the stookie’s anifax gland, which reverses aging, has been 
widely studied by Life Sciences in conjunction with Space 
Sciences. The unit’s Mutation Studies Group is renowned for 
its understanding of the effects of mutation and how to handle 
those who use mutation to turn to crime. The biggest inter-
unit co-operation is with the Med Division: Life Sciences and 
the Med Division have worked closely on cloning research, 
brain and memory manipulation (as witnessed in the work 
done on Mean Machine Angel) and other areas designed to 
either enhance the effectiveness of Judges or manipulating 
the psyche of perps.
 SJS
The Special Judicial Squad is a semi-independent organisation 
within the Justice Department, responsible for monitoring the 
behaviour of all Judges within Mega-City One. Routinely, the 
SJS handles all complaints made against speci ﬁ c Judges, 
making recommendations to Sector Chiefs for disciplinary 
proceedings. However, the SJS’s fearful reputation comes 
from the power they have at their disposal when investigating 
a fellow Judge for corruption and murder – their authority is 
absolute and their jurisdiction all-encompassing. Even senior 
Judges are not immune to investigation, interrogation and 
arrest at the hands of the SJS if they prove wanting. The SJS 
usually operates as small self-contained squads of Judges 
charged with speciﬁ c investigations, though they have been 
known to assign undercover SJS Judges within regular street 
patrols in order to monitor the behaviour of Judges going 
about their normal duties.
The reputation of the SJS is well justi ﬁ ed; Judges who 
form its ranks are all proven hardliners with a track record 
for being merciless applicants of the law where standard 
perps are concerned and equally diligent when it comes to 
judging their colleagues. The SJS Judges are selected for 
their implacability, absolute dedication, complete loyalty to 
their division chief and obsessive dedication to rooting-out 
– and dealing with – internal corruption. Most Street Judges 
are wary of their involvement in any case at any level and 
rightly so: on occasions the SJS’s obsession with identifying 
and crushing wrong-doing within the Justice Department 

===PAGE 65===
 The Justice Department
64
has led to some rash and incorrect decisions. Many Street 
Judges would rather handle instances of Judge wrong-doing 
themselves rather than get the SJS involved directly, mainly 
because they can take the time to investigate impartially and 
thoroughly. The SJS would take a similar approach but their 
hard-line attitude means that minor, sometimes justi ﬁ ed, 
transgressions are twisted into unforgivable instances of guilt 
that send otherwise good Judges for a stretch on Titan.
Another issue with the SJS, and one that cannot be 
overlooked, is the entire Judge Cal incident. When the insane 
Deputy Chief Judge and head of the SJS, seized control of 
Mega-City One, the SJS became his praetorian guard: most 
were brainwashed into pledging loyalty but some were quite 
obviously keen to offer their loyalty without the need for 
brainwashing. In Cal’s reign the SJS became an instrument 
of terror, abusing their powers to a degree that would forever 
tarnish their reputation. After Cal died, deputy Chief Judge 
McGruder worked hard to restore the SJS’s reputation 
and systematically weeded-out all those who exhibited the 
excessive traits Cal exploited. Still, the whole Cal incident is 
remembered by many rank and ﬁ le Judges and there is the 
unspoken belief that, for all McGruder’s work, the SJS could 
be all too ready to adopt a similar, power-abusive position, if 
similar circumstances were to arise.
Internal Investigations
All Judges are subject to periodic Accountability tests 
conducted by the SJS. A Judge’s arrests and case ﬁ les are 
scrutinised and challenged and, if any questionable area is 
uncovered, the Judge is summoned before an SJS investigator 
to explain the circumstances. These interrogations are hard, 
uncompromising and often uncomfortable. The Judge is 
subject to constant lie detector monitoring throughout and 
the SJS probe into every tiny detail before they are satisﬁ ed 
that the Judge is clean.
To see if a Judge is subject to an Accountability investigation, 
roll 2d6 each year. If the result is a natural 2, then the SJS 
investigate. If the Judge has failed any Law check through 
rolling a natural 2, then that particular case is the focus of the 
investigation. The Judge must succeed in an In ﬂ uence 8+ 
roll to satisfy the SJS that no corruption or wrongdoing has 
taken place. If he fails, the SJS launches a more detailed 
investigation, going through the Judge’s entire arrest 
and case records searching for evidence that will build a 
case against him. The Referee should adjudicate such 
circumstances, either abstracting the results or perhaps 
using the investigation as the basis for a scenario.
Responsibility of Judges
No Judge is above the law. Any Judge who breaks the 
law, either unconsciously or deliberately, is subject to 
investigation by the SJS. Every Judge is obliged to report 
any colleague who is suspected of abusing his or her 
power or transgressing the law to the SJS Judge assigned 
to his Sector.
Of course, not every Street Judge ful ﬁ ls this obligation 
unless there is clearly no alternative course of action. 
There are numerous mitigating circumstances that could 
and should, be taken into consideration before a Judge 
is judged, and many rank and ﬁ le Judges simply do not 
believe that the SJS approach an internal investigation 
with this mitigation in mind. However, any Judge who fails 
to report a colleague’s wrongdoing is guilty of withholding 
information and perverting the course of justice and will, 
himself, be subject to SJS scrutiny.
Referees – and players – therefore need to look carefully 
at how their characters conduct themselves. Any Judge 
character who abuses his position is liable for an investigation; 
and his colleagues have a duty to report him to the SJS...
Disciplinary Action
Despite the assurances of the Justice Department’s Public 
Relations Unit, Judges are only human and very few will 
go through their careers without some form of disciplinary 
action staining their records. Each Judge is expected to be 
the perfect embodiment of a hard but fair judicial system but 
they risk their lives every day patrolling the streets, ensuring 
the safety of a population who, by and large, are ungrateful. 
When a Judge turns bad, if only for a brief moment, it is 
of great concern to the Justice Department and disciplinary 
action tends to be both harsh and swift. 
Whilst Judges have the beneﬁ t of a ﬁ fteen year long training 
program, players do not and so it is somewhat inevitable 
that minor transgressions of the law will occur throughout 
the game. However, it has also been made clear throughout 
the Judge Dredd game as to just what being a Judge truly 
entails and so players should make special efforts in order 
to curb more excessive behaviour. When a player in control 
of a Judge performs an action that is contrary to the nature 
of Judges and the Justice Department, the Referee should 
warn the player of the consequences of his actions. He may 
get away with nefarious behaviour for a while but, sooner 
or later, the Justice Department will ﬁ nd out what he is 
doing. It may even be another Player Character Judge who 

===PAGE 66===
 The Justice Department
65
informs the SJS or the Sector Chief of his activities. When 
a Player Character is caught by the Justice Department 
performing actions that are unbecoming of a Judge, the 
Referee has four options of disciplinary action that he can 
impose upon the player, each depending on the severity of 
his transgressions:
Penal Colony of  Titan
This is the dread sentence every Judge fears. With a 
minimum sentence of twenty years, criminal Judges are 
sent to the penal colony on the moon of Titan to serve an 
agonising term of hard labour. Humans cannot breathe 
the thin, almost non-existent, atmosphere of Titan and 
so prisoners are biologically modified to function in this 
harsh, cold environment. Upon completing their sentence, 
prisoners are returned to earth, where they are carefully 
watched by the Justice Department as they try to adapt to 
becoming normal citizens and leave behind their shattered 
lives. Servitude on Titan is generally reserved only for 
the most heinous of crimes: treason, mass murder and 
murder of a fellow Judge. Any Player Character Judge 
sent to Titan is generally assumed to have been removed 
from the campaign.
Cursed Earth Penal Work 
Camps
With a minimum sentence of ten years, it is believed in the 
higher circles of the Justice Department that imprisonment 
within a Cursed Earth penal camp is somewhat less harsh 
than Titan, though prisoners may not be so sure. Frequent 
rad-storms and raids by mutant gangs can often end a 
prisoner’s life in the most brutal of ways and the hard labour 
forced upon them claims still more. Upon returning to Mega-
City One, prisoners are vetted by the Justice Department to 
ensure they have picked up no Cursed Earth infections or 
mutations but are then left to fend for themselves as ordinary 
citizens. Imprisonment within the penal camps is usually 
reserved for blatant transgressions of the Law - accepting 
bribes, torturing citizens and embezzling Justice Department 
funds can all earn a Judge a place in the Cursed Earth, 
As with servitude on Titan, any Player Character Judge 
assigned to a penal camp may be assumed to have been 
removed from the campaign.
Discharge
Judges not convicted of actual crimes may still be discharged 
from the Justice Department. This is usually the course of 
action taken for Judges who ﬂ agrantly disobey regulations, 
such as having a love affair with a citizen or fellow Judge 
or who simply fail to make the grade as a worthy Judge 
despite their years of training. Such Judges, whilst no longer 
permitted to take the law to the streets, are often offered 
auxiliary positions within the Public Surveillance Unit or as 
aides to Sector Chiefs, though some opt to leave the Justice 
Department altogether and adopt the life of a citizen. In very 
rare cases, a Judge with a previously exemplary record may 
not be discharged and instead reprimanded. Any Judge 
discharged from the Justice Department is assumed to have 
been removed from the game unless every Player Character 
Judge is simultaneously discharged
 for gross negligence.
The  Long Walk
Where a Judge has clearly exhibited an error of judgement 
that does not warrant a criminal penalty – or if he simply 
decides that he cannot serve on the streets to the best of his 
ability any longer – he can opt to take the Long Walk.
Equipped with his Lawgiver and a shoulder-arm of his own 
choice, the Judge leaves Mega-City One via the Sector One 
gate and strides, alone, into the Cursed Earth, ostensibly 
to bring justice to the lawless masses of the American 
wastelands. If a Judge is retiring honourably, then his walk 
out of the city is marked by an Honour Guard and a volley of 
Lawgiver ﬁ re. If he is taking the option for less honourable 
reasons, then his passage out of the city is marked only by 
those who choose to come and see him off. Taking the Long 
Walk (or being asked to think about taking it) is considered 
severe enough a penalty to atone for minor transgressions 
since the survival rate for most Judges is weeks or months, 
rather than years.
 Street Judges
The Street Judges are the rank and ﬁ le of the Justice 
Department. Its most visible presence, they are responsible 
for the routine enforcement of law and order on Mega-City 
One’s streets.
Every Street Judge is assigned to a Sector for a minimum 
period of 4 years. At the end of this period they may be 
transferred to a new Sector, in order to broaden their 
knowledge and experience or be retained at the existing 
Sector, to continue their good work. Some senior Judges, 
like Dredd, for example, are allowed ‘free street’ status; that 
is, they are not assigned to a Sector and are allowed to patrol 
Mega-City One’s streets under the direction of the Council of 
Five or the Chief Judge according to where the Judge will be 
most effective. For most Player Character Judges, however, 
a Sector assignment is mandatory.
Judges are expected to work semi-independently but it is the 
norm for Judges to be grouped into small teams of between 
3 and 6 known as Patrols. A Patrol works a certain area, 
performing a certain duty, for one or more watches (see 
below). The members of the Patrol are the ﬁ rst call for back 

===PAGE 67===
 The Justice Department
66
up and are expected to know where their colleagues are at 
any given time and to offer necessary support automatically. 
It is common for Patrol members to work different areas 
whilst on duty, rather than remaining in a group but how 
the Patrol deploys is down to its members to decide. If a 
Patrol includes a Senior Judge, then he takes command of 
the Patrol. If not, a Patrol Head must be assigned and this 
is usually a decision taken by the members of the Patrol 
with the Patrol Head role being assigned to a different team 
member each watch. Some Patrols do not switch their Patrol 
Head, having formed a comfortable working unit with a 
natural leader.
Day in the Life of a Judge
A typical day for a Street Judge begins when his ﬁ rst shift (or 
watch) starts. There are three eight-hour shifts within each 
day – A-Watch (beginning at 06:00hrs), B-Watch and C-
Watch (ending at 06:00hrs). These shifts usually begin when 
the Judge awakes from his mandatory eight hours sleep (or 
ten minutes in a sleep machine) and so each Street Judge 
will take two shifts in every 24 hour period. He breakfasts and 
then attends a compulsory brieﬁ ng session at Sector House 
control for a general overview of what he can expect to 
encounter in the sector during his shift, this being conducted 
by a duty of ﬁ cer known as the Watch Commander. Every 
Sector House usually has three Watch Commanders and 
once the brie ﬁ ng is over, Tac Group Leaders organise 
Street Judges into their patrol groups and assign them 
speciﬁ c duties or details. Unlike the Watch Commanders, 
Tac Group Leaders will oversee their colleagues’ activities 
on the street personally or by radio contact. Responding 
to the unpredictability of Mega-City One crime and Justice 
Department cutbacks, Judges may be called upon to work 
a second consecutive shift and sometimes twenty-four hour 
stretches are necessary. Under these circumstances, Judges 
are ordered to take ten minutes in the Sleep Machine prior to 
embarking upon another patrol. Drug stimulants are usually 
carried on patrol to counteract further signs of fatigue.
Duties and Details
Tac Group Commanders (always Senior Judges) assign 
every Patrol with a Detail for that watch. The Details and 
duties are summarised in the Watch Detail table. The Referee 
acts as the Tac Group Commander and may either assign 
a speciﬁ c detail or case, or role randomly to determine the 
activities for the watch.
Watch Detail 
2d6 Detail
2-8 General Patrol
9-12 Special Detail (Roll 2d6):
2 Block Patrol
3 Block War
4 Crime Blitz
5 Crowd Control
6 H Wagon Duty
7 Mall Detail
8 Pat Wagon Duty
9 Speciﬁ c Case
10 Surveillance
11-12 Trafﬁ c Detail
General Patrol
The Patrol is assigned to general Lawmaster patrol of a 
particular area within a Sector. Essentially the Judges are 
providing a visible street presence to deter crime and deal 
with spontaneous petty and serious crimes committed within 
their patrol area.
Block Patrol
As for General Patrol but the Patrol team is assigned to a 
speciﬁ c City Block where they patrol its levels, boulevards 
and plazas. 
 Block War 
The patrol is assigned to assist in quelling a Block War in 
their sector. Block Wars are incredibly dif ﬁ cult to control 
and require the full arsenal of a Sector House: H Wagons, 
Pat Wagons, Riot Squads, Manta Prowl tanks and so forth. 
A variety of tactics are used – from sonic cannon to stun 
combatants, through to surgical heavy-weapon strikes on 
the most recalcitrant and heavily defended offenders. As a 
result, the patrol may be involved in any and all of these 
activities.
One area of a Block War they will be involved with is the 
aftermath. Floor-by-ﬂ oor crime blitzes of a city block inevitably 
follow a Block War, with teams of Judges using lie detectors 
to verify who, and who was not, involved in the carnage. 
Tek Judges frequently accompany these patrols gathering 
essential forensic evidence which is used to support arrests 
and sentencing. Common dangers involve combatants who 

===PAGE 68===
 The Justice Department
67
refuse to lay down their arms and are prepared to take out 
a Judge or two if they cannot continue warring with the 
opposing block. Thus, Block War aftermath patrols are 
laborious and hazardous; and every Judge assigned to this 
detail is on perpetual guard.
Crime Blitz
The Patrol is responsible for conducting a Crime Blitz – a 
random, detailed inspection of a single citizen’s premises, 
looking for evidence of even the smallest crime and 
dispensing justice accordingly. A patrol works together on 
a Crime Blitz and spends the whole watch focused on the 
home of one citizen. The Patrol usually has a Tek Judge 
accompanying them for technical and forensics support or 
can summon a Tek Squad via Sector Control if necessary.
Crowd Control
Crowds are a frequent problem in Mega-City One: either 
organised demonstrations, crowds gathering as an audience 
for some performance or another; fan-groups of the latest vid-
star pin-up, and so on. Given the fractious nature of Mega-
City One citizens, wherever there is a crowd, there is trouble. 
Taps, rabble-rousers and the like are always attracted to a 
crowd, there to exploit the mass mentality in whatever way. 
And, even if petty criminals or troublemakers are not present 
or have been dealt with, crowds can turn ugly and violent 
for the tiniest of reasons (pushing, shoving, queue-jumping 
and so on). A Patrol’s duty is to deal with the petty criminals, 
watch the crowd for signs of trouble (and stamp on it) and 
deal with trouble when it inevitably ﬂ ares. Patrols can call 
on Pat Wagons equipped with riot foam for such incidents 
although, by and large, they are capable of dealing with most 
incidents without the need for heavy back up.
H Wagon Duty
The Patrol is assigned to an H Wagon, performing an aerial 
patrol of the Sector. The H Wagon is piloted by a member 
of Tek Division and the Patrol Team is responsible for 
responding to requests for aerial surveillance, pursuit, back-
up and intervention.
Mall Detail
Similar to General or Block Patrol but the Patrol team 
is located in one of Mega-City One’s vast, labyrinthine, 
shopping malls that are the size of a city block. Mall Detail 
involves dealing with shoplifters, taps, irate shoppers and 
unscrupulous shopkeepers keen to con honest citizens out 
of their hard-scrounged credits.
Pat Wagon Duty
The Patrol team is assigned to Pat Wagon duty; patrolling 
the streets aboard a Sector Pat Wagon and supporting 
Judges on general patrol or other duties. Pat Wagon duty 
can involve anything from perp pick-up and processing to 
riot control.
Specific Case
The Patrol team is assigned to investigate a speci ﬁ c kind of 
case or to support another Judge or team of Judges in an 
investigation. The Referee should decide the nature of the 
case (which should not be a routine, easily solved crime, 
where the perp is apprehended) and at what point the team 
is assigned to it. The team might be assigned as soon as the 
case is reported or comes to light; alternatively, they might 
take over from another team which is ﬁ nishing its watch and 
needs to be briefed on progress so far. The kinds of duties 
vary from case to case but common elements include:
• Interviewing suspects
• Searching for or processing evidence at the crime scene
• Following-up on leads
• Detailed surveillance
• Investigating possible linked cases or incidents
• Listening to and processing confessions and alibis from 
suspects
Surveillance
The team is assigned to surveillance. This may take a variety 
of forms:
• General surveillance of an area where a number of 
crimes have been committed
• Surveillance of a speciﬁ c building
• Detailed, round the clock surveillance of a suspect or 
witness in a crime
• Remote surveillance using Spy in the Sky drones, again 
on a speciﬁ c person
• Stake-out of an area that may be the scene of a crime
Surveillance details can be con ﬁ ned to a single watch or, 
by necessity, require extended watches to be effective. 
Surveillance can be routine or focused on a speci ﬁ c crime 
– therefore a surveillance detail might be part of a speci ﬁ c 
case (see above) although the Judges are not involved in 
any other aspect of that case.
Traffic Detail
The Patrol team is assigned to cruising the skedways, 
megways and other highways of Mega-City One, on the 
lookout for traffic and other road-committed offences. 
Much of the time is spent in the Lawmaster saddle, 
watching for speeders and slowsters, jay-walkers, 
aggressive drivers, road-ragers and so on. Judges are 
generally confined to the highways of their sector and, 
if required to give pursuit, must notify control if they are 
required to pass into another Sector.

===PAGE 69===
 The Justice Department
68
 Med Judges
Med Judges ful ﬁ l a triple role: the welfare and treatment of 
Judges and citizens (including perps injured whilst being 
apprehended); stafﬁ ng the Sector House hospital wings; and 
research, working closely with Tek Division and Psi Division 
on speciﬁ c research projects.
Though trained to be compassionate, Med Judges soon 
become hardened to the realities of Mega-City One, inured 
to the terrors the citizens can inﬂ ict upon themselves and the 
Street Judges. The majority of Med Judges can be found 
within ambulance back-up squads or toiling away within the 
hospitals of the Sector House across the city, working to 
heal Street Judges in order to get them back onto the streets 
as quickly as possible. 
The ﬁ rst four years of a Med Judge’s career are spent 
attached to the hospital wing of a Sector House, rotating 
duties between tending to the injured in the med-bays and 
working the meat wagons in attendance at the scene of an 
incident or accompanying a Patrol team in its typical watch 
duties (although Med Judges tend to be summoned to 
a scene, rather than there as part of a Street Judge unit). 
After this initial four year term, Judges can transfer to other 
Sectors and gain the opportunity to specialise, joining the 
research teams who work under the aegis of the Hall of 
Justice but are based at laboratories and research facilities 
scattered across the city.
Senior Med Judges are able to specialise further within Med 
Division, researching cures to diseases, terminal radiation 
and lethally concocted poisons. Within a street patrol, the 
Med Judge is the best ally any of his colleagues may have – 
his specialised skills can often mean the difference between 
life and death to a seriously injured Street Judge wounded 
in the line of duty. 
 Wally Squad
Justice Department’s undercover team, the Wally Squad 
is tasked with attaining deep-cover and penetrating the 
underclasses of Mega-City One, posing as a citizen, perp or 
potential perp in order to bring criminals to justice.
The Wally Squad Judges are often treated with derision by 
those from Street Division but their undercover operations to 
inﬁ ltrate the largest criminal organisations within Mega-City 
One have proved vital to keeping law and order. To become 
a Judge in the Wally Squad is to leave behind much of the 
training of the Academy of Law and few have the aptitude 
necessary to adjust. A Wally Squad Judge becomes, to all 
intents and purposes, a citizen and his old colleagues often 
notice a great shift in attitude towards the application of 
the law. It has been known for Wally Squad Judges to sink 
too far into their cover identity and ‘turn native,’ sometimes 
escaping from the Justice Department completely to take 
over and run the criminal organisations they were sent to 
destroy. Wally Squad Judges are never deployed as part of 
ordinary street patrols.
Wally Squad Campaigns
As Wally Squad Judges are fundamentally different to 
other Justice Department divisions (being in plain clothes, 
for one), it is recommended that every member of a Player 
Character group be a part of a Wally Squad team, rather than 
a single individual. This makes it easy to keep Wally Squad 
characters integrated into the ﬂ ow of play. Nevertheless, 
extensive undercover ops are an excellent basis for a Judge 
Dredd campaign, with their own dangers, challenges and 
issues in the pursuit of justice.
Other Divisions
Accounts Division
The Justice Department is the most powerful organisation 
within Mega-City One but like all great bodies it is dependent 
on a steady credit ﬂ ow to maintain a competent level of public 
service. The Accounts Division (Acc-Div) works tirelessly 
to maintain ﬁ scal records, balance ledgers, set the annual 
budget for Justice Department operations and enforce taxes 
charged to citizens. A succession of disasters over the last 
three decades has damaged Mega-City One’s ﬁ nances, 
forcing the Justice Department to curb its recruitment 
programmes and cut back on support for Street Judges. 
Atlantic Division
Mega-City One’s territory extends 2,000 kilometres across 
the heavily polluted Black Atlantic ocean, halfway to Brit-Cit 
and marked by the undersea service-plex of Atlantis. Atlantic 
Division is responsible for patrolling these waters, ensuring 
both Justice Department and civilian transports are protected 
from pirates, mutant marine life forms and freak meteorological 
disturbances. Atlantic Division also polices the Mega-City One 
docks under the auspices of the Harbour Squad. Despite 
having learnt many painful lessons from its defeat at the onset 
of the Apocalypse War, long-standing budget restrictions 
mean Atlantic Division has yet to upgrade the ﬂ eet (including 
the underwater SKUNK missile defence stations) to efﬁ ciency 
levels comparable with Brit-Cit’s own, smaller navy. 

===PAGE 70===
 The Justice Department
69
Wally 
Squad

===PAGE 71===
 The Justice Department
70
 Public Surveillance Unit
The PSU is the organisation behind Mega-City One’s 
millions of street cameras and Spy-in-the-Sky units. Its 
brief is to monitor the public lives of all citizens from the 
moment they leave their apartments to when they once 
more enter and lock the door behind them. Tek personnel 
assisted by robots sort through the information gathered by 
the cameras and then classify it as one of four categories: 
benign, suspicious, threatening or dangerous. A suspicious 
rating prompts further surveillance and a long-term archive, 
as will threatening but the latter often validates the passive 
involvement of Street Division operatives. A dangerous rating 
is automatically routed to Street Division so that immediate 
action – preventative or curative – can be undertaken with 
minimum delay. The PSU is headquartered within the Statue 
of Judgement in Sector 44, governed by a ‘nerve centre’ sited 
in the Statue’s rockcrete head. Though citizens are unaware 
of this, the location of PSU is an everyday warning to all 
Judges that nobody is above the Law. One day, their own 
indiscretions may be captured on ﬁ lm slug and used against 
them. For twenty-two years, Judge Jura Edgar was head 
of PSU. Her lengthy tenure and high success rate proved 
decisively that few were better suited to the role but her 
obsession for information-gathering overstepped its bounds 
when Jura tried to blackmail high-ranking Judges with 
secret information contained within the so-called ‘Cal Files,’ 
a legacy of the terrible deeds carried out by the tyrannical 
Chief Judge Cal in 2101. When Chief Judge Hershey came 
to power in 2122, one of her ﬁ rst acts was to remove Jura 
from her post and exile her to Cursed Earth Work Camp 17. 
Hershey subsequently appointed former SJS Chief Niles as 
the new head of PSU.
 Space Corps
Space-Div augments the local administration of off-world 
colonies populated wholly or partially by Mega-City One 
expatriates. Many of these colonies have been founded 
on mineral-rich sites creating a basic local economy and a 
foundation for trade links between the colony and Mega-City 
One. It is standard practice for the Justice Department to 
leave the more remote colonies to settle their own disputes 
but should they be threatened or attacked by hostile forces, 
Space-Div can provide a swift military response by way of 
the Space Corps, an army of Judges and civilian soldiers 
backed by a mighty array of weaponry including starﬁ ghters 
and deep space battle cruisers.
 COE
COE stands for Covert Operations Establishment – the 
undercover espionage wing of the Justice Department 
employed to counteract threats to Mega-City One and 
outlying territories by native or alien parties through the 
gathering of tactical intelligence that is then disseminated 
to other Divisions for further analysis. The COE is incredibly 
secretive – more so than the SJS – and it reports directly to 
the Chief Judge rather than through the Council of Five.
 Sector Houses
The Sector House is the base for Judges and the 
representation of law in each of Mega-City One’s 305 separate 
sectors. All Judges will spend at least part of their career 
stationed at a Sector House; each functions as a miniature 
Grand Hall of Justice and is the headquarters of personnel 
assigned to maintain the law within the boundaries of a 
single sector. Sector Houses provide temporary detention 
facilities for criminal suspects until they can be properly dealt 
with by the judicial system and holding pens for convicted 
perps en-route to the iso-cubes. Every sector house has 
interrogation suites, armouries, brie ﬁ ng rooms, vehicle 
garages, ofﬁ ces and communication and control centres, 
the latter being responsible for co-ordinating the movements 
and activities of all on-duty Judges, notifying them of crimes 
in progress and arranging the all-important back-up. Sector 
Houses are built like fortresses; spiked with artillery to keep 
the more dangerous citizen riots and lunatic fringe groups at 
a distance and, perhaps most importantly, to keep perps and 
suspects in so that they can be processed.
Some Sector Houses were built before the Atomic War 
and are easily identi ﬁ ed by their height, as pre-atomic 
Sector Houses stand at around ﬁ fteen to thirty storeys high, 
whereas more recent constructions start at around ﬁ fty 
storeys. All Sector Houses are built to approximately the 
same dimensions and can easily accommodate two hundred 
staff, though numbers of three hundred and above are 
sometimes crammed into their conﬁ nes to deal with sectors 
suffering from high crime rates. All Sector Houses have at 
least one large garage where Lawmasters, Pat-Wagons 
and H-Wagons are maintained and repaired. Most Sector 
Houses also have a hospital wing where injured Judges, 
citizens and perps receive treatment for the kinds of wounds 
and injuries that do not require immediate transfer to a 
hospital. The medical facilities are not extensive and Judges 
are kept isolated from citizens and perps but, staffed by 
the highly competent Med Division Judges, they offer good 
medical facilities for dealing with the kinds of trauma most 
likely to have been incurred during the application of justice: 
cuts, bruises, contusions, concussions, broken bones, stab-
wounds and gunshot wounds.
The Sector House is powered by highly efﬁ cient fusion power 
generators designed to keep the wheels of justice moving 
and these are isolated from the main grid in the event of a 

===PAGE 72===
 The Justice Department
71
sector blackout. A state of the art megaband communications 
centre located at the top of the Sector House ensures any 
reports of crime or information vital to the welfare of the 
sector can be instantly processed and relayed to any Judge 
patrol in the vicinity. Sensors built into the holding posts 
that proliferate across Mega-City One relay information 
back to Sector Control: that a perp has been locked to one, 
when, the Judge who put him there, and brief details of the 
sentence. After criminals are arrested, they are brought into 
check-in areas by Catch Wagons where they are processed 
and locked in sub-level holding cubes pending delivery to an 
iso-block where they will serve their sentence. 
The higher ﬂ  oors of the Sector House are reserved for 
Judges alone, containing brie ﬁ ng rooms, research areas, 
ofﬁ ces and, sometimes, dormitories that Judges can use to 
catch some much needed rest. The brieﬁ ng rooms ﬁ ll several 
times a day with crime reports and updates on the state of the 
sector while a few levels above are the of ﬁ ces of the Watch 
Commanders and Sector Chief, who is the de-facto Chief 
Judge of the entire sector. The Sector Chief receives regular 
– sometimes hourly – reports on the crime levels within the 
sector and, although the organisation of the Judges is left 
to the Watch Commanders, he or she sometimes orders 
for patrols to be quickly restructured in response to or 
anticipation of, local circumstances. The support staff of the 
Sector House are every bit as ef ﬁ cient and busy as their 
colleagues out on the streets: the information is constant – 
twenty four hours a day, seven days a week, never pausing, 
rarely ebbing.
Street Judge Routines
At the start of each watch, Street Judges head to the armoury 
to restock their weapons and equipment before attending the 
essential brieﬁ ng session for their watch. The brieﬁ ng session 
is a mixture of verbal address by the Watch Commander 
and direct information feed via the Justice Department’s 
advanced brain-interfaces. Verbal brie ﬁ ngs run through the 
highest priority items, allowing the Watch Commanders to 
take questions and stress matters of particular importance 
or urgency. The direct info-feeds are concentrated bursts of 
information concerning low-level matters, crime statistics, 
incident updates and other information pertinent to the 
sector. This is the conveyance of pure facts that do not 
require questioning but which each and every Judge needs 
to know in preparation for his watch.
Once the brieﬁ ng round (taking no more than ten minutes) is 
complete, Judges go straight to their assignments, details of 
 Sleep Machine
Sleep Machines were developed in the 
early years of Chief Judge Fargo’s tenure. 
It was readily apparent that dedicated Judges 
were skimping on rest and, even when able 
to take some rest, found it hard to wind-down 
from the day’s exertions. The Sleep Machine 
was therefore developed to cope with the 
kind of exhaustion many Judges experienced 
simply through dedication to upholding the 
law. Electromagnetic waves manipulate the 
brain’s natural activity patterns to introduce 
the equivalent of natural deep sleep, whilst 
other devices work on the physical body to 
replicate the relaxation experienced whilst 
sleeping naturally. Ten minutes in the Sleep 
Machine equates to eight hours rest but it is 
never recommended that the Sleep Machine 
be used for prolonged periods of time. Natural 
sleep is still essential: to maintain physical, 
mental and emotional health. If any Judge uses 
a Sleep Machine more than ﬁ ve days in any 
seven day period, he must roll End 8+, with an 
additional DM penalty for each consecutive use 
of the Sleep Machine (so, for example, using 
the machine seven days in a row would result 
in having to roll End 8+ with a –2 DM). If the roll 
is failed, the Judge suffers a –1 reduction in 
two, randomly chosen, characteristics. This 
characteristic reduction is regained once 
the Judge has spent a night getting 
proper, natural rest. 
which are communicated by the direct info-feed. If a Judge 
is being assigned to a speci ﬁ c case, he usually receives an 
additional verbal brie ﬁ ng by the Watch Commander and 
this includes those Judges (if any) who have already been 
involved with the case.
Off-duty Judges can grab food in the staff canteen, practice 
their shooting skills on the ﬁ ring range, enjoy valuable 
time off in the rec-decks or catch up on their sleep in the 
dormitories. If a major emergency beckons, ten minutes in 
a Total Relaxation Inducer (or Sleep Machine) will serve 
almost as well as a good night’s
 rest.
