Killdozer
The Killdozer forms the rear assembly of the combined Land Raider and, on its own, is a heavily armoured, tank-like weapons 
platform designed for all terrain conditions and sustained battleﬁ eld deployment. Its tracks are set in an asymmetrical pyramid 
conﬁ guration allowing for a small footprint and high manoeuvrability but with the same rough terrain performance as larger, 
lower-framed tanks. The battle unit has enough room for stabling four Lawmasters (or equivalent-sized bikes) with a rear-facing, 
armoured, loading/unloading ramp for rapid vehicle deployment. The interior cabin has a conning-tower command bridge with 
excellent visibility via 5cm thick armour-glass. The whole unit is EMP, radiation and chemically shielded, with an onboard life  
support system with a 4 week reserve, including air and water recycling facilities and preserved food storage.
In terms of weaponry, the Killdozer carries six linked rapid- ﬁ re 20mm cannon (equivalent to bike cannon), two pop-up turret 
mounted heavy lasers and, mounted on the roof of the conning tower, a pair of Mauley missile launchers. The missile battery is 
typically 50 x Hi-Ex missiles and 20 x Homing missiles. The launchers can be operated in linked or unlinked modes.
The Killdozer’s computer system allows it to act in full automatic in the same manner as a Lawmaster bike, taking commands 
by remote verbal instruction or from pre-programmed routines.

 Spaceships
Detailed discussion of spaceships is beyond the scope of the Judge Dredd main rules but will no doubt be covered in future 
supplements. However, as is clear from the Dredd saga, space ﬂ ight is not only commonplace, the Earth has established 
colonies throughout the galaxy and come into contact with numerous alien races.
Spaceships conform to the rules provided in both the Traveller Core Rulebook and High Guard. Faster than light travel 
is achieved through hyperdrive engines that equate to the standard, Traveller Jump Drive, with Manoeuvre drives being used 
for in-system transit (such as around the solar system). Justice Department maintains its own ﬂ eet of shuttles – notably for 
transporting prisoners to Titan – and hyperdrive equipped ships such as Justice One, used by Dredd during the quest for the 
Judge Child.
Corporate and privately owned spaceships are licensed via the Justice Department’s Space Licensing Unit, a part of Tek 
Division, which registers ship use, destinations, manifests and so forth. This enables Justice Department to trace any off-world 
cargoes that may pose a threat back to their source of entry.
Mega-City One operates its own starport: The Eustace Fargo Spaceport – a grade A facility – out of Sector 40; the part of old 
New York once occupied by the John F Kennedy terrestrial airport.

===PAGE 153===
 Mega-City One
152
A Mile High 
View
Mega-City One spans the eastern seaboard of the United 
States of America, ranging from Maine in the north right 
down to North Carolina in the south and across to the west 
as far as Atlanta, Dayton, Cleveland and up to Hartford: 
an area of around 125,000 square kilometres. The entire 
area of Mega-City One is urbanised: what little agricultural 
land that exists is contained within the massive Agridomes 
that provide year-round, fully-automated crop production 
of basic vegetable proteins that go into the staple food of 
the city; munce. Otherwise, that entire stretch of eastern 
North America is a seemingly endless urban sprawl, the 
result of over-zealous metropolitan planning to deal with 
the burgeoning population of the early 21
st Century and 
the development of new building materials and techniques 
allowing structures to go higher, more safely, more cheaply 
and in greater concentrations than ever before. Mega-City 
One spreads across and down the North American coastline 
like a virus, its nucleus being the Washington/New York/
Boston axis, and its only limit being the sensibility of the 
architects who realised that Mega-City One had to have a 
plan and had to have an end somewhere.
The notion was to offer security and prosperity to citizens, 
doing away with the underclass that had always troubled 
American society but at the same time giving everyone the 
kind of lifestyle that had been promised since the cheap, pulp 
science ﬁ ction novels of the mid-20
th Century: maximum free 
time; no need to work (unless you wanted to); obedient robots 
to handle the drudge tasks; hyper-fast, intelligent transport 
networks; a home, with all mod-cons for every citizen; free 
energy derived from all manner of sources (vast, solar-panel 
arrays, deep-drilled sinks below the Earth’s crust to tap into 
the limitless energy reserves of the mantle); fully interactive 
entertainments delivered through the internet, including 
tridee participatory broadcasts; and placing all citizens, 
irrespective of colour, creed or class, on an equal footing. 
Mega-City One was America’s last, unchallenged version of 
the American Dream: it had to work; people would make it 
work. The citizens of the Mega Cities would be prosperous, 
free to enjoy life and on an equal footing.
So Mega-City One evolved to provide all these things. As 
disparate cities merged and land at ground level became 
scarce, the developers just went up, creating buildings that 
would stretch up to a mile high. The city’s various sectors, 
centred on the old city boundaries, gained more and more 
levels to support the burgeoning starscrapers and city 
blocks. Man’s ambitions were lofty and the buildings loftier 
still. Perspective was lost as the physical perspective 
rocketed towards the heavens. Super-strong alloys like 
plasteen and plastisteel made the safest structures ever 
known. Numerous levels made layered cities, like the strata 
of a rock, so that a population far larger than a traditional 
city of the same area could be accommodated. And, for a 
while, the experiment worked. People ﬂ ocked into the Mega-
City, taking advantage of free homes and exceedingly cheap 
food and free energy, desperate to take advantage of the 
Lifestyle of the Future. The city ﬂ oor fell away from them; 
their horizons were dominated by the heights of the city 
blocks and the spaghetti-like morass of megways, skedways 
and inter-zooms. The future seemed boundless; the people 
were happy. Mostly. An abundance of leisure time and the 
service of obedient, uncomplaining robots, meant people 
could indulge themselves on a scale that had never been 
possible.
The Mega-City One experiment was copied everywhere. 
Mega-City Two developed along the west coast and along 
similar lines, the city absorbing every urban centre between 
Seattle and San Diego. Texas City sprang up around 
Houston and soon encompassed the whole of the state of 
Texas and parts of Arkansas. In Russia, the East Meg cities 
developed around Moscow and St Petersburg. In the United 
Kingdom, Brit-Cit saw London swarm across the south coast 
and pulse upwards to the midland cities of Birmingham, 
Leicester and across the Norwich. In Australia, Oz repeated 
the trend along the vastness of the east coat, taking in 
Sidney, Melbourne and up to Cairns. Every nation had to 
have a Mega-City. Sometimes two. All of them modelled 
themselves on Mega-City One but none of them equalled it; 
not in terms of ambition, grandeur or, sadly, crime.
It was inevitable that the American Dream would turn into 
a nightmare, because America had never had a good track 
record where dreams were concerned. The incredible 
complexities of administrating Mega-City One meant that 
traditional policing rapidly became inefﬁ cient. With a population, 
at its height, of 800 million, governing the city was beyond 
even the capabilities of the politicians who had been Mega-
City One’s architects. Much could be automated, of course; 
and much was. But what cannot be automated and controlled 
is the human propensity for boredom and sel ﬁ shness. The 
citizens of Mega-City One had everything they wanted but, 
as is always the case, people wanted more. When there are 
two few police and when those police are virtually invisible, 
selﬁ shness sets-in and people forget about the rules that 
society needs to function properly. When the judicial system 
grinds to a virtual halt because it has failed to keep pace with 
the rapid progress of a city’s expansion, people can, and do, 
get away with murder. Create any utopia and humans will be 
guaranteed to turn it into a dystopia. The American Dream 
turned sour and then went rotten. It was inevitable. Progress 
is always pursued by the elements of decay.

===PAGE 154===
 Mega-City One
153
Crime, then, became rampant. The city became very, very 
full. The Immigration Laws of 2055 aimed to curb the swarms 
of people who wanted to get into an over-full metropolis but 
merely succeeded in causing mass civil unrest. Deep in the 
heart of Mega-City One, buried in the layers of metro-levels, 
segregated by the skedways and overzooms, organised 
crime – something almost stamped-out in the early 21
st 
Century – spewed its way back into life and set about 
corrupting those who could be corrupted, far from the sight 
of traditional justice. Mega-City One’s dark places became 
darker. The higher reaches became the goal of those who 
had plenty of money and they ensured that they insulated 
themselves from the damp-rot seeping up through the 
plasteen of the city blocks. That dream of all citizens living in 
equality evaporated like tears dropped into a blast furnace: 
segregation and class distinctions asserted themselves just 
as they always would – because humans are, inherently, 
selﬁ sh brute-beasts who revert to a natural state of war at 
the ﬁ rst opportunity. As an experiment in architectural and 
technological magniﬁ cence, Mega-City One was a triumph. 

===PAGE 155===
 Mega-City One
154
As an experiment in social harmonisation, it was an abject 
failure. As one wit remarked, ‘If that guy Darwin was still 
around, he’d be sayin’, “Gee. Like, I told you so!”’
 Mega-City One 
Essentials
Mega-City One is organised into 305 judicial areas called 
sectors. Each sector follows, very roughly, old borders and 
district divisions from the pre-Atomic War United States. 
The States divisions are gone, although the legacy of the 
cultures of those states still live on through quaint traditions 
and practices – such as the urbane sophistication of the old 
New England sectors in the north.
Every sector is a self-contained administrative unit under 
the effective legal control of the Justice Department Sector 
House. 
MegCentral (Sectors 1-20)
Population: 30 million.
Famous Landmarks: Mega-City Chamber of Horrors, Gus 
Grissom Spaceport.
The geographical centre of Mega-City One where most of 
the larger space and hoverports are located.
MegEast (Sectors 21-108)
Population: 100 million.
Famous Landmarks: Academy of Law, Alien Penitentiary, 
Alien Zoo, Central Mega-City Library, City Hall, Costa Del 
Meg, Empire State Building, Grand Hall of Justice, Mega-City 
Museum, Museum of Death, Ocean Sprawl, Psi-Division HQ, 
Sinotown, Statue of Judgement, Statue of Liberty, Weather 
Congress, the White Cliffs of Dover.
MegEast has the highest crime rates in the city, for major 
smuggling rings work the docks along the East Wall. Man-
made canals assist sky truks in conveying goods from the 
merchant hoverships anchored outside.
MegSouth (Sectors 109-160)
Population: 85 million.
Famous Landmarks: Amalgamated Munce, Meg-Oil, Little 
Cuba, Nukco, Plastoid Industries, Power Tower, Roadliners 
Inc, Sunshine Synthifoods.
The centremost sectors are given over to a massive industrial 
zone called the Dust Belt. All structures within the belt draw 
their energy from Power Tower and subsidiary stations. A 
safety ‘dead zone’ separates the factories from city blocks 
sandwiched between the Dust Belt and the South Wall
.
MegWest (Sectors 161-240)
Population: 75 million.
Famous Landmarks: Apetown, The Jungle, Mega-City Vibe 
Bowl, Moonray Tower.
Meg-West borders the Cursed Earth and is vulnerable to 
mutant break-ins and alien contagion.
MegNorth (Sectors 241-300)
Population: 95 million.
Famous Landmarks: Big-1 Publishing, MegaMedia Inc, 
NERDCO.
Home to more wealthy citizens and corporate headquarters 
than the rest of the city combined.
Meg-West Hab Zone (Sectors 
301-305)
Population: 15 million.
The only section of Mega-City One beyond the City Wall, the 
North-West Hab Zone suffers the city’s second highest crime 
rates. The megway aptly named Hell’s Highway is the only 
physical link between the Hab and its parent city, spanning 
the radioactive wasteland of Nuke Alley.
Sector Secretariats
Every sector is administered locally through a robotic 
bureaucracy that is controlled by the sector Secretariat. The 
Secretariat is effectively beholden to the Justice Department 
but it takes charge of general, civil affairs, whilst the Justice 
Department, through the Sector Houses, looks after law and 
order. The Secretariat ensures highways are maintained 
(again, through robot repair crews), schools supplied, hospitals 
provided and so forth. The Justice Department, via the Sector 
House, can inﬂ uence and override the Secretariat at any point 
and without any warning; and it frequently does. When Block 
War breaks out, political niceties need to take second or third 
place to the Rule of Law. The Secretariats then, have little 
more than a basic (though extensive) administrative function 
in each sector. The competence of the Secretariat contributes 
much to the sector’s character: lax Secretariats inevitably mean 
deprivation in some sectors, leading to higher crime rates and 
more work for the Judges. Even though the Secretariats are 
largely robotised, humans still govern the upper echelons and, 
because the humans of the 22
nd Century are perhaps far more 
ﬂ awed than in earlier centuries, levels of competence, public 
duty and civic pride vary wildly.

===PAGE 156===
 Mega-City One
155
 Mayor of Mega-City One
A ceremonial role, the Of ﬁ ce of the Mayor was dissolved 
during Judge Cal’s rule but reinstated later under Chief 
Judge Volt’s reforms. The mayor is considered to be the 
First Citizen of the city, elected by the people, for the people, 
but in reality he or she is little more than a ﬁ gurehead. Civic 
power rests with the Secretariats and all truly important 
political decisions are taken by the Justice Department 
through the Council of Five and Chief Judge. The mayor, 
supported by his own robot bureaucracy, rubber-stamps 
wide-scale city projects, looks at accounts from the Sector 
Secretariats and occasionally gets involved in key decisions 
regarding the true politics of the city. But, in reality, there are 
few citizens who have anything like the force of personality 
and political will necessary to stand-up to and challenge 
the Justice Department. Life is much easier whenever the 
mayor sticks to his position and keeps his nose out of the 
important things, such as how best to run the city. The last 
time politicians were left to run anything, atomic war broke 
out. The people might have forgotten that but the Justice 
Department certainly has not.
Economy and Income
With robots performing 97% of the revenue-generating 
activities of Mega-City One, the economy of such a huge city 
is actually highly ef ﬁ cient. Production is 24/7 with little time 
lost to illness or industrial dispute. Things get made, grown 
and exported. Mega-City One is the foremost producer in the 
world with exports going to the other mega cities across the 
globe. As a result, its revenues are high and steady – even 
following the tragedy of the Apocalypse War.
Mass automation means that citizens do not need to work. 
A burgeoning economy means that just about every citizen 
is on welfare. Every citizen receives a regular, monthly City 
Payment (known as the Mega Dole) from which to pay for 
rent, food, lifestyle and so forth. There is clearly little income 
tax but citizens are taxed on goods they buy and services 
they use, with 70% of the tax going into the Secretariat 
coffers and 30% going to the Mayor’s Exchequer. Those 
citizens who want a job and can ﬁ nd one (or make one), 
pay a ﬂ at 10% Job Tax, although the astute and wealthy can 
afford to easily ﬁ nd ways of avoiding Job Tax altogether.
Citizens can gain bonuses to their Mega Dole by undertaking 
certain civic duties, such as joining Citi-Def or taking part in 
local voluntary schemes but otherwise everyone receives 
the same, ﬂ at, Cr. 3,000 every month. If Mega-City One’s 
combined economic output is especially healthy in a particular 
quarter, then a further bonus is paid to citizens, based on the 
proﬁ t balance. This is one of the mayor’s duties: signing off 

===PAGE 157===
 Mega-City One
156
and belittled you, copied your block’s ideas and besmirched 
your name. Grab your spitguns and raid the Citi-Def armoury 
because, boy, those neighbours have got it coming.
Block War happens for the slightest reasons or none at 
all. Simply put, every able-bodied man, woman and child 
grabs a weapon, leans out of their window and unleashes 
it against any visible target in a neighbouring block. Citi-Def 
units, found in every City Block, are there to protect Mega-
City One as a civic defence force; but most commonly they 
fuel Block Wars, levelling entire storeys of enemy blocks 
with missiles, rapid- ﬁ re hi-ex rounds and generally turning 
from Citi-Def into Citi-Death. Once Block War is declared, 
mass hysteria takes over and just about everyone gets 
swept into the con ﬂ ict. Any weapon will do and age is no 
barrier. Schools take on schools; hospitals empty to go 
and join the carnage. Some citizens are professional Block 
Agitators, stockpiling weapons in readiness for the next war 
and cranking-up the rumour-mill to get a war started ASAP. 
These small minorities face life in the cubes when caught but 
that adrenaline rush felt when the cry of ‘Block War! Get the 
Drokkers!’ goes up, makes the risk worth it.
Every City Block has a weekly People’s Assembly, open to 
all citizens, where the agenda is to discuss life within one’s 
own City Block. This rarely happens. The assemblies are 
generally less concerned with local welfare than criticising 
neighbouring blocks and adding bile- ﬂ avoured grist to the 
rumour-mill. Block Agitators seek-out speaking positions 
at the assemblies in order to foment unrest and so Judges 
usually keep an eye on especially notorious and troublesome 
block assemblies to both prevent agitators and identify 
them. The Wally Squad has special Block Squads who go 
deep undercover to penetrate agitation rings and break 
them, before they break the blocks. But, truth be told, the 
Judges’ best efforts (and they really are rather good) make 
only a small dent in the propensity for Block War. Citizens 
have precious else to liven-up their dull days and, Judges 
have noted, petty crime in the wake of a Block War drops 
signiﬁ cantly for a sustained period (as does the population 
count, naturally).
City Block Organisation
City Blocks range in size greatly. Any City Block has a 
population of 1d6+1x10,000 citizens but there are more 
populous blocks and wealthy, less populous ones. Every 
block is built to a standard design: the lowest ten storeys 
are given over to public amenities (shops, food-joints, bars 
and the like); the middle ten to sixty storeys are apartments, 
ofﬁ ces and so forth; and the upper ten or so storeys house 
the block’s services: water, power generators, climate control 
and so forth. The very roof of the block houses the Citi-Def 
armoury and watch posts, so that, in the event of an attack, 
the bonus each quarter – although the Justice Department 
actually decides how much it will be and carefully makes 
sure that when a bonus is warranted it is neither too low nor 
too high. Even when times are lean, the mayor often signs-
off the bonus anyway, on Justice Department advice: nasty 
riots tend to follow whenever a bonus has been withheld 
and so the Justice Department ensures that a bonus fund 
is available to ensure a quarterly bonus can be paid, even 
when the economic performance for the quarter would 
otherwise warrant a low bonus or none at all.
The quarterly bonus paid as part of the Mega Dole is 2d6+3% 
of the Mega Dole’s ﬂ at rate. So, an average, a citizen can 
expect an additional Cr. 300 per quarter or as much as Cr. 
450. When times are lean, it may be as low as Cr. 150 but 
only in the direst circumstances is it nothing at all.
City Blocks
The vast bulk of citizens live in the towering City Blocks; self-
contained, high-rise communities of, on average, 50,000 
people. City Blocks contain all necessary amenities: shops, 
food-joints and restaurants, vid-theatres and concert halls, 
gyms and sport-domes, aggrodomes (for loosing-off pent-
up aggression, although aggrodomes are strictly licensed by 
the Justice Department following a series of large-scale riots 
– the result of uncontrolled, pent-up aggression) and so forth. 
Many citizens never, ever, venture out of their City Block, 
ﬁ nding everything they need within its plastisteel and synthi-
rock walls. Life within a City Block is relatively safe (in other 
words, the chances of being mugged, robbed, assaulted 
or abused are slightly less than on the open streets) but 
stiﬂ ingly dull. As most citizens have no jobs, minds roll idle. 
Ghastly vid-shows offer train-crash entertainment, regularly 
peddling new crazes and shock-horror stories for no better 
reason than public titillation. Apartments are cramped; 
and it is impossible to ﬁ nd peace and quiet in a City Block. 
Everything is open to public scrutiny and, if the rumour-mill 
starts, even for the most innocuous behaviour, it can rapidly 
escalate into a witch-hunt. Judges have investigated many 
murders and assaults caused by nothing more than a false 
rumour triggering a mistaken belief that an innocent citizen 
has committed some heinous crime. City Blocks create a 
mob-mentality. Individuality is drained-out and replaced with 
an inane allegiance to the block’s communal identity. Every 
neighbouring block has it easier, richer, bigger, better and 
freer than one’s own. Petty jealousy is fuelled on a daily 
basis. Linda Evans Block starts a new fashion trend? Joan 
Collins Block asserts that it started the same trend ﬁ rst (even 
though it did not). The result is Block War.
 Block War
Sometimes there is simply no alternative but to blast the grud 
out of those no-good neighbours. They have taunted you

 Mega-City One
157
Citi-Def can retaliate against aerial attack. Unfortunately, 
the Citi-Def emplacements are equally suited to retaliating 
against other City Blocks, as already noted.
The residential storeys of the City Block are named from 
Z to A and then AZ through to AA the higher one gets up 
the block. Higher is generally better. Premium apartments 
are found in the AZ to AA range and the ‘Double Letter’ 
citizens like to look down, physically and culturally, on the 
‘Singletons’.
Apartments conform to a standard size. A to Z apartments 
are 66m
2 with two bedrooms, kitchenette, bathroom and 
living room. AA to AZ apartments are 122m 2 with a variety 
of conﬁ gurations (up to four bedrooms, bigger lounges and 
kitchens, luxury bathrooms and so on). Every apartment 
comes equipped with a kitchen unit, cooler unit, water 
puriﬁ er and an integrated ﬂ at-screen vid-display to access 
the City Block’s local vid channels and those broadcast city 
wide. On average each residential ﬂ oor has up to a hundred 
apartments and ﬂ oor pride is every bit in evidence as block 
pride. Inter-storey wars are not uncommon, with rivalries 
fuelled by snobbishness spilling over into bloodshed. Floor 
rivalries are usually only supplanted by block rivalries and, 
when the Block War cry goes up, inter-storey enemies 
suddenly become inter-storey allies as they take aim at the 
hated neighbours across the plaza.
Beneath the public amenities storeys are storage areas, 
vehicle parks and maintenance rooms. The basements 
of City Blocks are warrens of dark passages, labyrinthine 
tunnels and narrow, dangerous walkways. Only maintenance 
robots and the criminal underclass descend into the bowels 
of a city block; those and a smattering of refugees and 
mutants who want to keep well away from the streets so they 
remain in the city unmolested. Entire, forgotten communities 
of muties, trogs, perps and other wretches inhabit the City 
Block underbellies, meaning that the true population of 
a City Block can be larger than the known, upper storey 
inhabitants.
Block Assemblies
Every block has a citizen assembly. The idea is to have an 
open forum where every one of a block’s residents has a say 
in how the block is run. In reality, things are nowhere close to 
this lofty ideal. Those who desire power and shout the loudest 
dominate proceedings; most citizens are too apathetic to get 
involved with anything as boring as block administration and 
politics. Thus, most block assemblies end up being dominated 
by a handful of petty powerbrokers who enjoy lording it over 
their silo-like domains or stirring-up trouble as an antidote to 
the tediousness of Mega-City One life.
Essentially, block names are quirky, 
reﬂ ect social whimsy and have no real 
basis in logic. When naming blocks for Judge 
Dredd campaigns, look to what is popular in 
politics, entertainment and sport currently. Some 
example block names that might appeal are:
• Amy Winehouse Rejuve Conapts
• Ant and Dec Towers
• Georgie Brown Block
• Gokky Wan Underblock
• Jonny Woss Block
• Katey Winslet Block
• Leo DiCaprio Block
• Nick Cotton Sub-Slum Block
• Phil Tufnell Lux Apartments
... and so on...
Some block assemblies are open forums with no elected 
representatives. Others elect members, rather like the old 
parliaments of the pre-Atomic War era were elected. In others 
still, representatives are self-appointed, seizing control and 
becoming petty tyrants or dictators, ruling through sheer 
force of presence, charm, intimidation or bullying. This is 
particularly prevalent in those sectors where the mob still has 
a signiﬁ cant inﬂ uence. The maﬁ a gangs either control a city 
block outright or control the people who run the assembly. 
Either way, block assemblies rarely do much to represent 
the welfare of the block and those living in it. Assemblies are 
generally shouting shops and hotbeds of hatred directed at 
neighbouring blocks. The actual things that matter within a 
block – like the standards of living conditions, maintenance 
and repairs and so forth, are usually ignored until they 
reach a point when it is usually too late. Block assemblies 
look to Judges to maintain law and order; Citi-Def units to 
protect them (or blast the grud out of neighbours) and the 
Sector Secretariat to anticipate things like maintenance and 
repair. Negligence, petty corruption, naked self-interest and 
personal agendas are rife. Common sense and civic duty 
are commodities in very short supply.
Block Names
The fashion is for city blocks to be named after celebrities, 
sports stars, politicians and public ﬁ gures from the pre-Atomic 
War era. For some strange reason the fashion seemed to

slightly-better-off citizens. A typical conapt is much larger 
and better furnished than those in other blocks and may 
even occupy an entire level of their own. Just like city blocks, 
conapts are given popular names for easy identi ﬁ cation. A 
conapt is typically no higher than ﬁ fty levels, although some

City blockers are known to harbour resentment and even
