 Thought Crimes
203
No rolls are required to remove the condition but expertise in 
Medic and Life Sciences (psychology) reduces the number of 
months required for treatment by their skill levels. Treatment 
must be undergone for the full term; if it is broken, then the 
sufferer is not cured and suffers a relapse to the condition’s 
previous intensity. At the end of the term, the character 
must roll Int+End 9+. If successful, the condition has been 
successfully cured. If unsuccessful, then the character is 
cured but requires drugs to keep the condition fully at bay. 
If drugs are not taken, there is an End 9+ chance that the 
condition will return with a new intensity (but reduced by the 
character’s End and Int DMs).
 Insanity
Insanity is a mental instability so deep-rooted that the 
character is completely unable to function in society and 
poses a constant danger to himself and others.
Any character that has a mental instability condition that 
goes untreated sees that condition increase in intensity by 1 
for every year it is left untreated. Once the intensity reaches 
12 or higher, the character is clinically insane. He loses all 
ability to control his condition and he effectively withdraws 
from society – and may become suicidal or homicidal. The 
only safe way to treat an insane person is to con ﬁ ne them 
and subject them to intensive, long-term treatment. The 
treatment period is half the intensity level of the condition 
+2d6 in years. Some insane characters, depending on age, 
may never be cured.
Player Characters in a truly insane condition may be retired 
from play at the Referee’s discretion – although insane 
characters do provide some interesting opportunities for 
roleplaying (and insane Non-Player Characters always 
do).
 Psionic 
Equipment
All psionic equipment is licensed by the Justice Department. 
Naturally, possession of unlicensed psionic equipment 
results in a spell (5 years and upwards) in the iso-cubes. 
The equipment listed here is the most common range of 
items that are available to Psi Division from its stores and 
through co-operation with the Tek and Med Divisions; but 
also on the black market through the Mob and unscrupulous 
Psyk-Skools. The cost in Credits is the suggested cost for 
someone attempting to gain a piece of equipment on the 
black market: Referees may double, triple or even quadruple 
these costs if they feel it is appropriate.
Anti-Psi Spray
An aerosol neutraliser, the Ant-Psi Spray projects a cloud of 
nano-particles, 1 metre in diameter, into the air. The nano 
particles are molecularly calibrated to absorb psionic energy 
and dissipate it harmlessly. Every blast of the spray neutralises 
3+1d6 Psionic Strength points placed into an active Power 
that must be directed through the cloud’s area of effect. The 
cloud itself dissipates after 1+1d6 Combat Rounds.
A standard canister of spray provides 5 bursts.
Anti-Psi Spray Cost: Cr. 5,000 per canister.
Aura Array
This headset device can be used by non-psions and replicates 
the Read Aura Power of the Aura Perception Talent (see 
page 185) at level 0. The user can see and interpret psionic 
auras but cannot manipulate, mask or project them.
Aura Array Cost: Cr. 10,000.
Disruption Unit
This small unit emits a constant electro-psionic ﬁ eld that 
disrupts psionics within its sphere of in ﬂ uence. The unit 
carries a 100-point charge, with its operation drawing on this 
charge at the rate of 1 point per minute. The maximum setting 
is variable between 1 and 6 points, with each point applying 
a negative DM to all psionic use equal to the points setting. 
The disruption unit affects an area of 3 metres radius. The 
unit can be recharged from a standard power source, taking 
1 minute per point to recharge.
Disruption Unit Cost: Cr. 20,000.
Image Sliver
Image Slivers are psionically calibrated data wafers that 
can record and store psionically obtained data: thoughts, 
emotions, auras, memories, nightmares and so forth. The 
sliver is a blue-black data wafer 5mm x 5mm in length and 
width. It can store up to 10 hours of recorded thoughts, 
memories or emotions or the aura signatures of 10 people. 
Non-psions can access the material recorded on the sliver 
by inserting it into a standard neural interface jack if they are 
suitably augmented; psions can access the information in a 
similar way or, if they have the Telepathy or Clairvoyance 
Talents, simply by holding the sliver and spending a single 
Psi Point whilst concentrating on the sliver.
Non-psions who access a sliver ﬁ nd the information 
strange, random and often disturbing. The senses are 

===PAGE 205===
 Thought Crimes
204
ﬂ ooded with the recorded data and the feedback taps into 
the emotional areas of the brain to transfer the recording. 
The imagery found even in the most placidly recorded 
thoughts can introduce sensory overload and so End 9+ 
rolls are required otherwise the character suffers a –1 DM 
to all skills for a period of minutes equal to the length of 
the recording as they seek to adjust to the information they 
have downloaded. Memories, emotions and other data 
may also resurface at random intervals even after the sliver 
has been removed for 1d6 days after being accessed, with 
effects that need to be adjudicated by the Referee. Psions 
are attuned to the way mental data is recorded and do not 
suffer from these effects.
Image Sliver Cost: Cr. 250.
Inhibitor Drug
Psionic inhibitors dampen the brain’s ability to generate 
psychic effects. A character who takes (or, more often, is 
forcibly injected with) an inhibitor drug suffers a –4 DM to all 
Psionic Strength checks and cannot regain Psionic Strength 
points. Each hour the character may make an Endurance 
check to throw off the effects of the drug with a +1 DM for 
every previous check. Inhibitor drugs have no effect on non-
psionic individuals. 
Inhibitor Drug Cost: Cr. 5,000 per dose.
Mind Reader
A device for non-psions, a Mind Reader is a headset device 
that is worn by the user and linked to electrodes that are 

===PAGE 206===
 Thought Crimes
205
by the psion during rest periods (Psi Points being channelled 
into the battery rather than into the psion’s own mind, which 
will therefore increase the length of any rest period if the 
psion wants to regain his full Psi Points compliment).
A psion can draw on up to 3 points from the battery at a 
time. If not used, stored Psi Points degrade and are lost. 
Degradation begins after 3 days with 1 point being lost per 
day until the battery is empty.
PPB Cost: Cr. 30,000.
Psi Detector
For use by non-psionics, the psi detector is a hand-held 
device that detects residual or active psi energy within a 
radius of 500 metres. Where active psi energy is detected 
(in other words, Psionic Powers are in use), the detector can 
provide an indication of the amount of psi energy involved; 
the indication is low, medium and high, relating to the number 
of psi points invested in the particular Power under use.
For residual energy, the reading will always be low but 
retrograde analysis on rates of decay can offer a general 
indication of how long ago a Power was used, with an 
accuracy of up to 3 hours.
Psi Detector Cost: Cr. 12,000.
Psi-Drugs
These drugs restore Psionic Strength if taken when the 
character has already spent Psionic Strength points or 
temporarily increase the character’s Psionic Strength if 
taken when he is at full Psionic Strength. 
A character who takes more than one dose of Standard or 
Double Psi-Drug or a single dose of the Special drug must 
make an Endurance check, with a –1 DM per dose of psi-
drug taken in the last week (not including the one just taken). 
If the check is failed the character falls ill with a serious fever, 
suffering 3d6 damage and permanently reducing his Psionic 
Strength by 1.
connected to the temples or skull of 
the subject. The device provides the 
user with temporary psionic abilities, 
allowing him to read thoughts and 
emotions as though a psion with a 
skill level of 0 in Telepathy.
Mind Reader Cost: Cr. 50,000.
Mind Switch 
Device
A device that it is rumoured Psi 
Division possesses – but never 
proven. This device exchanges 
the minds of two subjects, in 
the same way as the Tapping 
Talent of Mind Switch (see page 
193). For the mind switch to be 
successful, the operator must roll 
Electronics or Computers 8+ to 
operate the device, with the Int 
DM of any unwilling participants 
acting as a negative DM for the 
skill roll.
The mind switch has a safe limit 
of a number of hours equal to the 
Effect of the machine operation 
roll, multiplied by the result of a 2d6 
throw. If minds are not swapped 
back at the end of this time, both 
subjects begin to accrue 1 level of 
Psionic Trauma for each 6 hour period 
thereafter.
Mind Switch Device Cost: Cr. 250,000 
(limited availability).
Personal Psi Battery (PPB)
A Personal Psi Battery stores Psionic Strength points for later 
use. A battery can hold up to 10 Psi Points and is charged

206
Psi Enhancer
Available either as a skull implant (in which the enhancer 
circuits are etched directly into the skull) or as an external, 
battery-powered headset-like array, the Psi Enhancer 
improves the Psionic Strength DM – but not the Psi Points 
– by +1 for every level of complexity. Psi Enhancers are 
necessarily expensive; especially for the implant option.
An implanted enhancer requires a surgical procedure as the 
circuitry needs to be physically wired to the brain. Psions 
undergoing the operation must throw Endurance 8+ (with DMs 
for the levels of medical expertise performing the operation) to 
avoid complications. If the roll is failed, check the effect on the 
table below to determine the nature of the complications:
Effect Complication
 4 Brain Damage: lose 1d6+1 Int. If Int is reduced to 
0, patient is left in a persistent vegetative state.
 3 Brain Damage: lose 1d3 Int.
 2 2 Levels of Psionic Trauma.
 1 1 Level of Psionic Trauma.
External Psi Enhancer Cost: Cr. 50,000 per level. 
Implanted Psi Enhancer Cost: Cr. 80,000 per level, plus 
hospital and surgery costs.
Psi Scanner
This device scans the brain to determine psionic potential. The 
device connects to the skull of the subject via electrodes and 
conducts a deep, highly intense, highly accurate EEG scan 
speciﬁ cally focused on detecting latent Psionic Strength.
If the subject has untrained psionic capability, the scanner 
detects it in a roll of 8+, with DMs for Medic or other, 
appropriate science skills.
Psi Scanner Cost: Cr. 10,000.
Psionic Shield
Any armour incorporating a helmet or hood can be outﬁ tted with 
a psionic shield, which blocks Telepathy. Unlike the Telepathy 
Power, Shield, a technological shield is invulnerable to assault 
and blocks Send Thoughts. It cannot be lowered without 
removing the helmet or hood containing the shield. Buildings 
and vehicles can also be psionically shielded but this is much 
more costly, increasing the cost by 10%. 
Psionic Shield Cost: Cr. 40,000.
Talent Chip
A speciﬁ c Psionic Talent is encoded onto a data wafer that 
is accessed via either a Psi Enhancer or a standard neural 
interface. Note that a Talent Chip will not confer a Psionic 
Talent on someone who is none psionic.
The chip gives the psion the Talent at level 0. The cost is 
based on how many Powers are included as part of the 
chip’s data array.
Talent Chip Cost: Basic Talent, Cr. 20,000 + Cr. 2,000 
per Power; Advanced Talent, Cr. 50,000 + Cr. 3,000 per 
Power.
Trauma Grenade
A speciﬁ cally anti-psion device, this psychoactive grenade, 
when it explodes, induces Psionic Trauma in any psions 
caught in its blast radius of 3+1d6 metres. Psions must throw 
End+Psi 10+ to withstand the unleashing of the trauma-
inducing energy or suffer 1+1d3 levels of Psionic Trauma.
Trauma Grenade Cost: Cr. 3,000
Trauma Suppressant
These drugs are designed to suppress Psionic Trauma. The 
drugs come in six strengths, corresponding to the levels of 
Psionic Trauma. One level of strength suppresses one level 
of trauma; thus, to suppress two levels of Psionic Trauma, a 
Level 2 Suppressant needs to be taken.
The drug does not cure Psionic Trauma; it merely controls 
its effects allowing a psion to live a normal, everyday life. 
However, the body and mind can become inured to the 
drug’s effects; for every month, roll End+Psi 9+. If the roll 
succeeds then the body begins to resist the drug’s effects 
and it will require a strength of suppressant one level higher 
than actually needed or desired to suppress trauma.
Trauma Suppressants have some physical side effects. 
These start to manifest within a month of taking the drug. 
Roll 1d6 and add the drug’s strength to determine the speciﬁ c 
side effect. The side effect only needs to be rolled once but 
as stronger levels of drug are taken, the side effects become 
more acute and evident.
Trauma Suppressant Cost: Cr. 1,000 per level of strength, 
per monthly dose.

===PAGE 209===
 Crazies, Fatties, Muties & Perps
208
Adding 
Strangeness 
to Judge 
Dredd Games
There really should be no limit to the strangeness ﬁ nding 
its way into Judge Dredd settings. In his time Dredd has 
encountered inter-dimensional entities, ghosts, zombies, 
giants, the most peculiar crazes and the most warped 
mutants. Judges in your campaigns should too – but these 
things should be used sparingly. Judges have enough on 
their plates handling the thousands of ‘normal’ crimes 
committed in their sectors every day and the truly bizarre 
should be introduced every so often, to keep the challenges 
fresh, rather than on a regular basis, which would limit their 
impact.
But there really is nothing that is off-limits for a Judge 
Dredd campaign: forget scienti ﬁ c accuracy – anything and 
everything happens in Mega-City One and not always with 
a rational, or indeed any, explanation. Even the Tek and Psi 
Divisions are frequently bafﬂ ed by the things they encounter. 
Citizens, meddling with things they should not, are often 
the cause for strife and bloodshed: boredom plays a large 
part, as does morbid or even sinister curiosity. For reasons 
inexplicable to man, reality can be bent, distorted and broken 
resulting in many of the bizarre and dangerous things Mega-
City One experiences from time to time.
So, ludicrous, unscienti ﬁ c, extremes can and do, af ﬂ ict 
Mega-City One on a not-infrequent basis and Judge Dredd 
Referees should not feel constrained by creating totally 
rational, logical or scienti ﬁ cally based explanations or 
justiﬁ cations. Judge Dredd’s world is a heightened, fantastic 
twist on our own. It is a glorious parody on every ﬁ ctional and 
SF trope available, sprinkled liberally with black humour and 
a knowing wink to the audience.
 Aliens
Mankind has clearly gone to the stars in Judge Dredd’s 
saga. A good portion of the ‘Judge Child’ epic takes Dredd 
across the galaxy, encountering all manner of aliens and 
supernatural creatures along the way. This book does not 
have the space to cover all the alien races documented in 
the Judge Dredd saga, although future books and articles 
may well do so. However, aliens are known, traded with and 
warred with. Some of the best known are documented below 
but guidance is also given on creating aliens for use in Judge 
Dredd campaigns.
Mega-City One’s tourist industry bene ﬁ ts from no import 
quite as much as it does from the extraterrestrials who ﬂ ood 
the city a hundred million times a year to get a taste of the 
Big Meg, see the Judges in action, get tapped, take a few 
snapshots and then return home with thrilling tales to tell 
their clutch-mates. Custom-built hotels like the Giger Plaza 
house these alien visitors, specially furnished to cater to their 
environmental and cultural needs. Apartments exist pre-
conﬁ gured for the more frequent tourist species such as the 
Geeks from Geekus 2 and the Uqqan from Qu (major Mega-
City One trading partners). Tourist species are required to 
remain among those of their kind for the duration of their 
holiday so that they may avoid clashes with incompatible 
galactic neighbours. Translators hired from the tourist party’s 
own species facilitate friendly communication between the 
aliens and the people of Mega-City One. 
All aliens have their intergalactic passports checked by 
Eustace Fargo Spaceport Customs on arrival and visas 
are granted for periods up to 30 days. Extensions to that 
time limit must be applied for through the Alien Bureau. 
Suspicious cases are passed over to the Alien Bureau’s 
Justice Department wing for a full comp-ident examination, 
including a personal history and criminal record beamed 
straight from the visitor’s home world. Illegal aliens or those 
determined to pose a threat to the city will be detained in 
the nearby Alien Penitentiary until extradition papers can be 
arranged. 
Not all of the illegal aliens are so easy to detect. Some 
determined souls desperate to escape their former lives, 
seeking sanctuary in Mega-City One, manage to smuggle 
themselves into the city via cargo shuttles, hiding in crates or 
otherwise using strange alien powers to mask their presence 
from customs sensors. However, on leaving the spacecraft, 
the alien soon realises he has few options open to him but 
to make his way to Alientown where so many of his fellow 
immigrants have found themselves effectively imprisoned. 
Unable to establish a decent lifestyle for themselves, 
the Alientownees struggle to hide from frequent Justice 
Department sweeps and the consequential arrest and 
deportation. Alientown is so large and its denizens so hard 
to catch that the Judges have no choice but to tolerate its 
existence, so long as the inhabitants keep to themselves. 
Illegal aliens are not entitled to receive welfare payments 
or healthcare so must ﬁ nd ways to become self-suf ﬁ cient. 
Though the Judges may once in a while turn a blind eye, 
a xenophobic fringe movement known as the Grand Earth 
Council (GEC) are dedicated to ridding their city of the aliens 
by any means including arson and murder. The Judges are 
sworn to protect the aliens from the GEC attacks out of duty, 
not because they support their presence. 

===PAGE 210===
 Crazies, Fatties, Muties & Perps
209
Life is not too tough, for aliens are granted Mega-City 
One citizenship after living in the city for ﬁ ve years without 
earning a criminal record, but job opportunities are just as 
slim as they are for human citizens. Alien Welfare Of ﬁ cers 
work alongside applicant citizens to help ease their way 
into mainstream society. One success story is AlienLine; a 
taxi ﬁ rm founded by aliens that only employs off-worlders. 
Human citizens like the novelty of being driven about town 
by pulsating slime moulds and Zilusian Swamp-Creatures 
and pay that little bit extra for the experience! Apart from the 
odd tourist party, aliens are not that common a sight on the 
streets of Mega-City One. Most citizens will have only seen 
aliens from the comfort of their apartments watching ‘bug 
ﬂ icks’ on their Tri-D sets or visiting the world famous Alien 
Zoo with its many bizarre and monstrous exhibits.
Creating Aliens
The system for creating aliens used in the Traveller Core 
Rulebook (page 41) applies to Judge Dredd. Referees 
should design aliens that meet the need of a particular story 
but, if necessary, they can be generated randomly, using the 
system below.
All aliens use 2d6 as the basis for generating the core 
Traveller characteristics. However, roll 2d6 to determine if 
any characteristics are modiﬁ ed:
2d6 Result
2 Three characteristics are rolled on 1d6 rather 
than 2d6. Determine which three randomly:
1: Str, 2: Dex, 3: End, 4: Int, 5: Edu, 6: Soc.
3-5 As above but two characteristics are rolled on 1d6.
6-8 No characteristic modi ﬁ cation.
9-10 Two characteristics are rolled on 2d6+6. Determine 
which two randomly, using the chart above.
11 Three characteristics are rolled on 2d6+6. 
Determine which three randomly, using the 
chart above.
12 All characteristics are rolled on 2d6+6. 
Additionally, if the Int score is 15 or higher, the 
alien is also psionic and has a Psionic Strength 
score of 2d6, plus 1d3 Psionic Talents (use the 
range of Talents from the Thought Crimes 
chapter, beginning on page 184).
Next, roll 2d6 and note the Effect by subtracting 8 from the 
result. If the Effect is 0 or negative, then the alien has no alien 
traits. If the Effect is positive, then the alien has that number of 
alien traits, either chosen or rolled randomly on 2d6 using the 
Effect as a DM. The traits correspond with those found in the 
section on Aliens in the Traveller Core Rulebook.
Finally, determine the alien’s skills. Every alien has six skills, 
distributed as follows:
1 skill at level 3
2 skills at level 2
2 skills at level 1
1 skill at level 0
Skills can be chosen to match the alien’s purpose or 
environment or rolled randomly:

2d6 Trait Description
3 Aquatic As per the trait on page 41 of the Traveller Core Rulebook.
4 Armoured The alien has 1d6 points of natural armour. Roll 1d6 to determine the nature 
of the armour:
1: Thick hide, 2: Scales, 3: Crystal or chitin, 4: Fur, 5: Natural energy ﬁ eld, 
6: Cybernetic implants.
5 Atmospheric Requirements As per the trait on page 41 of the Traveller Core Rulebook but roll 1d6 
to determine the alien’s atmospheric needs:
1: Methane, 2: Carbon monoxide, 3: Pure oxygen, 4: Nitrogen, 5: Pure 
hydrogen, 6: A combination of any two of the above.
6 Engineered As per the trait on page 41 of the Traveller Core Rulebook. Roll 1d6 to 
determine why/how the alien has been engineered:
1: As a foodstuff, 2: As slaves/servants, 3: As soldiers/warriors, 4: As 
ambassadors/diplomats, 5: As merchants, 6: As spies.
7 Fast Metabolism As per the trait on page 41 of the Traveller Core Rulebook.
8 Feral As per the trait on page 41 of the Traveller Core Rulebook.
9 Flyer As per the trait on page 41 of the Traveller Core Rulebook.
10 Large As per the trait on page 41 of the Traveller Core Rulebook.
11 Mineral Digester The alien eats and digests rocks, minerals and metals, shunning organic 
food. Its is equipped with teeth and claws capable of cracking through its 
food stuffs, meaning that it naturally has the Natural Weapons trait.
12 Mutant-Like Traits The alien has 1d3 traits chosen from the table of Mutation Traits, found in 
this chapter on page 215.
13 Natural Weapon As per the trait on page 41 of the Traveller Core Rulebook.
14 No Fine Manipulators As per the trait on page 41 of the Traveller Core Rulebook.
15 Slow Metabolism As per the trait on page 42 of the Traveller Core Rulebook.
16 Xenophobic The alien hates all other life forms and is always antagonistic towards them. 
It is not necessarily aggressive but it never trusts any but its own species. If 
the chance to harm another species becomes available, then it takes it.
Aliens abroad in Mega-City One are generally equipped 
with Cr. 1,000x1d6, clothes, a carryall of some form, any 
special environmental equipment they need and a glazed 
expression.
 Crazies
People go crazy in Mega-City One for all kinds of reasons. 
The most common is Future Shock, creating ‘Futsies’ 
– the condition whereby the sheer strain of life in the 22
nd 
Century overwhelms the psyche, driving the sufferer into a 
violent, uncontrollable rage directed at other citizens and the 
trappings of Mega-City One life. However, there are other 
psychological conditions that grip citizens and these are 
outlined below.
‘Dredd’ Syndrome
Sufferers of ‘Dredd’ syndrome are gripped by the absolute 
conviction that the Judges, usually Dredd (being the city’s 
highest pro ﬁ le lawman), are actively hunting them down 
and will catch them for crimes they either committed years 
before, have contemplated committing or might commit in 
the future. Dredd Syndromists cannot sleep; their nightmares 
are plagued with the recurring theme of an arrest by Judges, 
hideous torture and then the severest sentence imaginable. 
Sufferers need psychiatric help to be able to function in 
their day-to-day lives; otherwise, every time a sufferer sees 
a Judge, he must roll End 10+ to keep calm and not draw 
attention to himself. If he fails, he gives-out all the signals 
of a perp and automatically ﬂ ees in panic. He is generally 
a danger only to himself and if/when caught, becomes so 
overwhelmed by the experience that he withdraws into an 
almost total catatonic state and must be conﬁ ned to a psycho 
cube for extensive – and possibly indeﬁ nite – treatment.
2T(FRU)T
This bizarre disease gripped Mega-City Two prompting 
Dredd and Spikes Harvey Rotten to cross the Cursed Earth 

===PAGE 212===
 Crazies, Fatties, Muties & Perps
211
with the vaccine. The disease is virulent and reduces its 
sufferers into cannibalistic zombies who crave living, human 
ﬂ esh (the forbidden fruit) and will stop at nothing to taste 
it. The disease requires a successful End 10+ to fend-off if 
one is exposed to it. If the Endurance roll fails, the disease 
incubates for 2d6 days before rapidly kicking-in. Sufferers 
are overtaken by the desire to eat other humans and launch 
into a murderous frenzy, usually wielding knives, in a bid to 
sustain the craving. Sufferers gain +4 to Str and Dex as a 
result of the disease and Int is reduced to 1. Additionally, 
sufferers gain +4 to the Melee skill, reﬂ ecting their diseased 
desire to consume fresh, human ﬂ esh.
The 2T(FRU)T vaccine, developed by Med Division, is 
effective and those treated can be completely cured within 
2d6 days, if they are given the correct course of treatment.
Block Mania
A psychological disorder that compels the sufferer to 
develop a psychotic allegiance to their own city block and an 
irrational hatred for all other city blocks, leading to escalating 
levels of violence. Block Mania can be a form of mass 
hysteria, caused by those troublemakers who take perverse 
enjoyment in whipping-up inter-block violence. Block citizens 
can resist exposure to the hysteria by rolling Int 8+, realising 
the stupidity of running with the crowd. However, Block Mania 
can also be induced by exotic, psychotropic drugs of the kind 
used by Orlok the Assassin to bring Mega-City One to its 
knees in the run-up to the Apocalypse War. These drugs 
need to be administered either via food or a block’s water 
supply and, if citizens are exposed to Block Mania drugs, 
then an Int 11+ roll is needed to resist the effects.
Again, a vaccine has been developed by Med Division and, 
when administered, it counteracts Block Mania (as long as it 
is drug-induced) within 2d6 hours.
Future Shock
Futsies simply cannot cope with the strain of 22 nd Century 
life and snap, engaging in violent rampages against any 
who stand in their way. The triggers of futsism are not fully 
understood and the only cure is intensive psychotherapy over 
a course of 1d6+2 months. Sufferers gain +3 Str and Dex and 
Int is reduced to 2 as they launch frenzied attacks against 
anything that can be construed as representing a threat. 
The attacks continue until the sufferer has been restrained. 
Sedatives keep the sufferer calm whilst psychotherapy in a 
psyko cube is administered.
Lemming Syndrome
A form of mass hysteria that leads citizens, in their dozens, 
to simply walk to their deaths from the highest point of a city 
block they can reach. The cause is unknown but it is thought 
by Med Division that it is a form of Future Shock. One person 
suffering from Lemming Syndrome can attract others. A 
citizen can avoid contracting the syndrome by rolling Int 
6+; however, for every 6 sufferers preparing to jump, the 
target number rises by 1. So, if a citizen sees 36 or more 
fellow citizens preparing to hurl themselves into oblivion, 
he must throw Int 12+ to avoid being caught in the mania 
and following their example. Only restraint can prevent a 
Lemming sufferer from leaping to his messy fate.
Ludditism
Another form of Future Shock, but a milder one that is aimed 
solely at machines and robots. The trigger is usually the loss 
of a prized job to a computer or robot and a citizen in such 
a position must throw Int 6+ to avoid succumbing. Ludditism 
sends the sufferer into a psychotic, machine-wrecking 
rage for 3d6 hours, during which restraint is necessary. All 
machines are targets but people generally are not – unless 
they attempt to defend machines. The condition is treatable 
by 2-3 months of psyko cube therapy.
Mentalism
Some citizens are just plain crazy. They do not go in for 
the crazes of Mega-City One or succumb to the general 
despondency most citizens display daily. They are happy 
with their lot. They smile, whistle, act in an up-beat manner 
and generally attract the suspicion of everyone (including 
Judges) around them. Mentalists are willing to try most 
things suggested to them in a bid to be normal (i.e. gloomy, 
pessimistic and prone to crazes or just general boredom). 
Psychotherapy can help but generally mentalists are stuck 
with their afﬂ iction. They often believe that they are stuck 
in some form of dream, vid-show or even a comic-book 
rendition of their lives and recount their experiences to an 
imaginary audience, viewer or reader. Just like I’m doing 
now…
Petty Crime Addiction (PCA)
Petty Crime Addicts cannot help themselves from committing 
petty crimes. These generally involve mild, antisocial 
behaviour but the sufferer feels compelled to commit the 
offence even though he knows the consequences if caught. 
PCA sufferers must roll End 10+ to control their urges, 
whenever the opportunity for petty crime presents itself. 
PCA sufferers are sometimes dif ﬁ cult to diagnose and Med 
Division is not wholly convinced that the condition really 
exists – although groups like Perp Aid try to cure PCA 
sufferers through intensive group therapy. This can be 
effective but if a perp commits a at least three petty crime 
rampages within a short period of time, the recommended 
course of action is a lobotomy to ensure that all tendencies 
to offend are removed.

===PAGE 213===
 Crazies, Fatties, Muties & Perps
212
Rumour Zombies
Not unlike Block Mania or Lemming Syndrome, Rumour 
Zombies develop an irrational need to mete out vengeance 
on anyone who, according to rumour, has committed some 
form of crime or social outrage. Those with a tendency 
to believe rumours and act on them, must throw Int 6+ to 
ignore the growing sense of rumour-fuelled anger; however, 
for every 6 sufferers preparing to act, the target number 
rises by 1. So, if a citizen sees 36 or more fellow citizens 
preparing to take vengeance on some poor, innocent sap, 
he must throw Int 12+ to remain in control. Those who fail 
are driven to gang-up on, terrorise and even murder, the 
object of the rumours. The sufferer never knows why they 
act in this way and can never pinpoint the source of the 
rumour. If questioned, they simply know that ‘someone told 
someone that he did something…’ but the justi ﬁ cation is 
always vague. Treatment is 1d6 months of rumour aversion 
therapy in a psyko cube.
Stookie Gland Addiction
Stookie Gland addicts want to avoid aging – pure and 
simple. The addifax gland of the Stookie not only halts aging, 
it reverses it; one dose reverses physical aging by 2d6+3 
years. Unfortunately, Stookie gland serum is addictive; users 
must roll End 8+ to control cravings for the serum – and 
every ﬁ ve years retarded by the drug increases the target 
number for the roll by 1. Thus, a citizen who has gained 15 
years must roll End 11+ to control his addiction. After the 
ﬁ rst dose the effectiveness, in terms of the years gained, 
lessens. The second dose restores 2d6 years; the third 1d6; 
the fourth 1d3 and all subsequent doses offer only 1 year 
of age retardation. However, once a user has gained the 
equivalent of 21 years of youth, addiction is permanent; he 
has to continue taking the drug even though he gains very 
little youth. As the drug is illegal and supplies rare, addicts 
are pushed to ever-greater lengths to get their ﬁ x – and this 
usually means crime. If an addict cannot gain a dose of 
serum every month once he is addicted, he regains age at 
the rate of 1 year every 6 days. Addicts denied their ﬁ x can 
be dead within a few weeks as time catches up with them.
Med Division has synthetic addifax serum which is used 
to control the urges and is administered so that users can 
serve-out their sentences in the iso cubes. For many though, 
this is simply a death sentence, because, denied the real 
serum, they will rapidly return to their real age.
Umpty Candy Addiction
The most marvellous taste in the world, developed by 
the hapless Uncle Ump, is psychologically, rather than 
physically, addictive. The odd taste has no lasting effects 
but for every 6 bars consumed, the user must roll Int 8+ to 
avoid hallucinations. These hallucinations generally take 
the form of large, anthropomorphic Umpty Bars, dancing 
in a tantalising fashion before the sufferer. The sufferer is 
tempted into the dance, in the hope of gaining more candy. 
The treatment is enforced withdrawal and psychotherapy 
in a psyko cube (in addition to the sentence for Umpty 
Candy use).
 Fatties
The cult of ﬂ ab began as a by-product of the ‘ugly’ craze 
back in 2102 when a weight-increasing product called 
‘Flabbon’ ﬁ rst became commercially available. Flabbon 
offered lazy citizens the chance to take part in a craze 
of their own that did not demand any effort other than 
eating. Within months, a new sub-culture had ﬂ opped into 
the limelight, unimaginatively nicknamed ‘fatties.’ These 
champion scoffers were so obese that they required 
belliwheels to keep their gargantuan stomachs from 
dragging along the ﬂ oor. Fatties spent their days eating 
and drinking, dreaming of the day when they might have 
gained enough weight to compete in the Intercity Eating 
Championship where gut-busting fatties like Tony Tubbs 
have weighed in at a staggering two tons.
The craze has largely abated but fatties still exist. Eating 
is compulsive and fatties go to great lengths to gain food 
or Flabbon. Fatties consume everything they can see, 
consuming vast meals several times a day and using appetite 
stimulants increases hunger. Fatties are generally 100kg 
overweight (with the average weight being 80kg, fatties 
include all those of 180kg plus). For every 50kg over 200kg 
of weight, the fatty’s characteristics undergo the following 
modiﬁ cations:
Str –1
End +1
Dex –2
However, whilst still mobile and using a bellywheel, fatties 
can be a hazard – especially if they stampede after food. 
Any fatty must roll Int 10+ to curb his food cravings, if faced 
with the opportunity to eat. If he fails, he stampedes. The 
momentum of a stampeding fatty causes 2d6 +End DM 
damage to anyone who cannot get out of the way
All skills that have either a Strength or Dex-based component 
undergo a –1 DM for every 50kg of extra weight (or fraction 
thereof), above 100kg. Eventually, fatties cannot even 
move, becoming bed-ridden and even bellywheels will not 
help them.

===PAGE 214===
 Crazies, Fatties, Muties & Perps
213
 Mutants
The radiation of the Atomic War led to the widespread 
occurrences of mutation across great swathes of the 
population. Until recently, to prevent further corruption of the 
gene pool, all the Mega Cities of America forbade mutants 
from living within their walls, conﬁ ning them to the irradiated 
wastelands of the Cursed Earth.
Mutants have struggled for decades with this injustice. Some 
truly are evil and corrupt but many are innocent victims of 
a dreadful cataclysm, more deserving of sympathy than 
ostracism. Dredd ﬁ nally saw this after many visits to the 
Cursed Earth and his increasing sympathy led to a relaxation 
of the Mutant Act, which prevented mutants from setting foot 
in Mega-City One.
Mutants, or Muties as they are known pejoratively, are a 
common facet of Dredd’s America. They proliferate in the 
Cursed Earth, of course, but are also found within the walls 
of Mega-City One, where they are feared, pitied, hated and 
avoided in equal measure.
Mutation Rules
Any creature can be mutated. To create a mutant choose 
the kind of creature to mutate and follow the procedure 
given below, using the normal statistics for the creature but 
adjusting where necessary to give the desired result.
Severity of Mutation
First, determine the severity of the mutation using 2d6.
Effect on Characteristics
Next, when the severity of mutation has been established, decide 
which characteristics the mutation affects, then modify those 
characteristics according to the table below. Characteristics 
can be adjusted either positively or negatively, depending on 
the nature of the mutation (having no arms, for example, would 
lower Strength; having four might increase it).
Light  +/–1
Moderate  +/–1d3
Radical  +/–1d3+1
Severe  +/–1d3+2
Shambolic +/–1d3+3

Mutant Traits
Mutation is often more than skin deep and some mutants 
possess special abilities. To simulate these, roll for Mutant 
Traits as indicated by the Severity of Mutation table. The 
result provides the number of traits that are selected from 
the Mutation Traits table, on the following page. Note that 
mutation traits effects are in addition to beneﬁ ts or detriments 
gained as part of the severity of mutation.
Finally, determine the mutie’s skills. Every mutant has up to 
six skills, distributed as follows:
1 skill at level 3
2 skills at level 2
2 skills at level 1
1 skill at level 0
The skill levels can be combined but no skill should be above 
level 4. Skills can be chosen to match the mutie’s purpose 
or environment or rolled randomly – use the Alien Skill table 
on page 209.

Mutant Traits
Trait Effects
Absorbing The mutant drains 1d6 characteristic points when it comes into physical contact with an opponent. 
Roll for which characteristic points are drained randomly:
1: Strength, 2: Dexterity, 3: Endurance, 4: Intelligence, 5: Psionic Strength, 6: In ﬂ uence (for 
Judges) or Social Standing (by withering the appearance of the opponent).
Characteristic point drains are in addition to any normal physical damage the mutant inﬂ icts.
Acid Exuder The mutant exudes an acidic ichor which sprays whenever the skin is penetrated or which can 
be spat/squirted and so on, causing damage to the attacker; roll 1d6 to determine the strength 
of damage the acid inﬂ icts:
1: 1 point, 2: 1d3, 3: 1d3+1, 4: 1d6, 5: 1d6+1, 6: 2d6.
If squirting the acid or attempting to direct it with accuracy, the mutant must succeed in a Dex 8+ 
roll. It may use any skill it has in Natural Weapons as a DM to the roll.
Agile +1d3 Dexterity
Antennae Large antennae which sprout from a random location and grant a new primary sense; roll 1d6:
1: Echolocation (sonar), 2: Thermoperception (heat), 3: Insanoception (insanity), 4: Nociception 
(pain), 5: Electroperception (electrical energy), 6: Emotiception (a particular emotion, such as 
fear or hatred).
The primary sense has an effective range equal to the mutants Strengthx10 in metres.
Armed The mutant has an additional 1d3 limbs that terminate in a weapon-like protruberance (such as 
a scythe-like claw or a bony club). The weapon in ﬂ icts 1d6+1 damage and is used successfully 
on a Str 8+ roll. It may use any skill it has in Natural Weapons as a DM to the roll.
Armoured The mutant has a natural 1d6 points of armour protection across its whole body
Arms Gain an extra pair of limbs; roll 1d6:
1: Tentacles, 2: Spider-like legs, 3: Hooves, 4: Flightless wings, 5: Flippers or ﬁ ns, 6: Pincers.
Asymmetric One half of the mutant is larger, longer or taller than the other. Increase either Strength or 
Endurance by +1d6 but reduce Dexterity by the same amount.
Beaked The mutant has a bony, bird-like beak. It gains a bite attack for 1d6+1 damage which it can use 
successfully on a Dex 8+ roll. It may use any skill it has in Natural Weapons as a DM to the roll.
Big The mutant is especially large. It gains +1d6 Strength and Endurance but loses 1d6 Dexterity. 
Attacks against it are at a +1 DM owing to the mutant’s size.
Blind No vision organs. The mutant sees using other senses but its Dex is reduced by 1d3. It is not 
susceptible to any form of attacks that blind or impair physical vision.
Burning The mutant glows with burning heat, injuring anyone within melee range; roll 1d6 to determine 
the damage per round: 
1: 1 point, 2: 1d3, 3: 1d3+1, 4: 1d6, 5: 1d6+1, 6: 2d6.
Chameleon The mutant has a natural, chameleon-like camou ﬂ age which grants it the equivalent of Stealth 
4.
Clawed The mutant has claws instead of hands. It loses 1 point of Dex but gains 1 point of Str. The claws 
inﬂ ict 1d6+1 damage on a successful Dex 8+ roll.
Clever +1d3 Int.
Climbing Has a spider or lizard like ability to scale surfaces, gaining Athletics (co-ordination) 4 for climbing 
purposes.
Deaf The mutant has no hearing organs. It is therefore oblivious to spoken language and commands, 
and all sonic attacks.
Disturbing Creature is covered with unsettling forms, such as baby arms or multiple eyes or mouths. 
Remaining in its presence requires an End 8+ roll; if this is failed, then the mutant’s appearance 
is too disturbing to contemplate and the observer is forced to get out of its sight. If the roll is a 
success, the observer suffers a –1 DM to all skills whilst in its presence.

216
Droning The mutant produces a buzzing noise that sedates listeners, either verbally or through rubbing 
together its limbs or other physical components. Everyone in a 10m radius must roll End 8+. If 
unsuccessful they become relaxed and unwilling to attack or oppose the mutant for 1d6 minutes.
Feeble –1d3 Strength.
Frail –1d3 Endurance.
Head The mutant has an extra head – not necessarily human – which is imbued with the same 
intelligence as the primary head. The additional head has a personality of its own: roll 1d6:
1: Arrogant, 2: Passive, 3: Psychopathic, 4: Antagonistic, 5: Kind and compassionate, 6: 
Sarcastic but witty.
Horned The mutant is equipped with horns and gains a gore attack. The attack is successful on a 
Strength 8+ roll and inﬂ icts 2d6–1 damage.
Icy Radiates freezing coldness, injuring anyone within melee range; roll 1d6 for the degree of 
damage caused every round:
1: 1 point, 2: 1d3, 3: 1d3+1, 4: 1d6, 5: 1d6+1, 6: 2d6.
Intangible The mutant can phase itself out of existence for 1d3 Combat Rounds. Whilst Intangible it cannot 
be harmed physically and can pass through solid substances. After using this power, it cannot 
be used against for another 1d6+1 Combat Rounds.
Inverted All vital organs are external, thus all successful attacks upon it inﬂ ict twice rolled damage.
Leaper Has powerfully sprung legs, gains +1d3 Dex and Athletics (co-ordination) 4 when leaping.
Levitating Has the capability to move over any solid or liquid surface without sinking or leaving tracks. The 
mutant levitates at a maximum height, in metres, equal to its Strength and moves at 6m per 
Combat Round but has no other Psionic Powers.
Mimic Changes into the shape of a creature it successfully touches, maintaining its own characteristics 
but assuming a duplicate image of the organic creature contacted. It can maintain this shape for 
a number of Combat Rounds equal to its Endurance.
Mournful Appearance is so sad or pathetic, that observers must succeed in an Intelligence 8+ or be so 
ﬁ lled with compassion that they cannot possible harm or allow the mutant to come to harm.
Obfuscating Produces a cloud of mist that blinds observers. The cloud has a radius in metres equal to the 
mutant’s Endurance and those caught in it suffer a –3 penalty to all skills.
Observant One of the creature’s sensory organs either grows bigger (x1d6 in size) or multiplies (x1d6 in 
number), adding a +3 DM to any skill when using that sensory perception; roll 1d6: 
1: Sight, 2: Sound, 3: Smell, 4: Taste, 5: Touch, 6: Referee’s choice.
Quick The mutant gains an additional Signiﬁ cant Action every Combat round and +2 to Initiative rolls.
Regenerating The mutant heals 1d6 points of damage every Combat Round.
Roaring The mutant produces a thunderous sound which deafens listeners, who must succeed in an 
Endurance 8+ roll or be completely deafened for a number of Combat Rounds equal to the 
mutant’s Strength.
Scintillating A shifting polychromatic appearance that confuses onlookers, who must succeed in an Intelligence 
8+ roll or suffer a –3 DM to all skills when dealing with the mutant in any direct way.
Screaming Horrible screams which terrify listeners, who must succeed in an Endurance 8+ roll or ﬂ ee for 
their lives. The terror inﬂ icted by the scream lasts for 1d6 hours after the initial attack.
Skinless The mutant has no skin covering its muscles and sinews. It has no Armour Points and cannot 
wear armour. It also suffers an additional +1 damage when physically attacked.
Slimy Leaves a sticky trail wherever it travels.
Slow The mutant shambles at a maximum of 3 metres per Combat Round and suffers –1 to Initiative 
rolls.
Small The mutant gains +1d6 Dex but reduces Strength by the same amount.
Spiny The mutant is equipped with a mass of sharp spines. Opponents in melee suffer half their own 
rolled damage if they successfully land an attack.
Trait Effects

===PAGE 218===

217
Stench Gives off nauseating odours that sicken inhalers, who must succeed in an Endurance 10+ roll 
to avoid dropping prone and vomiting. If affected, no other actions can be taken for 1d6 Combat 
Rounds until the nausea passes.
Sticky Secretes a strong glue or mucus which causes items to adhere to its skin, requiring a Str 10+ 
roll to remove.
Sting The mutant has some form of stinger. It causes damage on a successful Dex 8+ attack. Roll 1d6 
to determine the damage the sting causes:
1: 1 point, 2: 1d3, 3: 1d3+1, 4: 1d6, 5: 1d6+1, 6: 2d6.
Strong +1d6 Str.
Stupid –1d6 Int.
Swimmer Has webbed digits and gills. The mutant can swim at 8m per Combat Round and can breathe 
under water for a number of minutes equal to its Str+End.
Tongue The mutant has a long, toad-like tongue which can be used to strike, for 1d3 damage, at targets 
up to a number of metres equal to the mutant’s Dex. To strike a speci ﬁ c location or area, the 
mutant must succeed in a Dex 8+ roll.
Tough +1d6 End.
Warped One random location changes to that of a different species; roll 1d6 for the location affected and 
1d6 for the species it changes to:
1: Head, 2: Upper Body, 3: Lower Body, 4: Arms, 5: Legs, 6: Referee’s choice.
1: Cat, 2: Dog, 3: Bull, 4: Goat, 5: Pig, 6: Referee’s choice.
Wings Gains bat, bird, butter ﬂ y or insect wings granting a Flying Movement equal to normal 
Movement.

 Perps
Everyone in Mega-City One is a potential perp. Innocuous 
activities can be crimes and the ever-vigilant Judges are 
always on the look-out for citizens who commit crime, 
however small, or are just about to. Ignorance of the law is 
no defence; blatantly ﬂ outing it earns time in the cubes.
Every Judge on a routine patrol encounters many perps in a 
single watch. The system here is designed to provide some 
random, on-the-spot perps and crimes for Judges to deal 
with in the course of their duties.
Determine the Nature of the 
Perp
Roll 2d6 to discover the perp’s nature:

And how competent is the perp with that weapon? Roll 1d6–
3 to determine the skill level in the weapon he has. If the 
result is negative, then the perp is unskilled and suffers a –3 
DM when trying to use the weapon.
Determine the Creep’s Attitude
How is the perp going to react to the Judge’s intervention? Roll 2d6 
to determine the perp’s Desperation (see the I Am The Law 
chapter, page 76). If the perp is armed, apply a +2 DM to the roll.

How Many Citizens Are Causing 
an Obstruction?
Roll 4d6–4 (generating a number between 0 and 20) to 
determine how many additional citizens are in the vicinity 
of the perp, who could become obstructions, targets or 
hostages. As an option, roll 2d6 to see how they react once 
the Judge intervenes…
2d6 Reaction
2 Aid the perp somehow – Judges are really 
hated in this part of the sector.
3 Harass the Judge – this is sheer victimisation!

around, regardless of the crime underway.
5 General obstruction – not their fault; they do not 
want to be there.
6 Get out of the way and remain uninvolved.
7 Get out of the way and remain uninvolved.
8 Get out of the way and remain uninvolved.
9 Get out of the way and remain uninvolved.

line of ﬁ re.
11 Victim Material – citizens place themselves in a 
situation the perp can use (hostage, for instance).
12 Citizens aid the Judge – but risk taking the law 
into their own hands.
Assorted 
Adversaries
This section provides game statistics for the kinds of aliens, 
crazies, mutants and creeps the previous sections have 
reviewed. It includes creatures such as the dinosaurs of 
the Cursed Earth, aliens such as the feared Kleggs and 
other strangeness to challenge and perplex Judges in your 
campaigns.
 Apes
The scientists of the 21 st Century performed many genetic-
enhancing experiments upon animals with the aim of 
increasing their intelligence. Though these experiments are 
now illegal, intelligent apes can be found in great numbers 
in an area of Mega-City One known as the Jungle. Although 
most apes are content to keep to themselves and their own 
community, all too many have turned to crime as a means of 
rising above both fellow ape and human. The most famous 
of these was Don Uggie Apelino who, with the help of Fast 
Eeek and Joe Bananas, ran a number of mobster rackets 
until ﬁ nally sentenced to imprisonment within the Mega-
City Zoo by Judge Dredd. Many ape criminal gangs still 
persist today, operating out of the Jungle, though none have 
managed to achieve the notoriety of Don Apelino. Another 
ape fast gaining a reputation for himself is the simian 
bodyguard of private investigator Galen DeMarco, a gorilla 
known as Travis Perkins.
Typical Statistics: Str 2d6+2, Dex 2d6+3, End 2d6, Int 
2d6, Edu 2d6–2, Soc 2d6–2
Average Chimp: Str 9 (+1), Dex 10 (+1), End 7 (+0), Int 
7 (+0), Edu 5 (–1), Soc 5 (–1)

 Crazies, Fatties, Muties & Perps
220
Skills: Athletics (co-ordination) 4, Athletics (strength) 3, 
Melee (unarmed combat) 1, Steward 1, Tactics 1.
Traits: Apes can detect enemies by their scent alone. 
They automatically detect all enemies within 10 metres by 
their scent, increasing to a 20 metres if they are downwind 
or decreasing to 5 metres if they are upwind.
Typical Weapons:  Clubs, antique ballistic weapons 
(gangsters only).
Armour: None.
Gorilla: Str 2d6+4, Dex 2d6+2, End 2d6+2, Int 2d6–1, Edu

6 (+0), Edu 5 (–1), Soc 5 (–1)
Skills: Athletics (co-ordination) 2, Athletics (strength) 4, 
Gun Combat (slug pistol) 1, Melee (unarmed combat) 3
Traits: Apes can detect enemies by their scent alone. 
They automatically detect all enemies within 10 metres by 
their scent, increasing to a 20 metres if they are downwind 
or decreasing to 5 metres if they are upwind.
Typical Weapons:  Clubs, antique ballistic weapons 
(gangsters only).
Armour: None.
 Dog Vultures (Cursed Earth 
Mutant Animals)
There are many species of mutated vermin to be found in the 
barren wastes of the Cursed Earth though packs have been 
known to ﬂ y across the walls of Mega-City One undetected, 
causing terror amongst the citizens until the Judges 
are called in to deal with the menace. One of the most 
common are dog vultures, mutant pack hunters that plague 
the unwary daring to travel across the irradiated desert. 
Capable ﬂ yers, dog vultures can stay aloft for hours, circling 
the landscape to prey on the weak and helpless. However, 
they are scavengers by nature and a strong display of force 
can easily cause them to ﬂ ee in panic. Dog vultures never 
attack targets that outnumber themselves and heavy losses 
amongst their pack will invariably cause them to take to the 
air with all possible speed. If a lone victim is sighted by the 
pack, they attack in force and attempt to overwhelm their 
target through sheer weight of numbers. 
Typical Statistics: Str 2d6+2; Dex 3d6–1; End 2d6; Int 
2d6
Average Dog Vulture: Str 9 (+1), Dex 10 (+1), End 7 
(+0), Int 7 (+0), Edu –, Soc –
Skills: Flyer 3, Melee (Natural Weapons) 1.
Traits: Dog Bite (Natural Weapon), Flight (vulture wings) 
6m per round.
Typical Weapons:  Bite (Melee Natural Weapon) 
1d6+2.
Armour: 1 point for thick fur.
 Gila-Munja
The Gila-Munja are a tribe of mutated assassins who live 
only to carry out their deadly deeds and will not rest until 
their contract is ful ﬁ lled. As assassins, the Gila-Munja are 
amongst the deadliest on Earth and are ideally suited to this 
task. Their thick hides are capable of changing colour almost 
instantly to match their surroundings ﬂ awlessly, often giving 
the impression that these creatures can turn invisible at will. 
Well adapted to life in the Cursed Earth, the Gila-Munja are 
feared throughout the wasteland but many often attempt 
to inﬁ ltrate Mega-City One to ful ﬁ l assassination contracts 
made with the lowest scum of the city. A criminal bent on 
hiring the Gila-Munja to end the life of an enemy must meet 
them in their homelands north of Texas City and launch a 
ﬂ are to announce his presence. The Gila-Munja listen to the 
contract being offered and the price should they, inevitably, 
succeed. Poor offers have been known to result in the Gila-
Munja killing their prospective employer. If they do decide to 
accept the contract, the Gila-Munja seal the deal by slaying 
one of their own number in a sacred ritual and pledging not 
to rest until the assassination has been completed. 
Small and squat, the Gila-Munja grow to be the height of 
a small man but underneath the grey oily skin is a mass 
of muscle ﬁ bre and tissue that belies their stature. Powerful 
legs allow the Gila-Munja to scale most vertical surfaces 
with ease and their razor-sharp claws are capable of slicing 
open even the thickest armour. Coated with an utterly lethal 
contact poison, these crab-like claws are the Gila-Munja’s 
primary weapon, remaining potent for several days after the 
creature is dead. 
Typical Statistics: Str 3d6–2, Dex 3d6+1, End 2d6, Int 
2d6, Edu 1d6–1, Soc 1d6–1
Average Gila Munja: Str 9 (+1), Dex 12 (+2), End 7 
(+0), Int 7 (+0), Edu 2 (–2), Soc 2 (–2)
Skills: Broker 2, Melee (Natural Weapons) 3, Stealth 4.

Typical Statistics: Str 3d6+4, Dex 2d6+1, End 2d6+5, 
Int 2d6–1, Edu 2d6–1, Soc 1d6–2
Average Klegg Mercenary:  Str 15 (+3), Dex 8 (+0), 
End 12 (+2), Int 6 (+0), Edu 6 (+0), Soc 2 (–2)
Skills: Broker 1, Computers 1, Gun Combat (slug ri ﬂ e) 3, 
Melee (natural weapons) 2, Survival 2.
Typical Weapons: Klegg Assault Cannon (see below), 
Bite 2d6+1, Fists 1d6+3.

armour.
 Klegghound
The klegghounds are never far from their alien masters and 
are used to track, round up and slay any prey the kleggs 
themselves are unable to capture. Muscular in build, these 
huge, reptilian dog-like creatures are renowned for their ability 
to track prey, not by smell but by taste alone. By consuming 
a possession of a victim or simply licking the road where tyre 
tracks have been left, klegghounds can relentlessly pursue 
their prey for many hours with a high degree of accuracy. 
Kleggs and their hounds tend to form tight bonds whereby 
the creature will follow its master’s wishes almost intuitively 
though their animal intelligence makes them rather easier 
to fool when the kleggs are not around. Klegghounds prefer 
to attack in great numbers, where their powerful build and 
superior close combat abilities will bring down almost any 
enemy. They are skilled at acting in concert with the kleggs 
themselves and are often used in frontal assaults of enemy-
held positions whilst the kleggs lay down awesome amounts 
of ﬁ repower.
Traits: Chameleon, Pincers.
Typical Weapons: Pincers, 2d6+3, plus contact poison 
(additional 3d6 damage).
Armour: 1 point for oily skin and sinew.
 Kleggs
The kleggs are a race of alien mercenaries, made famous 
on Earth during the reign of the insane Chief Judge Caligula 
when they were used to pacify the citizens of Mega-City One 
in 2101. Kleggs resemble 4 metre high, bipedal crocodiles and 
are utterly savage, taking payment not in credits or groats but 
only in fresh meat. Their favourite contracts are generally those 
that involve being hired to annihilate sub-species of creatures 
on distant worlds, being permitted to eat the sub-species as 
payment. Incredibly strong and carrying a lethal array of heavy 
weapons, kleggs are all but unstoppable in battle and the 
combination of their thick hides and blast armour make them 
practically impervious to small arms ﬁ re. After a signi ﬁ cant 
number were annihilated by Judge Dredd, most kleggs have 
vowed never to return to Mega-City One but some still dwell in 
ruined sectors and the Cursed Earth, having been left behind 
nearly twenty years before and others will dare to set foot in 
the city if the payment is high enough. 
Kleggs rarely engage in convoluted tactics and stratagems, 
as their brute strength and weapons ensure virtually no other 
race can withstand them in combat. Their loud, rasping 
voices announce the arrival of the kleggs and bring terror 
to their victims, something the aliens use in order to sow 
confusion amongst an enemy. A popular war cry is ‘Klegg-
Haiiii!’ though they are also given to longer and altogether 
more poetic chants such as ‘Meaty beaty, chop ‘em neatly, 
death or glory, no retreatee!’
Klegg Assault Cannon
The assault cannon used by the kleggs is an extremely large weapon, having more in common with a street cannon 
than any other riﬂ e. It is far too large and heavy for a human to use but perps able to procure one of these incredibly large 
weapons have been known to mount them on vehicles.

 Mutants
The mutants here are created using the Mutation rules 
beginning on page 213 of this chapter. They serve as 
examples of the kind of mutants found in the Cursed Earth 
although there are, (un)naturally, countless variations.
Necky the Prospector 
– Radical Mutation (Human 
Mutant)
This mutant’s neck is attached to the poor wretch’s chest 
rather than his shoulders and his feet are an outlandish 
approximation of bird’s feet – scaled and taloned. The 
mutant has no Mutational Traits and perhaps keeps his 
sunny disposition because of it. He works in the rad-tar pits 
bringing-up old treasures from the pre-war days, which he 
cleans off and offers for sale at his pathetic shack up on 
the hill. His most prize possession is his working stump gun, 
which he has used to see-off marauders quite a few times 
now. Their skulls line the stockade around his tar pit, as a 
warning.
Str 12 (+2), Dex 12 (+2), End 7 (+0), Int 6 (+0), Edu 3 (–1), 
Soc 3 (–1)
Skills: Athletics (co-ordination) 3, Gun Combat 0, Science 
(prospectin’ rad-tar pit) 2.
Traits: None.
Typical Weapons: Stump gun and 10 shells.
Armour: None.
Dumbo Raddox – Severe 
Mutation (Human Mutant)
This mutant’s hands are where his ears should be and his 
ears are on the ends of his arms. His head is distended, 
crushing his features together into a long line but boy is 
that brain of his big! They call him Dumbo because of the 
hands and ears, not because he is dumb in any way but 
he lets people think he is before blinding them with astute 
observations and lines of poetry he has memorised from old 
books. And as he talks, lights dance around head, soothing 
and calming even the rowdiest of folks.
Str 5 (–1), Dex 1 (–2), End 5 (–1), Int 13 (+2), Edu 9 (+1), 
Soc 8 (+0)
Skills: Advocate 2, Life Sciences (biology) 2.
Traits: Mournful, Scintillating (lights generated by that big 
old brain).
Typical Weapons: None.
Armour: None.
Alien Finder General – Severe 
Mutation (Human Mutant)
The Alien Finder General of the Cursed Earth is the goat-
headed, self-appointed persecutor of aliens who dare to land 
on Earth and pollute the sanctity of his lands. Accompanied 
by his mutated posse and riding mutated horses, the Alien 
Finder General scours the radlands in search of aliens (or 
mutants who look like aliens) which are rounded-up and sold 
at the slave auctions that travel through the shanty towns, 
selling captured aliens into servitude and drudgery. Any 
alien who resists is put to the death – and many that do not 
are slaughtered simply to make an example.
Str 10 (+1), Dex 11 (+1), End 15 (+3), Int 10 (+1), Edu 9 (+1), 
Soc 10 (+1)
Skills: Animals (riding) 2, Animals (training) 2, Deception 
1, Gun Combat (slug pistol) 2, Melee (blade) 3, Melee 
(unarmed combat) 2, Stealth 2.
Traits: Warped (goat head), Tough.
Typical Weapons: Broadsword (4d6), Spitgun.
Armour: 3 point Cloth padding.
 Rad Rat
The mutant rats of 2124 are a terror to behold, for they can 
grow to huge sizes and bring down a fully grown human with 

===PAGE 224===
 Crazies, Fatties, Muties & Perps
223
ease when attacking in numbers. Believed to have mutated 
deep within the Cursed Earth after the Atomic War, mutant 
rats have become a plague both in the wasteland and within 
the darker regions of Mega-City One, where they scavenge 
and prey upon the weak. Their bite carries a dreadful poison 
that has mutated in potency along with the rats themselves. 
The typical mutant rat will grow to around two feet long, 
though specimens over twice this size have been reported in 
ruined sectors and Mega-City One’s sewer system. Mutant 
rats always attack in packs, in ﬂ icting dozens of poisonous 
wounds on their victims in a matter of seconds. Alone, they 
tend towards cowardice, though one should never be backed 
into a corner it cannot retreat from as this will likely result in 
instant attack. 
Typical Statistics: Str 2d6–2, Dex 3d6+1, End 2d6, Int 
1d6–2, Edu –, Soc –
Average Rad Rat: Str 5 (–1), Dex 12 (+2), End 7 (+0), 
Int 2 (–2), Edu –, Soc –
Skills: Athletics (co-ordination) 4, Melee (natural weapons) 
1, Survival 5.
Traits: Poisonous.
Typical Weapons:  Bite 1d3–1, plus the incisors of 
mutant rats contain a terrible poison that can cause severe 
pain and even death. Mutant rat poison requires any victim 
bitten and injured, to roll End 11+. If he succeeds, he takes 
a further 1d6 damage from the poison’s ability to induce 
nausea. If he fails, he suffers 1d6 damage every hour 
until Rad Rat vaccine (found in most medipacks) can be 
administered.
Armour: None.
 Sewer-Gators
The antiquated sewers and Undercity of Mega-City One 
are the home of ﬁ ve-metre long alligators who feed on 
rats and any citizen who gets in their way, such as sewer 
maintenance men or the ill-fated Jimmy Tarbuck Block 
Survival Club. The sewer-gators are white and blind but 
their incredible senses of hearing and smell can detect 
the scent of fresh ﬂ esh over a great distance. The sewers 
are deep enough that gators can swim silently up on 
their victims undetected and then rush them, jaws open, 
decapitating their food in one bone-splintering crunch. 
Though maintenance droids have been reprogrammed to 
hunt down and kill the ‘gators, a few smaller specimens 
have slowly found their way to sewer outlets leading into 
the city’s extensive man-made canal system. 
Typical Statistics: Str 3d6+4, Dex 2d6+3, End 3d6+2, 
Int 1d6–3, Edu –, Soc –
Average Sewer-Gator: Str 15 (+3), Dex 10 (+1), End 
13 (+2), Int 1 (–2), Edu –, Soc –
Skills: Athletics (strength) 4, Melee (natural weapons) 2, 
Survival 2.
Traits: Blind.
Typical Weapons: Bite 2d6+6, Tail-Lash 2d6–1.
Armour: 4 points for thick, scaled, albino hide.
 Troggies
The term ‘troggies’ originally referred to a secretive cult living 
deep beneath Mega-City One, intent on destroying the world 
above them. After the cult met its end at the hands of Judge 
Dredd, ‘troggies’ has come to refer to all the sub-humans 
living in the Undercity. Believed to have evolved from original 
inhabitants trapped in the old cities when Mega-City One was 
built on top of them, troggies have become adapted to their 
lightless environment. All but losing the ability to see, they 
can nonetheless sense their surroundings through a highly 
developed sense of smell and while physically weak, their 
large numbers can pose a threat to anyone daring to venture 
into the Undercity. Troggies attack any sentient beings they 
outnumber – although they stay clear of the Undercity’s 
more powerful denizens, such as sewer-gators.
Typical Statistics: Str 2d6, Dex 2d6+1, End 2d6, Int 
2d6–2, Edu 1d6–1, Soc –
Average Troggy: Str 7 (+0), Dex 8 (+0), End 7 (+0), Int 
5 (–1), Edu 3 (–1), Soc –
Skills: Melee (bludgeon) 1, Survival 2, Tactics 1.
Traits: Blind, Light Sensitive (any bursts or ﬂ ashes of light 
cause intense pain, rendering troggies helpless for 1d3 
Combat Rounds).
Typical Weapons: Club.
Armour: None.
 Tweaks
Peaceful, bear-like aliens, Tweaks are rock and ore 
digesters with an advanced but secretive clan society that 
prefers to keep itself isolated from outsiders. The Tweak 
home world is exceedingly rich is precious ores, metals 
and gems which, naturally, is a great attraction to other

Although peaceful, they can be provoked to great wrath and

Mean Machine Angel
Mean was the runt of the bunch: docile and peaceful whilst the 
others were cruel and rotten, Pa decided to cut the niceness 
out of Mean altogether and so took the gawky, ﬂ ower-loving 
boy to see a cyber ripper-doc in Texas City who, with a gun 
to his head, carried out the substantial corrective surgery 
that created the Mean Machine.
Mean has had large parts of his brain – those containing 
all passive/control functions – removed or altered. Mean is 
now permanently surly but his moods are controlled by a dial 
on his forehead which, with a ﬂ ick, goes from surly, through 
mean, then to vicious and, on 4, brutal. And Mean’s temper 
alters accordingly. To ice the cake, Mean’s right arm was 
removed and replaced with a cybernetic claw, lending him 
greater strength and an inbuilt weapon.
Even though his family has been killed, Mean has endured, 
having been resurrected once and been both an escapee and 
the test subject for a Justice Department mind control drug.
Str 11 (+2), Dex 7 (+1), End 12 (+2), Int 4 (–1), Edu 2 (–2), 
Soc 1 (–2)
Skills: Deception 1, Drive (wheeled) 1, Melee (unarmed 
combat) 3, Survival 4.
Traits: Cybernetic arm (+2 Str); Cybernetic 4-Point Brutality 
Meter. Mean’s characteristics and skills when set on 1 
(Surly) are as above. Each subsequent setting increases the 
following by a further 1: Str, End, Melee (unarmed combat). 
Thus, on 4 – Brutal, Mean has Str 14, End 15 and Melee 6.
Weapons: Cybernetic Arm 3d6+1. Head-butt 4d6, +1 for

Armour: 4 points for cybernetic enhancements and 
general meanness
Fink Angel
The forgotten Angel, Fink went off the live in a hole in the 
ground at a young age and never looked back. He scurried 
through the Cursed Earth, alone, for years, poisoning watering 
holes and robbing the unfortunates who drank from the mired 
waters. Fink is a master poisoner, with a vast array of slow 
acting, fast acting, paralysing and other poisonous concoctions 
in his arsenal. The Cursed Earth has taken its toll and Fink is a 
cadaverous, twisted creature with a hatred for all life. His only 
friend is Ratty, his loyal rad rat; everyone else is mere prey.
Discovering the demise of his Pa and brothers, Fink went to 
Mega-City One to exact revenge on Dredd and the crew of 
Justice One. Judge Larter fell to Fink’s poisons and Judge 
Hershey nearly succumbed as Fink fed her, alive, into a 
Resyk plant.

throw every minute to mitigate that minute’s damage.
Paralysin’ Poison: This poison cause no damage but 
the target must make an Endurance 10+ roll or be completely 
paralysed for 1d6 hours.
Fink coats his spiked caltrops with his poisons or can 
administer them directly to a water supply.

 Dark Judges
Sinister supernatural Judges from an alternative, doomed 
dimension where life itself is a crime, the Dark Judges – 
Death, Fire, Fear and Mortis – have vowed to bring the same 
law and justice to Mega-City One and they have attacked it 
several times over the decades (most successfully as part of 
the creation of Necropolis).
The Dark Judges cannot be killed as they do not truly live; 
once they were mortal but the Sisters of Death – Phobia and 
Nausea – made them into their current forms. Ectoplasmic 
entities with strong psionic powers, the Dark Judges have the 
ability to become Intangible (as per the mutant trait) although 
they can manifest physical forms or take possession of 
another’s body. Judge Anderson used this to her advantage 
to imprison Death when he ﬁ rst assaulted Mega-City One, 
containing him in her mind and then encasing him in Boing®.
Judge Death
Wearing a parody of Dredd’s uniform, with bone-emblazoned 
armour pads and a portcullis helmet, Death is foremost of 
the Dark Judges. His favoured method of attack is to reach 
into a person’s body and squeeze the heart until its bursts. 
When in a physical form he is impervious to any form of 
damage save ﬁ re, which causes twice its normal damage 
but his essence is always able to ﬂ ee and go in search 
of another host. To take possession, the victim must roll 
Endurance 8+ and note the Effect. The Effect needs to beat 
Death’s Possession level of 6 or Death automatically takes 
control of the body, imprisoning the mind of its owner and 
commanding it fully.

be and proof, were it needed, that absolute power corrupts 
absolutely.

===PAGE 232===
 Robots
231
As robotics technology developed so did the use of robots 
in other areas: improved intellect circuits meant that robots 
could take over administration and bureaucratic tasks; 
decision-making circuits meant that roles requiring complex 
decision making could be given to robots without the 
complications of emotion introducing bias or prejudice into 
the process. The use of robots grew and the use of humans 
declined.
The argument was always that humans would have more 
time to pursue leisure activities; to enjoy themselves. But 
the social engineers who pressed for increasing robotisation 
ignored the fact that humans are, for their most part, 
creatures that must be kept occupied. If jobs are given to 
machines, leaving humans without purpose, they grow bored 
quickly. And with boredom comes frustration and despair. 
With frustration and despair comes fear and loathing. And 
with those, crime and depredation swiftly follows.
As robots took over more and more roles in society, crime 
grew in relation. But by the time this correlation was made, 
it was too late. Robots were too ingrained in society, 
too important. There could be no turning back. Robots 
dominated and humans were largely super ﬂ uous. Humans 
had lost the capabilities to ful ﬁ l the roles they occupied 
before robotisation. Dismantling the robotised systems of 
Mega-City One would be to both admit failure and to place 
the city at risk of hideous inef ﬁ ciency. Robots had to stay 
and humans would just have to accept it.
Just about every job of importance is being done or can be 
done, by a robot of one class or another. Robots are bosses 
and supervisors; they are teachers and zoom-train drivers; 
they are medics, doctors and surgeons. Every job, every 
role (save for the Judges) in Mega-City One will have robots 
engaged somewhere in the process – and often at the top.
In response, humans treat robots as slaves. Laws exist to 
recognise robots’ rights and the penalties to damaging a 
robot through hate-driven motives are stiff. But robots are 
still treated as subservient entities and more so following 
the two Robot Wars (see below) that have af ﬂ icted Mega-
City One. Yet, in reality, robots are the inheritors of Mega-
City One. They make it work, keep it functioning whilst 
around them humans live their meaningless hum-drum 
lives, congratulating themselves upon their technological 
advances.
The  Robot 
Wa rs
The First Robot War took place during 2099 when the 
carpentry robot, Call-Me-Kenneth, malfunctioned and 
attacked humans. Although destroyed, its brain was installed 
into a new body by misguided scientists and, stronger and 
more dysfunctional than before, it ran amok again – but this 
time managed to attract other robots to its cause.
Widescale robotic rebellion including just about every kind of 
droid, from humble servant units through to massive industrial 
robots, turning on humanity intending to wipe it out. Judge 
Dredd led the war against the rebel robots, even though he 
had resigned over Justice Department’s reluctance to pass 
tougher robot control legislation following Call-Me-Kenneth’s 
ﬁ rst breakdown.
Dredd discovered that Call-Me-Kenneth had malfunctioned 
to the point of tyranny. Kenneth subjugated robots in the 
same way humans had but exhibited even less mercy. A few 
brave robots were prepared to rebel and Dredd, captured by 
Call-Me-Kenneth and aided by Walter the vending machine 
robot, managed to mobilise a rebellion of robots against Call-
Me-Kenneth. After much blood and oil were shed, Kenneth 
was confronted on the Weather Control centre and tumbled 
to its ﬁ nal destruction.
The Second Robot war took place in 2121. The result of 
Nero Narcos’s attempts to seize control of Mega-City One, 
the circumstances behind the Robot War are far more 
complicated than a simple robot rebellion, as they involve 
agents of the destroyed East Meg One and the assassin 
Orlok, who helped trigger the Apocalypse War. However, 
the Assassinator robots of Narcos overran Mega-City One 
and a weakened Justice Department (sabotaged by rigged 
Lawgivers, designed to explode when ﬁ red by Judges) 
was forced into ﬁ ghting a guerrilla war against the massive 
armies of war droids. Dredd saved the situation with the 
help of Brit-Cit, which destroyed Narcos’s Cursed Earth-
based reinforcements. Dredd led his squad of Judges, 
supplemented by reprogrammed Assassinators, back into 
the city to turn the tables against Narcos.
The Second Robot War saw Senior Judge Hershey, now a 
member of the Council of Five, distinguish herself. It also saw 
the beleaguered Chief Judge Volt take his own life, dismayed 

===PAGE 233===
 Robots
232
at the failures in his judgement that allowed Narcos’s plans 
to almost succeed. From the ashes of the Second Robot 
War Hershey arose to become the Chief Judge of Mega-City 
One.
 Robots in 
Mega-City One
Robots are ubiquitous. Robots come in every size and 
shape. They are so common a ﬁ xture of society that citizens 
of Mega-City One do not even notice them unless something 
goes wrong. They are always there, in the background, 
working, getting on with their mechanical lives, a mechanised 
backdrop to a rigid society.
Judge Dredd lacks the space to go into a detailed robot 
design system, so the mechanics presented here are 
intended to allow Judge Dredd Referees to use a set of 
standards to design robots easily and to create new types 
to suit campaigns with relative ease. The fundamentals of 
robot design use the following template:
• Function – what is the robot’s purpose? This determines 
its software package and hence its skills.
• Conﬁ g – what sort of shape is the robot? Humanoid 
is the most common but countless other designs are 
found. Conﬁ g determines the robot’s locomotion to a 
certain extent.
• Size – how big is the robot, compared with a standard 
human? Size has a direct in ﬂ uence on the robot’s 
characteristics.
• Personality – does the robot have a personality? Many 
are simple drones, lacking any form of communication. 
Others have complex personalities, such as Walter, Judge 
Dredd’s former sycophantic vending machine droid.
• Characteristics – robot characteristics are based on those 
of humans but are inﬂ uenced by function and size.

===PAGE 234===
 Robots
233
Robots in the Judge Dredd stories are frequently used to 
drive storylines – as the various stories featuring Walter 
and the Robot Wars show. The aim of robots in the Judge 
Dredd game is therefore similar; not be conﬁ ned by detailed 
technical rules for building realistic robots but to create a 
framework so that when robots are needed, the system 
reﬂ ects the way they appear in the Judge Dredd tales.
All robots manufactured legally in Mega-City One are hard-
wired to prevent them ever breaking the law, no matter what 
provocation they or their owners face. They are also coded to 
avoid harming or allowing harm to come to, a human being. 
One of the ﬁ rst modiﬁ cations any perp will make to a robot, 
if he can spare the credits, is to insert a lawbreaker slug into 
its computer brain, allowing it to circumvent these hardwired 
rules and even attack humans upon request.
Function
Every robot has a primary function: what it is designed to 
do. Its function therefore determines its programming and its 
capabilities – which are essentially the same skills as those 
available to all characters in Judge Dredd.
There are six functional categories, each with a sub-
category. The Conﬁ g column shows which con ﬁ g options 
are available for robots in this Functional Category; the Size 
column shows the typical size ranges available – although 
smaller and larger variants can certainly be found; and the 
Personality column shows how many Personality Traits a 
Function Category has. Finally, the Will Modi ﬁ er is applied 
to the dice rolled for the Will characteristic of the robot (see 
Characteristics, on page 235).
Config
A robot’s conﬁ g largely determines how it looks and how it 
moves. The most popular con ﬁ g type is Humanoid, which 
means that the robot has two arms, two legs, something 
passing for a head and walks around in a manner that 
makes humans feel comfortable. It does not necessarily look 
human or behave in that way but its con ﬁ guration is similar. 
The Confg types are as follows:
Humanoid
The robot has two arms, two legs and a form of head, which 
generally houses visual receptors and audio systems. The 
robot walks with a human-like gait. Humanoid con ﬁ g types 
are limited to Sizes S, M and L.
