===PAGE 235===
 Robots
234
Humanoid-Hybrid
The upper part of the robot is humanoid – so, a torso, two 
arms and a head unit but the lower half – its locomotion – is 
designed to re ﬂ ect its function. A medium labour droid, for 
example, might have caterpillar tracks in place of legs. The 
locomotion types are as follows, with their game effects 
quantiﬁ ed:
Hover
The robot is ﬁ tted with an anti-grav unit allowing it to hover 
between a few centimetres and 3 metres above the ground. 
It can move at up to 7 metres per round. This form of 
locomotion is limited to S and M sized robots.
Tracked
The robot is ﬁ tted with caterpillar or tank tracks, allowing it 
to work effectively in rough terrain environments or those 
environments where high traction is essential. Tracked 
robots move at a base 5 metres per round and tracks can 
only be ﬁ  tted to M sizes and above. Tracked robots are 
able to climb stairs and negotiate inclines but their speed is 
halved when doing so.
Wheeled
The robot is ﬁ tted with between one and six wheels (mono 
and two-wheeled robots are gyroscopically stabilised). They 
can move at up to 8 metres per round but cannot negotiate 
stairs or very steep inclines. Wheels can be ﬁ tted to any 
robot of size S and above.
Multi-Legged
The robot is ﬁ tted with several legs (anything up to eight) and 
propels itself in an insect-like fashion. Multi-legged robots 
move at a base of 6 metres per round but gain +1 to Dex due 

===PAGE 236===
 Robots
235
to their high natural agility. Multi-legs cannot be ﬁ tted to any 
robot larger than size M.
Vehicular
Vehicular robots are, effectively, autonomous vehicles that 
require no driver or pilot and are able to complete routine 
tasks, such as loading and unloading, without any additional 
intervention. This con ﬁ g comes in L, VL and XL sizes only 
and must be either tracked or wheeled.
Static
Static robots are fashioned into shapes ﬁ tting their function 
– so are usually box shaped or shaped to meet a particular 
space requirement in their place of work. They have no 
locomotion of their own and remain in one place to complete 
their task. Static robots can be of any size.
Animal
Animal conﬁ gs are designed to resemble a particular animal, 
such as a dog, and are most usually multi-legged. Animal 
conﬁ gs come in S, M and L sizes.
Characteristics
Robots have the same physical characteristics as humans: 
Strength, Dexterity and Endurance and these characteristics 
are used in the same way as for any character in the 
Traveller Core Rulebook.
All robots have intelligence but this is not rated using a 
characteristic: it is a measure of the robot’s personality 
(see below) and its programming. All robots are designed 
to carry out speciﬁ c tasks and every robot will perform these 
tasks without question. Some robots do, however, have a 
higher measure of independent decision-making and this 
is represented by the Will characteristic. Will represents 
the robot’s ability to perform a task or instruction that goes 
beyond its function. For instance ordering a carpenter 
robot to go and make a cup of synthi-caf is surplus to its 
programming and it may or may not perform the task. Will is 
rolled on 2d6, with a modiﬁ er for the robot’s functional type, 
as noted above.
To discover if a robot can, or will, exceed its programming, 
roll 2d6 and add the robot’s Will DM. The target number for 
success depends on the task itself (see below)
• A simple task associated with the robot’s basic 
programming but with some slight differences: 4+.
• A simple task that is different to the robot’s basic 
programming but within its skill and physical capabilities: 
6+.
• A routine task that the robot would not normally be 
expected to accomplish: 8+.
• A difﬁ cult task contrary to its routine programming and 
one that can be legitimately questioned by its logic 
circuits: 10+.
Size
Robot size is graded into the following:
Tiny (T)   Less than 50mm in length, height and width
Very Small (VS)  50-500mm in length, height and width
Small (S)  500-1,000mm in length, height and width
Medium (M)  1,000-2,000mm in length, height and width
Large (L)  2,000-5,000mm in length, height and width
Very Large (VL)  5,000-10,000mm in length, height and width
Extra Large (XL)  10,000mm + in length, height and width
The size of the robot determines the type of con ﬁ g and locomotion it has available to it, as described above. Size also 
inﬂ uences the robot’s characteristics:
Size Strength Average Dexterity Average Endurance Average Will Average
T 1d3 2 (–2) 2d6+6 13 (+2) 1d3 2 (–2) 2d6 7 (0)
VS 1d3+1 3 (–1) 2d6+3 9 (+1) 1d3+1 3 (–1) 2d6 7 (0)

===PAGE 237===
 Robots
236
• A task completely and utterly against the robot’s 
programming, function and purpose: 12+.
For example, Judge Walker orders a shelf-
stacking robot in a Mega-City One mall to trip-
up a ﬂ  eeing perp who is running for the exit 
after having stolen some sucker citizen’s wallet. 
The Referee rules that this action is dif ﬁ cult 
and contrary to the robot’s programming. The 
robot must roll Will 10+ to obey Walker’s barked 
command. It has a Will of 4, giving it a –1 DM to 
the task, so it must roll 11+ to obey. The Referee 
rolls 6; the shelf-stacker robot looks blankly at 
Judge Walker, intones ‘The harming of shoppers 
as they go about their business is contrary to my 
nature, sir,’ and continues packing a shelf with 
boxes. The perp laughs, Walker curses and 
has to hobble the creep with a leg-shot. As he 
cuffs the screaming, bleeding perp, he warns 
the shelf-stacker droid that refusing to obey the 
commands of a Judge constitutes an Obstruction 
of Justice which could result in the robot being 
dismantled and used for spares. The robot, not 
really caring, continues to make the shelves look 
nice, tidy and full.
Personality
Robots are given a personality in order to make it easier for 
humans to interact with them. All robots respond to verbal 
commands and reply with vocalised con ﬁ rmations but the 
injection of one or more personality traits gives the robot a 
certain individuality and character and makes the process 
of dealing with a robot less of a chore for those who have 
to interact with them on a regular basis (or more of one, 
depending on the prevalent traits).
The number of Personality traits a robot has is determined 
by its Function. If the die roll results in 0, which it may do, 
then the robot has no discernible personality, responding in 
a clinical, functional, emotionless manner.
Where Personality traits are present, these can be chosen 
to ﬁ t the nature of the droid or rolled randomly on d66 on 
the below table. Not all personality traits are necessarily 
pleasant or co-operative; robot designers often introduce 
‘negative’ traits to liven-up the somewhat dull process of 
personality encoding or introduce negative traits to re ﬂ ect 
the robot’s function.
d66 Trait

12 Agreeable

25 Compliant

Where two or more traits are contradictory, either roll 1d6 to 
see which trait is dominant (the higher the result, the more 
dominant the trait) or re-roll one of the traits.
Programming
Robots are programmed with a set of skills enabling them to 
do their designated job within their function. Every robot is 
programmed with at least the following:
• A primary skill at level 2
• A secondary skill at level 1
• A tertiary skill at level 0.

===PAGE 238===
 Robots
237
The primary skill always re ﬂ ects the core function of the 
robot. For instance, a Butler robot comes with Steward 2 
as standard and may have Diplomat at 1 and Broker at 0. 
Choose the skills to ﬁ t the robot’s function.
Every robot then comes with an additional 1d6–2 skills (treat 
a result of –1 as 0) skills. One of these skills will be at level 1 
and the remainder at level 0. Again, these skills ﬁ t the robot’s 
general function but need not re ﬂ ect the speci ﬁ c role it is 
designed for. Again, for instance, a Butler robot may come 
with Advocate 1, Drive 0 and Medic 0.
If, for game purposes, a robot needs one of its primary, 
secondary or tertiary skills to be higher than those given for 
standard robots, Referees can increase them, but no robot 
may exceed 4 in a primary skill, 2 in a secondary skill or 2 on 
a tertiary skill. As a general rule, robots with raised skills in 
these areas will have no additional skills.
Referees are encouraged to create skills not found in 
the Traveller Core Rulebook  to reﬂ  ect a robot’s 
functional programming. For example, a Robo-Chef may 
have programmes for Cooking and Chopping; neither are 
standard Traveller skills but the robot can be reasonably 
assumed to have these routines programmed as part of its 
functionality.
Armour and Weapons
Any robot can be armoured and indeed combat models generally 
are. The amount of ‘natural’ armour a robot comes with depends 
on its size; additional armour depends on its function.
Size

VS 0
S1
M1
L2
VL 3
XL 4
Add the armour modi ﬁ er for the robot’s sub-category to the 
armour for its size to ﬁ nd its overall armour protection.
Weapons
Only combat robots come equipped with weapons although 
industrial robots will have will tools that can be used 
offensively (witness Call-Me-Kenneth and its fearsome 
carpentry drills and saws).
Combat robots can carry a weapon in each hand or have 
a weapon attached to each arm, if humanoid or humanoid-
hybrid in conﬁ g. In addition, a combat robot can have further 
weapon mounts – integrated hard points – to which weapons 
can be added. The number of weapon mounts depends on 
the combat robot’s size:
Size Weapon Mounts Basic Fire Control 
Program
T1 0
VS 1 0
S1 0
M2 1
L2 2
VL 3 3
XL 4 4
Any of the weapons found in the Equipment chapter can 
be ﬁ tted to a weapon mount, although the size of the weapon 
is obviously a factor.
One weapon mount can accommodate one weapon of up 
to 6kg in mass. If a weapon of a larger mass is required, 
then multiple weapon mounts, if available, must be used. 
For example, a war droid with 2 weapon mounts could carry 
an integrated missile launcher (8kg) but the launcher would 
occupy both weapon mounts.
In combat, the number of weapons a combat robot can ﬁ re 
simultaneously depends on its programming. Fire Control/0 
allows the robot to ﬁ re one weapon at a time at its Gun Combat 
skill rating. Any additional weapons ﬁ red simultaneously will 
be at a –3 untrained skill modi ﬁ er. Fire Control/1 allows an

additional weapon to be used at the Gun Combat skill rating; 
Fire Control/2 allows 2 additional weapons and so on. The 
Basic Fire Control Program of the table above shows the 
level of Fire Control program combat robots come equipped 
with, again based on size.
Improvised Weapons
Robots that are not equipped with weapons but have tools 
(and this includes metal limbs capable of being used as 
clubs) integrated as part of their function, can use these 
items as Improvised Weapons if their safety programming 
is overcome to allow them to act offensively. Attacks are 
always at a –3 untrained skill modi ﬁ er, unless the robot can 
use a particular skill related to its function to in ﬂ ict damage 
and the target is in a position where the skill can be used 
effectively. For instance, a damaged Robo-Chef is equipped 
with a meat cleaver on one of its arms. If it is ﬂ ailing around 
and striking out randomly, its attacks are at –3. However, if it 
manages to come across a human who is prone and unable 
to do more than struggle (like a piece of meat, waiting to be 
butchered), it can use its Chopping 1 skill to make an attack. 
Likewise, a Call-Me-Kenneth carpentry model could use its 
Woodworking program to employ its saws, drills, chisels and 
so on, against similarly helpless opponents.
Customising 
Robots
Whilst robots rarely excite the same passion in citizens as 
their vehicles do, many still believe it worthwhile to modify a 
robot beyond its manufacturer’s speciﬁ cations in an effort to 
increase its capabilities. Such customisation therefore tends to 
be very practical in nature. Perps are often very keen to modify 
their robots to engage in a variety of illegal activities they are 
unwilling to participate in themselves. The ﬁ tting of weapons 
and additional armour is common, though most perps will seek 
to get their hands on the valuable lawbreaker slug that will 
allow their robots to ignore the value of human life, potentially 
turning them into lethal battle machines. All modi ﬁ cations 
listed require a full day’s work, a mechanical toolkit and either 
an Engineer (electronics), Engineer (robotics) or Mechanic 
roll of 8+ to successfully complete. If the skill roll is failed, 
another attempt may be made on another day. Alternatively, 
a character may take the robot to a repair centre to have the 
modiﬁ cation performed automatically. However, this will take 
1d6 days and increase the cost of each modiﬁ cation by 50%. 
With the exception of the memory module and lawbreaker 
module, these modi ﬁ cations may only be purchased for 
robots, not computers.
Armour
A robot’s armour can be increased by 1 at a cost of Cr. 1,000. 
To increase a robot’s original armour by 2 costs Cr. 2,000, 
3 points will cost Cr. 4,000 and so on, doubling each time. 
Small and Medium robots may not have their armour score 
increased by more than 3  points in total. The maximum 
armour score a robot may have is 15.
Additional and Concealed 
Weapon Mounts
Any robot with manipulating hands may carry and use a 
weapon, just as any human can. However, some citizens 
desire to have weaponry permanently mounted on their 
robot and so ﬁ t weapons that spring up from concealed 
hatches when needed. A weapon mount costs Cr. 2,000 
for every 6kg of mass it can accommodate. A concealed 
weapon mount costs Cr. 5,000.
Memory Module
Capacity to store new programs is always at a premium in a 
robot’s computerized brain, particularly if it has been running 
for some time and is already full of necessary information. 
Additional memory modules are often purchased to expand a 
robot’s memory beyond its original speci ﬁ cations, allowing it 
a far greater ﬂ exibility. A memory module costs Cr. 2,500 and 
grants a robot an additional level in the programmed skill.
Lawbreaker Module
Highly illegal, the lawbreaker module commands an 
incredibly high price on the black market as ﬁ tting one into 
a robot’s computerised brain will allow it to break any law of 
Mega-City One, including attacking a human. The lawbreaker 
module effectively provides and +8 DM to the robot’s Will 
characteristic. Lawbreaker modules cost Cr. 2,500 although 
permits are almost impossible to get hold of from the Justice 
Department. On the black market, a lawbreaker module 
costs Cr. 4,000. Fitting a lawbreaker module also requires 
an electronic toolkit.
Synthi-Skin
Medium sized Humanoid con ﬁ g robots can be coated with 
Synthi-Skin, a ﬂ esh-like coating giving the robot an almost 
completely human appearance. At a casual glance or from 
a distance, a Synthi-Skinned robot looks like any other 
citizen, although on a close and detailed inspection the lack 
of imperfections in the skin betray the robot’s real nature. A 
Synthi-Skin coating requires special machinery to apply, which 
itself costs Cr. 500,000. Taking a robot to a specialists who 
will apply the coating costs Cr. 20,000. Justice Department 

===PAGE 240===
 Robots
239
licenses are necessary to have a robot legally covered in this 
way and a license costs Cr. 5,000 and permits up to three 
robots to be covered. Illegally skinning a robot or possessing 
a ‘skin-job’, earns up to 5 years in the cubes.
Sample 
Robots
Assassin Robot, Hondai War 
Systems KTT12
Highly illegal in Mega-City One, Hondai War Systems’ 
imported assassin robots have been linked to a number of 
mobster slayings and, despite the Justice Department’s best 
efforts, their use seems to be on the increase. The KTT12 has 
one of the most advanced brains ever developed but its great 
technology is channelled into just one thing: combat. Assassin 
robots are lethal killers, capable of gaining entry into almost 
any fortress and destroying all organised resistance. A single 
assassin robot has been known to take multiple blasts from 
heavy weapons and still reach its target fully operational. Even 
a well-trained Judge will prove no match for the KTT12 once it 
has been unleashed. The cost of this robot is all but prohibitive 
to even the wealthiest of perps and racketeers but it is certainly 
the weapon of choice when a slaying must be certain.
Specialised War Droid
Size: M; Conﬁ g: Humanoid
Str 12 (+2), Dex 12 (+2), End 10 (+1), Will 6 (+8 Lawbreaker 
Module)
Weapons: Any Assigned
Armour: 7
Programming: Melee (unarmed combat) 2, Gun Combat (all 
weapon types) 3, Tactics 1, Survival 0
Modiﬁ cations: Lawbreaker Module
Personality Traits: Ruthless, Cruel
Construction Mek, British 
LeyMek Block-Smasher
It is an urban myth that a team of demolition droids can smash 
an entire dilapidated block apart within a single day, a truism 
British LeyMek took to heart when creating their new model. 
The Block-Smasher stands almost 20 metres tall, towering 
over citizens as it sets to work. Because of their awe-inspiring 
size, the vast majority of Block-Smashers are programmed 
with a cheery personality to better calm nervous citizens 
wary of their huge, crushing demolition tools. Their tiny 
computerised brains seem all but inadequate for the robots’ 
huge size though British LeyMek assures its customers that 
not one Block-Smasher has ever gone rogue.

General Purpose Robot, Hondai 
Tee-Kay Mk II
There are millions of Hondai Tee-Kay general purpose 
robots to be found within Mega-City One, though the Mk 
II has proven to be the most popular model by far. Pre-
programmed with a range of generic functions whilst still 
allowing space for further customisation, the Mk II sees 
service throughout the city, from the depths of the sewer 
systems to the peak of the highest blocks. Flexible enough 
to perform almost any task a human can, the Mk II is readily 
adaptable to a multitude of roles.
Service/Labour Robot
Size: M; Conﬁ g: Humanoid
Str 8 (+0), Dex 10 (+1), End 10 (+1), Will 6 (+0)

Robodoc, Moderna Robots Mk 
IX
The standard medical robot of the Justice Department, the 
Mk IX can be found in almost every ambulance and hospital 
across Mega-City One. The Mk IX is capable of treating almost 
any patient on its own initiative, from delivering critical ﬁ rst aid 
to performing major surgery within a speeding ambulance. 
Once in the care of a robodoc, an injured citizen can be almost 
sure he will survive whatever injuries he is suffering. Many 
hospitals enhance the software packages of their robodocs in 
order to grant them the ability to conduct advanced research 
and experiments, thus furthering the cause of medicine without 
having to waste valuable human doctors.
Specialised Medical Robot
Size: M; Conﬁ g: Humanoid (4 arms)

Weapons: Robodocs carry a variety of medical tools that can 
become improvised weapons: syringes, bone-saws, scalpels, 
drills and so forth. Each super- ﬂ ex arm can be ﬁ tted with a 
different implement to allow the robodoc to perform several 
different physical operations simultaneously.

Robodog, Lethal Response Inc. 
Defender
A citizen’s city-block apartment is his fortress and many 
spend countless credits defending their property. The use 
of robodogs comes under regular annual review from the 
Justice Department but so far they remain legal so long as 
they are only ever activated within a citizen’s own property. 
Programmed with a variety personalities, robodogs can be 
loyal and loving pets to their owners but instantly turn into 
savage killing machines should the owner be attacked. As 
such, many would-be burglars have met a very grisly end at 
the teeth of a well-maintained robodog.

Servo-Droid, Moderna Robots 
Silver Service 14
Not all citizens want, or can afford, the versatility offered by 
most general purpose robots and are simply looking for a 
device that can help keep their apartments clean, prepare 
food and give a cheerful greeting in the morning The Moderna 
Robots Silver Service 14 is typical of the millions of servo-
droids found throughout Mega-City One Wherever a general 
purpose robot would prove to be ludicrously expensive, 
servo-droids are usually found and many citizens possess 
their own.
Service/Labour Robot
Size: M; Conﬁ g: Humanoid
Str 6 (+0), Dex 8 (+0), End 10 (+1), Will 2 (–2)
Weapons: None but if capable of an attack, robot arms inﬂ ict 
Unarmed Attack damage of 1d6+1
Armour: 2
Programming: Various but typically: Steward 1, Admin 0
Modiﬁ cations: None.
Personality Traits: Obedient, Sycophantic, Caring
Rossum Systems Combat Droid, 
MkVI
Used extensively in the Atomic War, these hybrid, tracked, 
combat droids are designed for rapid movement over hostile 
terrain and for delivering varied death upon an enemy. 
Fashioned into the likeness of old American GI troops (but 
with tracks, clearly), their faces are grotesque parodies of 
the human form, to match their combat programming. Illegal 
in Mega-City One, rogue Combat Droids of the MkVI variety 
are sometimes found in isolated regions of the Cursed Earth 
where they still believe the war rages on and humans (of any 
kind) are the enemy.
Standard War Droid

Mega-City One 
 Timeline
2023
The Volgan War between east and west ends. America, with 
a population approaching a billion people, begins the Mega-
City programme, with the ﬁ rst city, Mega-City One, focused 
on the Boston/Washington/New York/Atlanta axis. In time, 
the city will sweep the eastern seaboard from the Canadian 
border at Niagara down to Miami in Florida.
2031-2039
Mega-City One nears completion. Mega-City Two is already 
under development, with plans in place for Texas City. Each 
city is designed to hold around 100 million people; by 2039 
the populations have already exceeded these limits.
2040-2047
Mega-City One is deemed ﬁ nished in 2047. Mega-Cities 
Two and Three begin development. It is becoming clear, 
within months of Mega-City One’s completion, that traditional 
A Note on the Timeline
Judge Dredd’s story is extensive and sprawling 
– just like Mega-City One itself. Time travel, 
made possible by the Proteus device developed 
by Tek Division, allowed Dredd and Anderson to 
visit the city in the year 2120. This established a 
parallel timeline that makes the standard linear 
timeline convoluted in terms of explanation. 
Other events in both Dredd’s career and Mega-
City One’s history occur in parallel with the 
timeline presented here (notably The Dead Man 
story, heralding the epic Necropolis story) and 
these are left undocumented, mainly for the 
sake of simplicity.
The best reference for charting Mega-City One’s 
history is, of course, 2000 AD or the Complete 
Case Files. Judge Dredd Players and Referees 
are highly recommended to read these stories 
for themselves. The timeline give in this 
chapter offers a very, very brief account of 
the Dredd saga’s highlights.
policing and judicial methods are inadequate for the 
burgeoning population. Gangs, centred on the lower levels 
of Mega-City One and based around the old geographical 
city areas, begin to form, challenging both the police and 
government.
2051
Chief Judge Fargo attempts suicide. Solomon reluctantly 
takes over as Chief Judge. Judge Judd outlines his ideas 
for cloning.
2058
Solomon steps down, in favour of Chief Judge Clarence 
Goodman and returns to the streets where he is happiest.
2066
A new process of accelerated cloning, coupled with Genetic 
Control screening for potential Judge material, means that 
candidates are able to become full Judges in record time. 
The ﬁ rst experiments in these cloning techniques result in 
the creation of Joe and Rico Dredd. The two clones emerge 
from the vats at roughly the age of ﬁ ve and fully prepared for 
the tough training of the Judge programme.
2070
Caught in a maze of corruption, smears, diplomatic wrangling 
and political assassination, President Robert L Booth of the 
USA starts the Atomic Wars, which rapidly devastate most of 
the USA, Russia and Europe. The Mega-Cities escape direct 
nuclear attack thanks to the experimental laser defence 
systems installed just a year earlier. The Judges, already 
prepared for Booth’s treachery, have stockpiled arms and 
take the lead in the resistance against the President’s tyrant. 
Booth maintains control over America’s robotic army and a 
bloody civil war begins between the Judges and government 
troops. Cadet Judges Dredd and Rico are drafted into the 
active Justice Department forces and distinguish themselves 
in combat. The citizens petition the Judges to take over the 
running of the country.
2071
Most of America is in ruins: the lands between the Mega Cities 
turned into the scorched, irradiated wasteland known as the 
Cursed Earth. Booth’s Presidential Guard is ﬁ nally defeated 
at the Battle of Armageddon but only at the cost of the lives 
of a hundred thousand Judges and mek-troops. Judge 
Solomon, acting in his temporary position as Grand Judge 
for all three Mega-Cities, makes the famous ‘Judgement of

===PAGE 245===

244
Solomon’ speech, stating that the deposed Booth should be 
put into suspended animation and entombed in Fort Knox for 
one hundred years.
2072
A steady in ﬂ ow of homeless refugees from the ruins of the 
Cursed Earth swells the post-war populations of the Mega-
Cities; Mega-City One swells to 550 million; Mega-City Two 
to 350 million; and Mega-City Three to 260 million. Increasing 
numbers of city blocks are built but demand outstrips the 
supply and many citizens form shantytowns outside the 
walls of the Mega Cities. Improved construction techniques 
and new synthetic materials ease congestion somewhat 
but space in the Mega Cities is still at a premium and so 
draconian immigration conditions are imposed on city entry. 
Millions are condemned to return to the Cursed Earth.
2079
Joe and Rico Dredd graduate with honours to become full 
Judges. Judge Morph grants Joe Dredd his Full Eagle, 
proclaiming him the most talented and capable cadet he has 
ever graduated. A few months after leaving the Academy, 
Rico succumbs to corruption leading to his arrest by his 
own clone brother. Dredd arrests Rico for the various 
crimes including accepting bribes, vice and murder. Rico 
is sentenced to twenty years on the penal colony of Titan. 
He fathers a daughter when he engages in a relationship 
with a journalist visiting the colony. For a time after, Dredd 
offers emotional support for his niece but eventually comes 
to realise that his life as a Judge is incompatible with that 
as a father ﬁ gure and he turns his back on Rico’s daughter, 
dedicating himself wholeheartedly to the law.
2083-2086
Following years of wrangling over the questions of 
independence and government, civil war breaks out between 
Mega Cities One and Three. Mega-City Three asserts it is 
being disenfranchised by the other Mega Cities and that its 
individual identity is being subsumed. Mega Cities One and 
Two oppose the idea of an independent Mega-City Three in 
the interests of unity; however – and more importantly – the 
valuable resources controlled by Mega-City Three would be 
placed at risk.
 The war ends in stalemate and Mega-City 
One allows Mega-City Three to declare its independence. 
Mega-City Three adopts the name Texas City; Mega Cities 
One and Two develop closer links and form ‘The Union’.
2088
Luna-1 is established on the Moon in a project shared by all 
three Mega-Cities. Although it is hailed as a great scienti ﬁ c 
achievement, it is seen by many as a diplomatic exercise, 
designed to renew and cement relations with Texas City. 
Government of the colony is divided initially between all 
three cities, with a senior Judge being sent every six months 
to preside as Chief Judge.
2088-2099
Dredd cements his own reputation as Mega-City One’s 
foremost lawman.
2099
William “Whitey” Logan murders Judge Alvin for kicks 
while holed up in the old Empire State Building. Whitey is 
apprehended by Dredd and sentenced to life on the ‘Devil’s 
Island’ hi-speed Mega Circular prison.
In the same year the gigantic Statue of Judgement honouring 
the Judges, is unveiled. Its stands next to, but dwarfs, 
the Statue of Liberty. The city’s population has risen to a 
staggering 620 million in the city blocks and in other, static 
habitats and an estimated 18 million perpetually driving the 
highways of the city in mopads. 
The robot, Call-me-Kenneth, malfunctions and triggers 
the First Robot War. Dredd leads the ﬁ ght-back against 
the mechanical revolutionaries and eventually destroys 
Call-Me-Kenneth with the help of ﬁ fth columnist robots 
opposed to Kenneth’s tyrannical ways. Anti-robot lobbies 
follow in the wake of the war but radical Justice Department 
reforms ensure that robots cannot rebel against their human 
masters.
Rico Dredd returns to Mega-City One, having completed his 
20-year stretch on Titan, and is killed by Joe Dredd while 
attempting to take his revenge.
2100
The Mega-City 5000 bike race ends in Spikes Harvey Rotten 
failing to make the ﬁ nishing post after Dredd intervenes. He 
is sentenced to life in the iso-cubes for a variety of crimes.
Mega-City Two falls prey to the deadly viral disease, 
2T(FRU)T, which threatens the very existence of the city, 
turning those af ﬂ icted into insatiable cannibals. Dredd is 
given the task of getting the vaccine to Mega-City Two via 
an overland mission. Knowing he will need someone with 
outstanding bike skills, Spikes Harvey Rotten is released 
from custody and accompanies the Cursed Earth expedition. 
Spikes is killed as Dredd approaches Mega-City Two and 
falls-foul of old but vengeful war-droids led by the murderous 
General Blood n Nuts. Dredd ﬁ nally reaches the Mega-City 
with the vaccine and arranges for Tweak, an intelligent and 
peaceful rock-eating alien, to be returned to his home planet, 
keeping the secrets of Tweak’s world (an abundance of 
valuable precious metals and ores) from humans.

2101
Dredd returns to Mega-City One from his Cursed Earth 
mission to ﬁ nd that, in his absence, Deputy Chief Judge 
Cal’s power and in ﬂ uence with the SJS has increased 
dramatically. A robot look-alike is used to try and frame 
Dredd for a variety of crimes, including murder, and Dredd 
is sentenced to Titan like Rico before him. He manages to 
escape but is powerless to prevent Chief Judge Goodman 
from being murdered on the orders of Judge Cal, who takes 
control of Mega-City for a one hundred day reign of terror. 
Judges Grif ﬁ n and Dredd lead the resistance movement 
against Cal, employing effective guerrilla tactics to strike 
at the feared SJS and Cal’s feared alien mercenaries, the 
Kleggs. Cal, spiralling into greater acts of criminal insanity, 
plunges to his death before he can execute the entire 
city, taken-out by the semi-troglodyte simpleton, Fergee, 
a denizen of the city-bottom sewer slums close to the Big 
Smelly River.
2102
Judge Death arrives in Mega-City One, having crossed 
the boundaries separating his dimension from our own 
– a world where life itself has been declared a crime. Judge 
Death has come to judge Mega-City One and he slaughters 
dozens of citizens before his corporeal body is destroyed by 
Dredd. When the creature possesses Psi Judge Cassandra 
Anderson, she sacri ﬁ ces herself by allowing herself to be 
sealed in Boing®, trapping Death’s spirit within her mind.
In the same year as Death’s appearance, Psi Judge 
Feyy predicts doom for Mega-City One in 2120 
unless the Judge Child, destined to be the Chief 
Judge, can be found. Dredd leads the search, 
accompanied by Judges Larter, Lopez and 
Hershey. The quest takes them to Texas City 
and the ﬁ  rst run-in with the murderous Angel 
Gang, before they are forced to follow the ﬂ eeing 
Angels, who have abducted the Judge Child, across the 
galaxy. Judge Lopez dies following the taking of Oracle 
Spice in a bid to locate the Judge Child but Dredd tracks the 
Angel Gang and Judge Child to the planet Xanadu where 
the Angels are killed. Dredd understands that the Judge 
Child, Owen Krysler, has manipulated a trail of murder and 
mayhem across the galaxy and so he leaves the boy to his 
fate, returning to Mega-City One alone.
2103
Judge Death is freed by his compatriots; Judges Fear, Fire 
and Mortis. When the Dark Judges seal off Billy Carter 
Block and begin murdering its population, Judge Dredd and 
the revived Judge Anderson enter the block and force the 
indestructible beings to retreat across the dimensional span

2104
A series of Block Wars accelerate into Block Mania, under 
the inﬂ uence of Orlok the Assassin, a Sov Bloc agent who 
has contaminated Mega-City One’s water supply to induce 
the citywide Block Wars. The Mega-City erupts into chaos,

succumbing to the condition. An antidote is developed but it 
is now too late. East-Meg One’s plan to destroy Mega-City 
One is in full spin and Mega-City One is almost powerless 
to resist.
Supreme Judge Bulgarin of East-Meg One launches an all-
out nuclear strike heralding the Apocalypse War. Prolonged 
ﬁ ghting devastates both the Mega-City One and Sov Bloc 
sides but a daring raid into Sov territory results in the 
obliteration of East-Meg One by its own missiles and the 
invaders ﬁ nally surrender. Chief Judge Grif ﬁ n has been 
killed in the war, along with over four hundred million other 
citizens and Judges. Most of Mega-City One is in ruins and 
millions are homeless, setting up more makeshift shanty 
towns in and outside the city walls. Judge McGruder, head 
of the SJS, takes over as Chief Judge.
2105
The city is gradually rebuilt, bringing some much-needed 
employment to Mega-City One citizens but many parts still 
lie in irradiated ruin.
2106
Tek Division produces the Proteus, a prototype time 
machine. The machine is used by Judges Anderson and 
Dredd to travel forward to 2120, in order to discover the truth 
of Judge Feyy’s ﬁ nal prediction. In 2120 they discover the 
ruined Mega-City, ruled over by the Mutant, a vastly powerful 
psionic being created from the cloned tissue of Owen Krysler, 
the Judge Child. Dredd loses his eyes in combat and battles 
his undead future self. The Judges return to 2106 with the 
undead version of Dredd, and Chief Judge McGruder orders 
the termination of the Grunwalder – the robot leader of the 
Xanadu enclave where Dredd had left the Judge Child, and 
the destruction of his castle in an attempt to prevent the 
calamity from taking place. The inactive undead Dredd is 
placed in the Justice Department’s Black Museum and Judge 
Dredd is ﬁ tted with bionic eyes (an operation he grudgingly 
admits he should have had done years before).
2107
Judge Anderson is lured to Deadworld and duped into 
reviving the Dark Judges, who subsequently leave her for 
dead when they return to Mega-City One. The Dark Judges 
use their Dimension Jump technology to teleport across 
the city, always one step ahead of the Judges and killing 
thousands in the process. Eventually Psi-Division manages 
to predict their arrival point and the Dark Judges are trapped 
in limbo through the use of a prototype Justice Department 
Dimension Jump device.
2108
Shujan, the Warlord of Ji, a powerful oriental psion, comes to 
Mega-City One and raises the Seven Samurai – immensely 
powerful demonic entities that threaten the very security of 
the city. Shujan and the samurai are destroyed through the 
efforts of Psi-Division but the situation would not have arisen 
if Chief Judge McGruder had not been so cautious in her 
handling of the Warlord in the ﬁ rst place. McGruder resigns 
as Chief Judge and takes the Long Walk. Judge Silver 
assumes Chief Judge tenure. The Democratic movement 
in the city gains momentum, prompted by the death, at the 
hands of the Judges, of pro-democratic lobbyists.
Democratic Tendency takes over Channel 48 breakfast 
show. Mega-City One housewife, Hester Hyman, makes 
an impassioned plea to the people of the city to challenge, 
peacefully, the authoritarianism of the Judges. The members 
of Democratic Tendency are killed in a showdown with 
Judges but the seeds of a democratic uprising are sown, 
especially in the mind of Hester’s peaceful, law-abiding 
husband, Gort.
2109
A colony war on the fringes of the galaxy results in 8 million 
pints of blood being required for the human casualties. 
Mega-City One citizens face a compulsory blood donation 
programme. Rumours spread that Justice Department

needle-phobic citizen manages to waste over 3,000 pints of 
donated blood when he panics and crashes a hov-car into 
the containers loading donated blood into a shuttle transit.
The democratic movement arises again with new leaders. 
Peaceful protests are called against Justice Department and

to limit the demonstration’s impact but still 16 million citizens

2111
Kraken, last of the Judda clones in Mega-City One, is failed by 
Dredd on his Full Eagle Day despite a ﬂ awless performance. 
Kraken is sentenced to be terminated and administers his

almost a year, during which he is assaulted by the Sisters 
of Death; Nausea and Phobia. Dredd suffers from amnesia 
during this ordeal but ﬁ nally recovers his memory and returns

2112
Chief Judge Silver makes Kraken a Judge and uses him to 
replace Dredd, who is wandering the Cursed Earth. Under 
the inﬂ uence of the Sisters of Death, Kraken kidnaps Psi 
Judge Agee and the Sisters use her mind to form a bridge 
to Mega-City One from their own dimension, Deadworld, the 
home of the Dark Judges.
Kraken is forced to free the Dark Judges from dimensional 
limbo and they bring hell to Mega-City One, turning it into the 
Necropolis. Despite the efforts of Dredd and other Judges, 
over sixty million citizens are killed before Agee’s own death 
breaks the bridge between worlds and three of the Dark 
Judges are re-imprisoned in Boing®. Judge Death, however, 
remains free. 
The Necropolis crisis results in Chief Judge Silver’s death 
and McGruder, having returned from the Cursed Earth, re-
assumes the ofﬁ ce.
2113
The Democratic Referendum, established in the wake of 
Necropolis, is sabotaged by conspirators who also want 
to murder Dredd. Judges Dredd and Dekker round up 
conspirators and discover they include Judges Grice and 
DeGaulle. Dredd’s campaign exposes the weaknesses of 
the Democratic Referendum and Mega-City One’s citizens 
overwhelmingly re-elect Justice Department to govern the 
City.
Later that year, Chief Judge Silver, thought to be dead, returns 
in a zombie-like form, explaining that Judge Death had left 
him alive for regular torture. He demands that McGruder 
relinquish control of Justice Department to him but Dredd 
resolves the issue by executing Silver for promoting Kraken 
and giving the Dark Judges a foothold via the Necropolis 
incident.
2114
Sabbat the Necromagus arrives from the year 2178 and sets 
about reanimating the dead all over the world. Billions die 
as several Mega-Cities are completely overrun by zombies 
and the only option is to use nuclear weapons to prevent the 
undead from increasing in strength. Aided by Johnny Alpha, 
a mutant bounty hunter from Sabbat’s time, an elite squad of 
Judges led by Dredd ends the conﬂ ict. 
2115
Chief Judge McGruder instigates the Mechanismo project 
to supplement the dwindling ranks of the Street Judges. 
The project is conducted under extreme secrecy by Tek 
Judge Stich and results in the Mechanismos – robot Judges 
invested with programming that mirrors that of their human 
counterparts. When two units (5 and 8) malfunction and 
run murderously amok, Dredd confronts McGruder and the 
project is put into abeyance – but not abandoned. McGruder 
continues with the project. Unit 5, which has not been 
destroyed, reactivates and escapes into the Mega-City One 
sewers where it remains at liberty, emerging periodically to

Dredd becomes Sector Chief of the notorious Sector 301, a

2121
Nero Narcos commences his Doomsday Scenario with the

Meanwhile, Mega-City One battles against the Assassinator

control of the city’s nuclear weapons. Narcos’s Doomsday

Dredd regains control of Mega-City One.
In the wake of the Doomsday Scenario, Chief Judge Volt 
takes his own life. Deputy Chief Judge Hershey becomes 
the new Chief Judge.
2122
A Dredd-clone called Rico (named after Dredd’s late clone-
brother) requests transfer from Texas City to Mega-City One.

2123
A version of Judge Cal from a parallel dimension leads an army

Running 
Judge Dredd 
Campaigns
The Judge Dredd stories, as told in 2000 AD and its spin-off 
publications, offer excellent templates for crafting rewarding 
Judge Dredd scenarios. The stories can be categorised into 
several types:
One-Off Crimes
Many stories focus on a single crime: from it being committed, 
through its investigation and concluding in the sentencing of 
the perp. The aim of these stories is to show how justice is 
enforced in Mega-City One and how each type of crime has 
an impact on the citizens and the perp himself. Crimes range 
from the mundane (such as littering or speeding) through to 
the truly bizarre, such as bodysnatching or Stookie Glanding. 
Each story is framed differently but the components are 
essentially the same:
• The perp’s motivation for the crime
• The committing of the crime
• The crime’s effects
• The Judge’s involvement
• Resolution and Sentencing
Many crimes are opportunistic, rather than being executed 
according to a plan and although the crimes may be bizarre, 
they tend to focus less on the fantastic elements found in 
Judge Dredd stories and more on the motivation and effects 
on citizens and the city. Not only do these stories serve to 
illustrate a crime, they serve to offer an insight into the mind 
of the perp and how a Judge has to handle the situation.
Such stories are effectively One-Shots – single, self-
contained adventures that can be resolved in one (or two, at 
the most) session of game play. The crime is not elaborate 
or extensive but follows a straight-forward plan:
• The crime is committed
• The Judge investigates
• A chase or stand-off ensues
• Justice is dispensed
This kind of story is an excellent way for Referees to introduce 
the myriad different crimes committed in Mega-City One to 
new Players and, especially, those Players who have little 
2126
The Total War Terrorist group triggers multiple nuclear bombs, 
resulting in the deaths of four million people, in a bid to force 
the Judges out of power. The attempt is counter-productive, 
as the public is appalled at the enormous loss of life which 
was supposedly caused for their bene ﬁ t. Any remaining 
mainstream support for democracy dwindles still further.
2127
Following Total War’s terrorist outrages, hundreds of 
thousands of citizens have been left homeless and live in 
overcrowded, crime-ridden emergency camps. The Half-
Life plague, spread by a treacherous Psi Judge, results in a 
wave of murderous violence that eventually kills one million 
people. The Half-Life plague allows the Sisters of Death to 
launch another attack, with entire blocks being wiped out by 
plagues and poltergeist activity.
2128
Ciudad Barranquilla is in the grip of civil war and Mega-City 
One leads a peacekeeping mission. However this proves to 
be a regime-change ruse, designed to install a new Supreme 
Judge who is under the effective control of the Mega-City 
One Justice Department. 
2129
A dimensional portal opens to the parallel world of Kaluza; 
a dimension composed of dark-matter. The portal allows 
demonic creatures to invade the city, slaughtering whole 
sectors and causing a crime-increasing psychic backlash. 
The assault is halted only when Judges invade Kaluza and 
shut the portal down.
In the same year. thirty city-blocks designed by Findhorn 
Gask reveal themselves to be giant robots, which, once 
activated, rampage through the city, before being convinced 
to stop and turned into a satellite city. 
Also in 2129 the Council of Five rejects Dredd’s proposal 
to relax the mutant ban. Dredd responds with a massive 
crackdown on the city’s Mutant Farm facilities. 
2130
Following more pressure from Dredd, the mutant laws are 
reviewed again and repealed, out of fear that Dredd will resign. 
The change in the law results in widespread rioting by citizens, 
followed by frequent hate crimes against mutant migrants.
2131
The current year for Judge Dredd campaigns. Senior 
Judges, against the mutant rights laws, force a vote to 
replace Hershey as Chief Judge in the hope of undoing 

===PAGE 252===
 Macs Database
251
experience of the Judge Dredd stories and setting. They give the characters excellent exposure to the foibles of perps, a decent 
amount of action and the chance to exercise essential skills and, because Mega-City One is inevitably a violent place, a combat 
of some form.
If you are a Referee new to Judge Dredd, or your Players are new to the setting, it is recommended that the ﬁ rst few game 
sessions take this kind of One-Shot format. It helps to cement in place both the character of the city and the role of the Judges 
without large amounts of background or detail being necessary. The crime in question is the focus; how the Judges solve it and 
deal with the perp being the challenge. As the Players (and you, as Referee) become more familiar with the setting, these One-
Shots do not need to be so frequent, although a good sprinkling of them throughout a campaign reﬂ ects the fact that Judges are 
required to do a daily job of work and that petty crime happens every minute of every day – and has to be dealt with.
Mega-City One Snapshots
Mega-City One snapshots are not necessarily crimes: frequently they are the aftermath of a crime or a focus on the victims of a 
crime. Quite often, a crime results from a law-abiding pursuit and the aim of the story is to illustrate the lives of Mega-City One’s 
citizens and bring to life the tediousness of their existence, against the backdrop of the enormity of the city.
Judges do not need to investigate a crime and arrest a perp but 
they do need to interact with citizens, understand the nature of 
the incident they are involved with and, perhaps, pass some 
form of judgment or instigate some form of help. Dredd, for 
all his stony-faced dedication to the law, frequently involves 
himself in such stories – be they a child’s brain that needs 
implanting into a robot body so that she and her parents 
can enjoy a family life; or simply interviewing the victims 
of a crime to better understand the motives behind it.
The Player Character Judges should be involved 
in these kinds of scenarios too. They do not 
necessarily involve gunﬁ ghts, chases or on-the-
spot sentencing but they do engage Judges 
with the innocent, law abiding public allowing 
them to gain some sympathy with them 
and increase their awareness of the 
consequences of both a crime and 
a Judge’s actions on the general 
public.
Many of these snapshot stories 
are told through the eyes of the 
citizens, rather than from the 
perspective of the Judges, 
which can be hard to achieve 
in a roleplaying game and 
is not necessarily effective. 
However, these low-key, low-
action stories are a good way 
of breaking up the routine 
and allow both Referee 
and characters to become 
involved in a good story. 
Snapshots are very 
much like one-offs but 
can be more complex. 

===PAGE 253===
 Macs Database
252
The focus is on the people affected by the circumstances of 
the story and not necessarily on the crime. Snapshots allow 
Judges to see beyond the criminal tendencies of Mega-City 
One citizens and humanises the Judges (slightly). Snapshots 
can be good ways of introducing regular supporting 
characters or stories that can be used later, in more detailed 
story-arcs or epics, to give the Judges insight or simply to 
lend depth and colour to the campaign.
Snapshots should be used sparingly – perhaps as the 
antidote to a run of One-Shot crimes or in the aftermath of a 
detailed investigation or Epic Storyline. Used sparingly, they 
are effective tools for bringing depth to Judge Dredd games 
and can be especially effective if there are only one or two 
Players involved in the session.
On Patrol
As the name suggests, Judges are out and about – on foot 
or in the saddle – and simply dealing with whatever crimes 
and incidents are strewn in their path. On Patrol stories are 
short, sharp bursts of action allowing Judges to deal with a 
variety of petty crimes quickly or become involved in dealing 
with a major incident that has a simple and fast resolution.
They key to an effective On Patrol story is for the Referee 
to have a series of crimes ready-prepared that can dropped, 
in any order, into the Judges’ path. The Perps section in 
the Crazies, Fatties, Muties and Perps chapter 
offers a random way for determining crime and this can 
be an excellent tool for handling routine patrol scenarios. 
The essence is to have a variety of different crimes and to 
have each resolved quickly before the next is introduced. 
The pace should be fast – so no sooner has one perp been 
cuffed to a holding post than another perp is committing an 
offence across the street. If a series of crimes can be linked 
to a single, triggering incident, then this makes for even more 
fun as the Judges follow a paper-trail of incidents stemming 
from a single (and perhaps innocent) root cause. On Patrol 
scenarios are excellent for unplanned game sessions where 
the Players do not need a great amount of detail and simply 
want to engage in what is called ‘sandbox’ play – that is, just 
getting on with their jobs and exploring as a part of being a 
resident of a place. It helps to have some crimes structured 
before play begins but with experience Referees will learn 
how to produce crimes and handle them, on the ﬂ y.
On Patrol scenarios should therefore aim for diversity and 
fast resolution (so no convoluted gun battles), usually with 
a whimsical feel or heavy dose of irony to accompany the 
proceedings. The On Patrol stories found in the Judge Dredd 
saga are typically blackly humorous and illustrate both the 
absurdity of Mega-City One and the absurdity of the justice 
system. A good way on introducing light-relief and great for 
exercising the dispensation of instant justice.
Detailed Cases
Detailed case stories revolve around the in-depth study of 
a crime. From the Judges’ perspective they have no idea 
as to the perp and/or the motive and must use deduction, 
questioning, evidence and other investigational techniques 
to identify who committed he crime and why, then drive the 
case towards resolution.
Detailed case stories do not need to involve complex motives 
or bizarre crimes; indeed, many such cases in the Judge 
Dredd saga focus on straight-forward crimes committed 
for understandable, straight-forward motives. Examples of 
Detailed cases:
• Crimes of Passion
• Peeping
• Revenge
• Carefully planned heists and robberies
• Meticulously planned murders
• Missing Persons
• Blackmail/Extortion
• Fraud
But the list is perhaps endless. Any crime, with any motive, 
can form the basis of a detailed case.
A detailed case investigation should take several sessions of 
play to conclude and should include the following elements:
• Response to the initial crime report
• Dealing with witnesses, victims and the body (if one is 
present)
• Crime Scene Investigation, calling in Med, Tek and Psi 
support as needed
• Taking witness statements, with routine lie detector tests
• Interrogating potential suspects – sometimes discreetly; 
sometimes heavy-handed
• Following-up leads
• Using informers
• Identifying the suspect or shortlist of suspects
• Veriﬁ cation of the suspect(s)
• Making the arrest
• Sentencing
In most cases of this type, the perp has carefully planned the 
crime in advance. He need not be a criminal genius of the PJ 
Maybe variety but smart enough to know that forensics will 

===PAGE 254===
 Macs Database
253
provide valuable clues and acting suspiciously, and without 
solid alibis, will quickly lead to the presumption of guilt. 
Perps are therefore not necessarily opportunists; or if they 
are they have kept their cool and made as clean a getaway 
as possible, taking valuable evidence away with them or 
destroying it.
Judges need to employ a variety of techniques to solve 
a detailed case: investigation and attention to detail are 
watchwords. Witness statements need to be checked, 
rechecked and cross-referenced. Investigation takes time 
and may offer valuable clues or complete red herrings. 
Casual witnesses may need to be tracked-down and this can 
be time-consuming. A team of Judges can, of course, split 
their efforts but this can make it dif ﬁ cult for a Referee – and 
the group – to keep everyone’s interest: helpfully, Judges in 
the Judge Dredd stories are often shown working together 
frequently and as a team, so the Player Characters should 
be encouraged to follow that precedent.
The biggest challenge is for the Referee in creating a case 
that is complex, challenging but also rational in its structure. 
Detailed cases need careful preparation and the motives 
and experiences of Non-Player Characters established in 
advance and carry a mixture of both fact and irrelevance to 
ensure the Judges have to work for their results. Detailed 
cases are therefore harder to run and manage than the 
scenario types already discussed and will therefore be 
infrequent affairs but are nonetheless rewarding.
One thing to bear in mind is simplicity. Most detailed cases 
in Judge Dredd are not especially complex: detailed, yes but 
the twists and turns found in popular detective drama are 
curiously absent and this is a good thing. A simple case, with 
detailed and well-drawn characters, evidence and so forth, is 
far easier to manage than a complex, convoluted case with 
multiple red herrings, perpetrators and obscure motives. 
Complex cases are dif ﬁ cult to structure and errors easy 
to make. Keep the nature of the case straightforward and 
invest time in providing the detail. Fiendishly clever crimes 
are ﬁ ne of course but take far longer to structure and may 
even be difﬁ cult to present during the game session.
Epic Story-Arcs
Judge Dredd specialises in, and excels at, epic storylines. 
The Cursed Earth, Judge Cal, The Judge Child, Apocalypse 
War, Necropolis, The Pit and the Doomsday Scenario are 
examples of ambitious storytelling at its best. The themes 
in common:
• A city-wide or global threat
• One or more major, highly powerful villains with grand 
schemes in mind
• Recurring arch-enemies (The Dark Judges, Orlok, Cal 
and so forth)
• Treachery and corruption in high places
• Weaknesses of top-level judgment
• Incursion of advanced or supernatural forces
• Dire threats to the Justice Departments
• High body counts
• Destruction on a vast scale
• A rebellion or ﬁ ght-back against dreadful odds
• Ingenious solutions that save the day
• An aftermath that changes the way Mega-City One 
progresses
By their very nature, Epic scenarios take many sessions to 
complete – just as the stories in 2000 AD take weeks or 
months (usually months) to resolve. This means careful 
planning on the part of the Referee – but it also requires 
ambition. An epic storyline is the time to introduce as much 
bizarreness and adversity as possible; it is time for those 
grand gestures and uber-villains to be wheeled-out centre 
stage and your Judges be given a shot at the title.
However, these epic tales have a certain structure that is 
worth noting:
Slow-Burn Opening
With a few exceptions, most Judge Dredd epics begin with a 
slow-burn opening. An innocuous, routine case either builds 
into, or contributes to, the grand themes of the epic. The 
Apocalypse War, for example, begins with Block Mania. 
Necropolis builds with Dredd’s resignation and the Dead 
Man story spin-off. These slow-burn openings can be One-
Shots, Snapshots or Detailed Cases that, at ﬁ rst glance, are 
done and dusted once the arrest is made. However, events 
within those stories act as triggers for further events that 
build, gradually, into the theme of the epic story.
Slow Villain Reveal
The villains behind the epic are rarely revealed in the 
beginning. Their involvement develops over time. Orlok, for 
example, is not revealed as the Block Mania instigator for 
several episodes and War Marshall Kazan, the murderous 
leader of the Sov Bloc forces in the Apocalypse War, does 
not make an appearance until halfway into the story. Nero 
Narcos, in the Doomsday Scenario, appears towards the end 
of the story. It is therefore not necessary to have the major 

===PAGE 255===
 Macs Database
254
• Allow any one skill roll to be re-rolled – either the 
character’s or an opponent’s: however, only one re-
roll per situation is allowed and the Hero Point is spent 
regardless of the outcome.
• Bring a character back from the brink of death or 
suspend immediate death so the Judge can accomplish 
a drastic action (a Judge, peppered with automatic 
ﬁ re, manages to crawl towards the nuke and switch it 
off, even though, using the rules, he would have died 
metres before reaching it. The Judge expires heroically 
as the city is saved).
• Gain ONE particular plot insight, if the Referee deems it 
appropriate.
• Recall, remember or stumble upon an obscure clue, 
piece of knowledge or equipment that will help progress 
the plot.
If Hero Points are used (and look at any Judge Dredd 
story and you will see examples of how, if the story was a 
roleplaying game, Hero Points would have been used), then 
the Referee is always, always the arbiter on when, where 
and how. Players should not use Hero Points as ‘Get Out of 
Jail Free’ cards, nor insist on using Hero Points to mitigate 
bad or poor decisions. There is also no right to Hero Points: 
they are assigned at the Referee’s discretion and may last 
for one scenario or game session only.
When Hero Points are used, it is recommended that 
characters receive no more than 3 Hero Points for that epic 
storyline. When it is over, Hero Points, if any remain, ﬁ zzle 
into thin air and do not carry over into the more mundane 
stories of the campaign (unless the Referee decides 
otherwise and wants to use Hero Points on an extended 
basis). Judge Dredd is heroic but it is also gritty; use of Hero 
Points should be measured and used to drive particular story 
needs – not as a magical solution for all conditions.
Fantastic Peril
Many of the best epic stories in Judge Dredd have, as 
their basis parallel dimensions, time-travel and apocalyptic 
science that, if subjected to the microscope of reason and 
hard science, would fall apart instantly. Not so in epic stories: 
this is the place for the outlandish and the inexplicable. Plot 
devices do not need or require rational explanations or real-
life underpinnings. Top Secret Justice Department mega-
weapons are unveiled as plot devices and do not need to 
have been hinted at or explicitly stated in earlier stories 
or, indeed, in this rulebook. Time and dimensional travel 
works – but you do not need to explain how. Even fantasy 
concepts such as magic and sorcery can be fairly used in 
epic stories and do not need to be given a rational foothold 
villain front and centre from the word go. More important is 
their inﬂ uence, exerted through agents and proxies, which 
gradually focuses on the main villain himself and leads 
the characters into one or more confrontations with him at 
suitably dramatic points.
Action Focus
The epic Dredd stories focus less on investigation and more 
on action and adversity. This is a time for Judges to be 
physically tested and challenged. Combats are frequent and 
brutal; foes are tough and injuries common. Body counts are 
high, escalating as the Judges ﬁ ght their way to the source 
of the evil and attempt to save their city. Investigation and 
use of the little grey cells should not be ignored though – 
especially in the early stages of the story. But once the main 
plot is revealed and the Judges sure of the nature and extent 
of the threat, guns should blaze and buildings should fall.
The Judges Will Fail
Classic storytelling techniques always have the protagonist 
suffer a major defeat or drawback, only to pick himself up 
and overcome the odds to reach a satisfying climax. Judge 
Dredd epics are no exception. Dredd himself has been 
mortally wounded, blinded, irradiated, turned into a werewolf, 
zombieﬁ ed, Block Mania infected and even driven to the 
brink of despair. Despite his superhuman capabilities, the 
ﬂ awed human beneath; vulnerable and sometimes lacking 
in conﬁ dence, is exposed. Dredd has to ﬁ ght back and 
overcome these adversities to drive the epic to its conclusion. 
The Judges in your epic stories should be placed in similar 
positions. What this means is that, as a Referee, you should 
not ﬂ inch from allowing harsh decisions to place the Judges 
in serious peril – and even close to death – but they should 
also be given the opportunity to recover and ﬁ ght back, albeit 
encumbered by their hardships.
Note that Judges should not be immune to death. Poor 
decisions should be rewarded with harsh consequences; 
stupid actions with a common-sense return. Similarly, self-
sacriﬁ ce to advance the plot or increase the party’s chances 
of success should be nobly acknowledged and the Player 
immediately brought-back into play with a supporting 
character. But Judges are notoriously unsentimental and 
their training and dedication means that self-sacriﬁ ce, where 
no other option exists, is expected and taken.
Hero Points
Referees may wish to consider giving Player Character 
Judges a pool of Hero Points for use in epic stories or at 
the most difﬁ cult junctures. Hero Points work in the following 
ways:

===PAGE 256===
 Macs Database
255
for their use. Dredd frequently encounters the supernatural 
or irrational science in the course of his epic adventures; so 
should your Judges. Dredd rarely analyses them or seeks an 
explanation: he dismisses them as weird and gets on with 
it. So should your Judges. The intent of an epic story is fun, 
peril and adventure in a heroic comic-book mould. Strange, 
bizarre and fantastic stuff works and does not feel a need 
to explain how.
Let Mega-City One Have It Big 
Time
By rights, Mega-City One should be uninhabitable, given the 
nuclear, biological and supernatural attacks it has faced over 
its history. Entire regions have been blasted into oblivion 
only to be speedily rebuilt afterwards. Whole city blocks 
explode and millions die; but the city carries on. The reason 
is that Mega-City One is a character every bit as important 
as Judge Dredd – and your Judges. So, in an epic story-
arc, whole sectors can, with license, be nuked, submerged, 
transported to the domain of the Dark Judges, immolated, 
infested, contaminated and smashed to smithereens by alien 
weapons or giant, sentient robots. It is almost true to say that 
an epic storyline is not complete without such wide-screen, 
hi-deﬁ nition destruction – and so it should be in your own epic 
Dredd’s Statistics
Although game statistics for some of the famous 
perps have been provided, there are deliberately 
none for Judge Dredd. The reason for this is simple: 
Dredd is bigger than any game system can easily 
encompass. His use in the game should always 
be under the Referee’s control and his statistics, 
relative to the characters’, are immaterial. If Dredd 
enters regular play, he becomes the focal point 
for the game and his game statistics are so far in 
advance of Player Character Judges that it is unfair 
to offer them. Dredd is a symbol of ultimate justice; 
that helmet stays on for a reason.
Given his physical and intellectual prowess, Dredd 
can be assumed to have a minimum level of 6 in all 
skills Judges routinely use in their duties. He is skilled 
in all Street Judge Special Techniques. He can 
be wounded and knocked down but no foe has 
managed to kill him yet. He is human and 
mortal but as a symbol he is indestructible.
stories. At the end, those areas will stay ruined until rebuilt. 
This means Judges may have to relocate or get involved in 
the rebuilding. Inevitably, Mega-City One will prevail and rise 
from the ashes, its spirit and idiosyncrasies intact. Citizens 
will go back to their humdrum lives and Judges will return 
to their patrols to dole-out cube-time to litterers, slowsters, 
taps and Umpty Baggers – just as they did before the epic 
events occured.
Locations & 
Maps
In a city where every city block contains pretty much 
everything a citizen needs throughout his whole life, 
Players need not necessarily have to remember, or even 
be told to begin with, the location of the nearest kneepad 
stall or munce burger joint. You can simply assume they 
readily ﬁ nd a suitable place and will take just a few minutes 
to get there, whether by vehicle or on foot. 
When they start interacting with the citizens of Mega-City 
One and begin forming relationships with serious Non-
Player Characters, you will ﬁ nd they naturally gravitate to 
speciﬁ c locations without too much work on your part. Do 
not try mapping Mega-City One. Do not even try mapping 
a complete sector. People have tried it before and have 
gone insane. The multi-levelled nature of the city blocks 
and spaghetti-like road system is extremely complex 
and would require a high-powered computer to achieve 
success. For the purposes of the game itself, such maps 
are also irrelevant. You can safely assume that any city 
block is connected, at many levels, to its neighbours and to 
every block within several miles by zoom tubes, skedways, 
pedways and hoverports. Any citizen can reach pretty 
much anywhere in the city, given enough time. Judges 
on Lawmasters will be able to travel between blocks 
within just a few minutes. Though hideously complex, the 
transportation systems of Mega-City One work to a large 
extent. 
Instead, maps should only really become necessary when 
you come to focus on the actions of the Players. There is 
no need to map every level of a hundred-storey block but 
a diagram of the level the Players are travelling to, and 
a map of the apartment they are visiting, will likely prove 
beneﬁ cial. You do not need to plot every connecting road 
within three miles as the Players close in on a group of 
wreckers but the underpass in which they have halted 
trafﬁ c may be well served by a complete map showing their 
positions and those of overturned and burnt out vehicles. 
In short, when describing what Mega-City One is actually 

===PAGE 257===
 Macs Database
256
like, simply detail what you need to and leave the rest to 
your imagination and that of your Players. Anything else 
will likely be super ﬂ uous.
The Big Name 
Cast
Every so often it is fun to have the big names from the Dredd 
saga wander into a story; Dredd himself, Anderson, Hershey, 
Giant and so on. Remember that Mega-City One is a huge 
place and so the chances for your Judges to encounter such 
greats (unless based in Sector 44, which is where Dredd and 
company tend to operate) will be rare, but not impossible. 
The Judges may be called by Dredd as back-up or may ﬁ nd 
him backing them up on occasion, or may ﬁ nd the likes 
of Anderson providing detailed psionic support when the 
Judges need someone of a suf ﬁ ciently high level of psionic 
experience and no one else will do.
Referees should bring the named characters into scenarios 
every once in a while. It establishes continuity with the 
wider saga, exposes characters to the legends of Justice 
Department and establishes links that may be useful and 
important for later scenarios – especially epic story arcs. 
However, resist the temptation to have Dredd roar-in on 
his Lawmaster just to save the characters’ backsides; or 
for characters to summon Dredd every other day simply 
because they cannot be bothered solving something for 
themselves. Not only is Dredd busy, he will not be happy and 
will most likely deny frequent (and even infrequent) requests 
from junior Judges. Relying on Dredd too much as a deus-
ex machina will simply earn the Judges Dredd’s scorn and 
result in an of ﬁ cial reprimand and competency assessment 
by their Sector Chief when Dredd makes his feelings felt.
Replaying 
the Classics
Part of the fun of a roleplaying game based on a popular 
body of ﬁ ction is the opportunity to replay and perhaps alter 
the stories from the saga. Judge Dredd is no different and 
your Judges can have the opportunity to ﬁ ght in the Robot 
Wars, defy Cal (or perhaps side with him?), trek across the 
Cursed Earth, hunt for the Judge Child (and perhaps bring 
him back), arrest PJ Maybe and Chopper and face-off against 
Judge Death. The default year for Judge Dredd campaigns 
is 2131 but there is nothing to prevent you from setting your 
campaign at any point in Judge Dredd’s timeline and using 
your Judges as the focus for Dredd’s adventures and cases. 
Indeed, a great deal of fun can be had through changing 
how those adventures progress and letting the characters 
develop the famous storylines from their perspectives – and 
thus altering future events to create a completely unique 
version of Mega-City One. Perhaps Dredd does not exist; 
perhaps he perishes in the Cursed Earth or when shot by 
Judge Slocum during Cal’s reign of terror. The opportunity 
and decision is yours.
If you choose to remain faithful to the canon, then the 
characters could be assisting Dredd or acting in his place, 
but following events largely as they are resolved in the 
stories. The timeline at the start of this chapter places key 
events (although not all of them) in chronological order so 
Referees can see what future events will be inﬂ uenced if the 
campaign replays the classic stories.
Or, perhaps your Mega-City One is a parallel version of that 
found in 2000 AD. There is certainly a precedent for this, as 
Judge Cal’s return proves. In a parallel Mega-City One Dredd 
might be a routine Street Judge, head of SJS, Chief Judge 
or even serving a stretch on Titan for excessive brutality. 
Perhaps Cal or the Mutant or the Sisters of Death rule Mega-
City One. Did the Apocalypse War take place? Did East-Meg 
One win? All these ‘what if’ scenarios can be an excellent 
way of developing a very similar Dredd campaign without 
having to stick rigidly to the canon.
Crossovers 
and Mega 
Teams
Dredd has enjoyed crossover stories with several 2000 AD 
characters in the past and several older 2000 AD stories (Flesh 
and Harlem Heroes, to name two) are precursors to Dredd’s 
saga. Most notably Dredd has teamed-up with Johnny Alpha 
(Strontium Dog) and has encountered various heroes from 
non-Rebellion titles – including a certain caped crusader.
Team-ups and crossovers are quite welcome in Judge Dredd 
campaigns and the Strontium Dog game can be considered 
a sister game to Judge Dredd (and will use similar concepts 
found in these pages). The Referee is encouraged to use 
these crossovers but to do so sparingly to preserve their 
effect.

Location
Sector 13 is in the old state of New Jersey, bordering the 
island of Manhattan and the old conurb of New York. Heavily 
devastated during the Apocalypse War, it has seen some of 
the most vigorous rebuilding projects anywhere on Mega-
City One although there are still many no-go areas that are 
either too dangerous because of the instability of the ruined 
buildings, rad-pockets or because the criminal gangs have 
managed to impose street-rule.
The sector is bifurcated by the north/south axis of the 
Superslab and its eastern border (with Sector 14) is marked 
by the old Delaware River. The river, dreadfully polluted, 
has been sealed over by almost a century of Mega-City 
Building work; however the Apocalypse War uncovered 
some stretches of the dark, festering, Black Atlantic-bound 
waterway and these have yet to be recovered. The Delaware 
is nicknamed the Big Greasy by Sector 13 citizens and is 
known to be infested with rad-rats, mutated ﬁ sh, lizards and 
other unpleasantness.
Sector 13 is also close to the Eustace Fargo Spaceport, 
which makes it one of the ﬁ rst ports of call for returning 
spacers, alien tourists and disembarking passengers – as 
well as a retreat for perps looking to get off-world ASAP.

Glance
